<meta>This page moves from the table of contents to the editor's preface. It establishes the editor's philosophy—favoring spiritual vitality over historical completeness—and provides a chronological overview of Meister Eckhart's life, leading up to his trial for heresy.</meta>

# Preface.

---

With the freedom that love and reverence provide, I have omitted everything from this edition of Meister Eckhart’s *Mystical Writings* that says nothing to us. Meister Eckhart is too good for mere historical appreciation; he must rise again as a living voice.

Johann Eckhart or Eckehart was born between 1250 and 1270, probably in Hochheim near Gotha. He was a prior of the Dominican Order in Erfurt and a vicar in Thuringia; in 1300, he was at the University of Paris; in 1304, he was provincial prior of Saxony; in 1306, vicar general of Bohemia; in 1314, he became a <term>Master</term> <note>original: "Magister"; a high-level academic title in the medieval university system, authorizing one to teach theology.</note> and professor of theology in Strasbourg, and later in Cologne; in 1317, he was transferred to Frankfurt. In 1326, the Bishop of Cologne, von Ochsenstein, initiated an <term>Inquisition trial</term> <note>A formal legal inquiry by the Church intended to identify and suppress heretical beliefs.</note> against him; he appealed to the Pope and hurried to die in 1327. In 1329, a papal bull <note>An official decree issued by the Pope, named for the lead seal, or "bulla," attached to it.</note> appeared in which 26 of Eckhart’s propositions were condemned as heretical.

The teachers upon whom he mainly

<summary>This preface introduces the life of Meister Eckhart and the editor's selective approach to his writings, focusing on their spiritual relevance. It chronicles Eckhart's rise through the Dominican Order and his eventual condemnation by the Church.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, biography, Dominican Order, Inquisition, papal bull, heresy, medieval theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Meister Eckhart’s thought, identifying his intellectual influences—from Church Fathers to Scholastics—and positioning his mystical philosophy against both medieval realism and modern pantheism.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

relies are: <term>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite</term> <note>A 5th-century Christian philosopher whose works blended Christian theology with Neoplatonist ideas.</note>, Augustine, and Thomas Aquinas; it is also likely that he was familiar with the forbidden writings of <term>John Scotus Eriugena</term> <note>A 9th-century theologian whose works were condemned by the Church because they blurred the distinction between God and the world.</note>. Through Dionysius, he comes into contact with the teachings of the Neoplatonists. Thomas and the entire spirit of his age connect him with the <term>Realists</term> <note>In the medieval "problem of universals," Realists believed that general categories (like "Humanity") were more real than individual examples (like a specific person).</note>, so that, like them, he considers the final and most empty abstractions to be concrete things. On the other hand, this also leads him to view the genus and species as a higher reality than individuals; thus, despite a quite primitive knowledge of nature, he has strong premonitions of the theories currently emerging among us—partly as a result of, and partly in opposition to, the Lamarckian-Darwinian propositions.

He is as much an <term>epistemologist</term> <note>A philosopher who studies the nature and limits of human knowledge.</note> and a critic as he is a mystic. He is a <term>pantheist</term> <note>Someone who believes that the universe and God are identical, or that God is present in all things.</note>, but in almost the opposite sense of what has been understood by that term since the revival of Spinoza. This latter form of pantheism dissolves—though not in Spinoza’s own sense, to be sure—the concept of God into the material world; Eckhart, by contrast, dissolves the world *a n d* God into that which he sometimes calls the <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "Gottheit"; for Eckhart, this refers to the absolute, undifferentiated essence of the Divine that exists behind the personified "God."</note>, which is inexpressible and unimaginable, but which is in any case something beyond time, space, and individualization, and is something of the nature of the soul. In place of the "thing," he sets a psychic force; in place of cause

<summary>This page analyzes Eckhart's philosophical foundations, linking him to Neoplatonism and Scholastic Realism while distinguishing his "Godhead"-centered pantheism from modern versions. It highlights his surprising anticipation of evolutionary concepts and his focus on the soul over material things.</summary>
<keywords>Pseudo-Dionysius, Neoplatonism, Scholastic Realism, Godhead, Pantheism, Spinoza, epistemology, Lamarck, Darwin</keywords>

# Preface.

and effect into a single flowing. His <term>pantheism</term> <note>The belief that God and the universe are identical.</note> is <term>panpsychism</term> <note>The view that all parts of the material world possess a soul or mind.</note>; yet at the same time, he declares that he does not know what the soul actually is. His mysticism is skepticism; though, naturally, the reverse is also true.

The Christian dogmas and traditions hold almost exclusively symbolic significance for him; it is only the <term>Zeitgeist</term> <note>The "spirit of the times" or the prevailing cultural climate.</note> of his era that prevents him from asking how these symbols truly relate to reality. He regards the conventional ideas held by the Church as belonging to a lower level of understanding; however, he does not manage to recognize that these ideas possess no reality at all. Perhaps one day the future will say the same of our own relationship to certain scientific concepts. — Occasionally, moreover—as was likely the case later with <term>Spinoza</term> <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a philosopher whose radical views often required him to use "cautious" or coded language to avoid persecution.</note>—caution helped him choose how to clothe his thoughts.

He is the creator of German <term>scholarly prose</term> <note>original: "wissenschaftliche Prosa." Before Eckhart, philosophy and theology were almost exclusively written in Latin; he was a pioneer in using the German vernacular for complex intellectual thought.</note> and one of its greatest masters. He always writes as a speaker, always personally; his conceptual explanations never lack an emotional resonance, and similarly, his emotional exuberance and his immersion into the "unfathomable darkness" never lack the restraint of <term>sobriety</term> <note>original: "Nüchternheit," implying a clear-headed, rational, and disciplined approach to thought.</note>. He has brought the most distant things close to us; the nearest and most ordinary-seeming things he has made strange, questionable, and profound. He was a

<summary>This page describes Eckhart’s philosophical bridge between mysticism and skepticism, his symbolic view of Church dogma, and his foundational role in developing German as a language for scholarly and philosophical writing.</summary>
<keywords>Panpsychism, skepticism, symbolism, Zeitgeist, Baruch Spinoza, scholarly prose, German language, sobriety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's analysis of Eckhart's literary style, emphasizing that his language is not just a vehicle for thought but a vital, living creation. It transitions from his role as the creator of German scholarly prose to a defense of his specific vocabulary and his rejection of sentimental mysticism.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

A poet who aimed for the greatest things and was equal to the greatest tasks. One finds in his work sentences that are among the most captivating to be found in any language.

I have sought to preserve his syntax as much as possible, which he likely created for himself in close connection to spoken language; likewise, it would be a mistake to replace his technical <term>expressions</term> <note>original: "Ausdrücke"</note>—which he modeled after the patterns of Latin <term>scholasticism</term> <note>A medieval school of philosophy and theology that sought to reconcile Christian faith with Greek logic and reason.</note>—with the bloodless scientific jargon familiar to us today; with him, everything has color, temperament, and originality; his <term>expressions</term> have not yet been stripped of their metaphorical nature, nor have they been drained dry; they only create their meaning during the act of speaking. Frequently, however, it was necessary to translate the <term>terms</term> <note>original: "Termini"</note> that he had translated from the language of science into the vernacular back again, so that what I consider the decisive point of this edition becomes strikingly clear: that Meister Eckhart, for all his genius, was never a mystifying or moralizing petty pietist <note>The author uses a dismissive term for those who practice a superficial or overly sentimental form of religious devotion.</note>, that he never surrendered to a sugary <term>love of God</term> <note>original: "Gottesminne," a Middle High German term specifically used to describe the intense, mystical romance between the soul and the Divine.</note>, and that he never indulged in perverse <term>asceticism</term> <note>The practice of extreme self-discipline and the denial of physical needs, often through fasting or pain, to achieve spiritual goals.</note>: but rather that he was a bold shaker of both brains and hearts, one who wrestled for <term>world-knowledge</term> <note>original: "Welterkenntnis," referring to a deep, philosophical understanding of the nature of reality.</note> and who, life-

# Preface

...joyful and primal, who as a knower crossed the boundaries of language to sink, strong and intimate, into the unspeakable world beyond his <term>self-consciousness</term> <note>original: "Ichbewusstsein," referring here to the awareness of the ego-self as separate from the Divine.</note> and conceptual thinking.

***

The vast majority of what has been handed down from him has become entirely worthless to us, as it is merely logical <term>hair-splitting</term> <note>original: "Wortgetiftel," a dismissive term for overly complex or pedantic word-play.</note> which had to substitute for <term>natural science</term> in those days because observations and knowledge were lacking. If one considers how many supposedly philosophical, scientific, and medical books from the first half of the 19th century have become completely unreadable and "theological" to us, one will understand this better than if one believes that <term>Scholasticism</term> <note>A medieval school of philosophy and theology that sought to reconcile Christian faith with Greek logic and reason, often through rigorous categorization.</note> was a specialty of the <term>Middle Ages</term>. The following selection thus offers about a fifth or sixth of what has come down to us. Nothing of significance has been omitted.

The "Fragments" section was created by me; these are excerpts from sermons which otherwise did not warrant a full translation. — The titles of the individual pieces mostly originate from me; those handed down in <term>Middle High German</term> <note>The form of the German language spoken from roughly 1050 to 1350; while Eckhart wrote his scholarly works in Latin, his sermons were delivered and recorded in this vernacular.</note> were likely also not chosen by Eckhart himself.

<summary>The editor explains that the current edition filters out the pedantic scholastic word-play of the original texts to focus on Eckhart's enduring mystical insights. He also notes that he has curated a "Fragments" section and provided his own titles for the works.</summary>

<keywords>translation rationale, Scholasticism, Middle High German, Fragments, sermon excerpts, editorial selection, self-consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes Gustav Landauer’s preface. Following his explanation of why he omitted certain scholastic portions of the text, he now addresses his decision to skip a technical philological defense and suggests further reading on the historical context of Eckhart’s mysticism.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

It is not my intention to provide a philological accounting of my omissions and textual interpretations here, as it would be of no value to the reader. Anyone wishing to critically examine my translation may obtain information from me directly.

A more detailed introduction would either be merely a skillful grouping of what can already be read in long-existing works, or it would grow into a history of the <term>critique of knowledge</term> <note>original: "Erkenntniskritik," the branch of philosophy (epistemology) that investigates the origin, nature, methods, and limits of human knowledge.</note> in the <term>Middle Ages</term>. That could become a wonderful book, which is very much needed; but for now, I can only wish that I might write it one day. In the meantime, I recommend the reader look into Jundt's *History of Popular Pantheism in the Middle Ages* <note>original: "Histoire du panthéisme populaire au moyen âge," a 1875 work by Auguste Jundt that explores how mystical and pantheistic ideas spread among ordinary people, not just scholars.</note>; from it, they will learn that our Master did not simply "fall from the sky," but was carried by a powerful current of his time.

Several remarks on specific details can be found at the end of the book.

Hermsdorf (Mark).

Gustav Landauer.

<summary>Gustav Landauer concludes his preface by choosing to omit a technical academic defense of his translation, instead directing readers to historical works on medieval pantheism to understand Eckhart's cultural context.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Landauer, Meister Eckhart, critique of knowledge, epistemology, Auguste Jundt, pantheism, Middle Ages, translation methodology</keywords>

<meta>This is the opening page of a sermon by the 14th-century mystic Meister Eckhart, titled "Vom Schweigen" (On Silence). It establishes the central theme of the "birth of the Word" within the human soul.</meta>

## On Silence.

We celebrate the feast of the eternal birth <term>eternal birth</term> <note>In Eckhart's theology, this is the "Gottesgeburt," the idea that God is perpetually born within the soul of the believer.</note>, which God the Father has brought forth and brings forth without ceasing in eternity, while this same birth now occurs in time and in human nature. Saint Augustine <note>A 4th-century theologian whose writings deeply influenced Western Christianity.</note> says that this birth happens always. But if it does not happen within me, what does it profit me? For everything depends on it happening within me.

We have a word from the Wise Man: <note>Referring to the biblical Book of Wisdom 18:14–15.</note> “While all things were in the midst of silence, a hidden word came down to me from above, from the royal throne.” This sermon shall concern this word.

“In the midst of silence, a hidden word was spoken to me.” Ah, Lord, where is this silence, and where is the place where this word is spoken?

It is in the purest part of what the soul can offer, in the noblest part, in the ground <term>ground</term> <note>German: *Grunde*. This is Eckhart’s most famous concept, referring to the innermost essence of the soul where the distinction between the human and the divine disappears.</note>,

<vocab>eternal birth, Saint Augustine, silence, hidden word, sermon, soul, ground</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Meister Eckhart’s sermon on the necessity of inner silence for the "eternal birth" of God to occur within the human soul. He argues that divine revelation is not just a historical event but a personal, internal reality found in the soul's "ground."</summary>
<keywords>eternal birth, inner silence, ground of the soul, Meister Eckhart, Saint Augustine, mysticism, Book of Wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "ground of the soul" from the previous page, further defining it as the silent essence where God performs His work, distinct from the active faculties of the mind.</meta>

->### Meister Eckhart.<-

...yes, in the essence of the soul! This is the means: silence; for into this place no creature or image ever entered, and there the soul has neither activity nor understanding, and it knows no image of this place, neither of itself nor of any other creature.

All the works that the soul performs, it performs through its faculties <term>faculties</term> <note>German: *Kräften*. In medieval philosophy, these were the "tools" of the soul, like sight, hearing, reason, and will.</note>. All that it understands, it understands through reason. When it thinks, it does so through memory. When it desires, it does so through the will; and in this manner, it acts through its faculties and not through its essence. All its outward activity is always attached to a medium <note>German: *Mittel*. Eckhart is arguing that while we need tools like eyes to see or reason to think, we need *nothing* (silence) to experience God in the soul's core.</note>. It achieves the power of sight only through the eyes; otherwise, it can bring about or accomplish no seeing. And it is the same with all the other senses. All its outward activity is achieved through a medium. 

But in the essence <term>essence</term> <note>German: *Wesen*. This refers to the soul’s fundamental being, which Eckhart distinguishes from its active "doing."</note> there is no work. Therefore, the soul has no work in its essence, though the faculties with which it works flow out from the ground of its essence—or rather, in this ground, the "medium" is silence. Here alone is rest and a dwelling for this birth and for this work: that God the Father may speak His Word there; for by nature, this place is accessible only to the divine essence, without any medium. God enters here into the soul with His whole being, not

<summary>Eckhart explains that while the soul uses "faculties" (like reason and will) for outward activities, its inner essence must remain silent and void of all images to receive the "Word of God." He describes the soul's ground as a place of absolute rest where God enters directly without any intermediaries.</summary>
<keywords>essence of the soul, faculties, silence, ground of the soul, divine birth, medium, reason, memory, will</keywords>

any creature only by first

this present image, it approaches the crea-

creates with its faculties. Whether it be a stone,
a horse, a person, or whatever else

image that it has drawn from them,

Of no thing does it know so little as of itself, because of the means <term>means</term> <note>The "means" or "mediation" (Mittel) refers to the images or sensory data the soul uses to understand the world. Because the soul has no physical form to provide a sensory image, it lacks the "means" to see itself.</note>. And you must also know this: that the soul is free within, and can exist without any means and images, and this is also the reason why God can unite Himself freely with it without images or parables <term>parables</term> <note>In this context, "parables" (Gleichnisse) refers to likenesses, symbols, or any created thing used to represent something else.</note>. You must not hold back; you must grant to God, without any limits, the same possibility that you would grant to a master <term>master</term> <note>Eckhart often uses the "master" (Meister) as an analogy for a skilled artist or a philosopher like Aristotle to explain how skill leads to effortless action.</note>. But the wiser and more powerful a master is, the more directly his work occurs, and the simpler it is.

Man uses many means in his outward works; a great deal of preparation is required before he brings forth these works as he has formed them within himself. The mastery and the work of the moon and the sun are illumination; they do this very quickly. As soon as they pour out their radiance, in that very moment, the world is full of light at every end. But above them is the angel, who needs even fewer means for his works and also has fewer images. The highest of all, the Seraphim <term>Seraphim</term> <note>In the medieval celestial hierarchy, the Seraphim are the highest rank of angels, positioned closest to God and defined by their "burning" love and simplicity.</note>, has only one single image. # Master Eckhart.

...should keep oneself in a silence or in a stillness and in a peace, so that God may speak and work within them, if they thus wait solely for God’s work within?

I point out that my speeches and works are dedicated solely to good and perfect people, in whom above all the worthy life and the noble teaching of our Lord Jesus Christ is alive. These people shall now learn that the very best and most noble thing that one can attain in this life is for you to be silent and let God work and speak therein. Where all **powers** <term>powers</term> <note>Original: *Kräfte*. In medieval psychology, these are the faculties of the soul, such as memory, reason, and will. Eckhart teaches they must be stilled to hear God.</note> are withdrawn from all their works and **images** <term>images</term> <note>Original: *Bildern*. These are the mental forms or "species" we use to understand the world. To Eckhart, these images clutter the soul and must be cleared to make room for God.</note>, there this Word is spoken. Therefore it was said: "In the midst of silence, the secret word was spoken to me." <note>A reference to Job 4:12 or Wisdom 18:14-15, which Eckhart frequently cites to describe the "Eternal Birth" of the Word in the soul's silence.</note>

This refers to any created thing or being, as opposed to the uncreated God. To "forget the creature" is to let go of our attachment to the physical world.</note>, the closer you are to this and the more receptive you become. Often called "unknowing," this is a state where the mind moves beyond intellectual concepts to experience a reality that transcends human thought.</note> of your own life, as happened to Saint Paul when he said: "Whether I was in the body or out of the body, I do not know; God knows" <note>original: "Ob ich in dem Leib war oder nicht, das weiss ich nicht, Gott aber weiss es wohl"—referencing 2 Corinthians 12:2.</note>—there the spirit had so completely withdrawn all powers into itself that it had forgotten the body...

### On Silence.

...had, then there worked neither memory nor understanding, nor the senses, nor the **powers** <term>powers</term> <note>Original: *Kräfte*. In medieval psychology, these are the faculties of the soul, such as memory, reason, and will. Eckhart teaches they must be stilled to hear God.</note>; it happened the same way to **Moses** <note>Refers to the biblical account in Exodus of Moses on Mount Sinai; Eckhart uses this to illustrate how a soul in union with God is sustained beyond natural physical needs.</note>, when he fasted for forty days on the mountain and yet did not grow any weaker — just so should a person escape all senses and turn all their powers inward and reach a forgetting of all things and of themselves. In this sense, a Master spoke to the soul: withdraw from the unrest of outward works; flee, therefore, and hide yourself from the storm of outward works and inward thoughts, for they only create discord. But if God is to speak His Word in the soul, it must be in peace and stillness, and then He speaks His Word and Himself into the soul—not an image, but Himself. **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian. Eckhart often cites him to support the idea that God is beyond all human concepts and names.</note> says: God has no image or likeness of Himself, for "good" or "true" belongs to His very being. God performs all His works within Himself and out of Himself in a single moment. You must not believe that God, when He made heaven and earth and all things, made one thing today and another tomorrow. Although **Moses** <note>Referring here to the author of Genesis and the seven-day creation story.</note> writes it this way, he nonetheless knew much better: he only did so for the sake of the people who could not understand or grasp it any other way. God did nothing more than this one thing: He willed, and they came to be. God works without means and without **images** <term>images</term> <note>Original: *Bildern*. These are the mental forms we use to categorize the world. To Eckhart, these must be cleared because God is "un-imaged" and cannot be captured by human concepts.</note>.

# Meister Eckhart.

The more you are without **images** <term>images</term> <note>Original: *Bild*. In Eckhart’s philosophy, "images" are mental representations, concepts, or sensory memories. To be "without image" is to achieve a state of pure, empty receptivity.</note>, the more you are receptive to His influence; and the more you are turned inward and self-forgetting, the closer you are to this.

To this end, **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential 5th-century mystical theologian. Eckhart draws heavily on his "apophatic" or "negative" theology, which seeks God by stripping away all names and concepts.</note> exhorted his disciple **Timothy** and said: "Dear son Timothy, with unconcerned senses you shall soar above yourself and above all your **powers** <term>powers</term> <note>Original: *Kräfte*. The faculties of the soul, such as reason, memory, and will.</note>, and above modes and above being, into the hidden, silent darkness, so that you may come to a knowledge of the unknown **super-divine God** <note>Original: *übergöttischer Gott*. A translation of the Greek *hypertheos*, suggesting a reality that is "more than God" as humanly conceived.</note>." There must be a looking away from all things. God disdains to work through images.

Now you might ask: what then does God work without images in the **ground** <term>ground</term> <note>Original: *Grund*. The "Ground of the Soul" is the essential, spark-like core of a human being where the soul is most directly united with the Divine.</note> and in the essence? That I cannot know, for the powers can only perceive through images and must perceive and recognize all things in their own image. They cannot recognize a bird in the image of a man; and therefore, since all images enter from the outside, this work is hidden from the soul, and that is most beneficial. For **ignorance** <note>Original: *Unwissen*. Eckhart values this "unknowing" as a higher state than intellectual knowledge because it allows the soul to experience God beyond the limits of logic.</note> brings the soul to wonder, and causes it to pursue this; for it truly finds that "it is," but it simply knows not "how" or "what" it is. But when a person knows the cause of things, immediately they are also the

<summary>Eckhart explains that the soul must transcend all mental images and "powers" to experience the "super-divine God" in the silent darkness of the soul's ground. He argues that ignorance of God's specific nature is beneficial because it drives the soul toward a deeper, wonder-filled pursuit of the Divine.</summary>

# On Silence.

...things <note>This continues the thought from the previous page: because the soul's natural powers are limited to "images" or concepts of physical objects, it becomes weary of them.</note>, and it seeks to experience something else, yet it always finds a misery in knowing these things and has no staying power there; therefore, this **unknown knowledge** <term>unknown knowledge</term> <note>Original: *unerkannte Erkenntnis*. Yet it seems to me that if I could grasp it, I would know all truth." Then the other master said: "Go on, follow after it! For if you could grasp it, you would have all good things together and would have eternal life."

In this same sense, **Saint Augustine** <note>A 4th-century North African bishop and theologian whose writings on the interior life of the soul provided the foundation for much of Western mysticism.</note> also spoke: "I become aware of something within me that plays and hovers before my soul; if that were completed and established in me, that would have to be eternal life." It hides itself and yet it makes itself known; but it comes in a stealthy way, as if it wanted to take and steal all things from the soul. But by showing and revealing itself a little, it intended to entice the soul and draw the soul after it, to rob and deprive the soul of its very self. Regarding this, the **Prophet** <note>Likely a reference to the Psalms or the book of Ezekiel, reflecting the idea of the human spirit being replaced by the Divine Spirit.</note> said: "Lord, take from them their spirit, and give them instead..."

## On Unknowing.

"Where is he who is born King of the Jews?" <note>Matthew 2:2. Eckhart frequently uses this question from the Magi to discuss the internal discovery of God within the soul.</note> — Listen now to how this birth takes place.

Whenever he turns to God, a light gleams and shines within him and lets him know what he should do and leave undone, and provides much good teaching of which he previously knew and understood nothing. ### On Unknowing.

**Enlightenment**? This is so delicate and delightful that everything which is not **God** or divine becomes distasteful to you. **Saint Augustine** <note>original: "Sankt Augustin". Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian for Eckhart’s thought.</note> says: There are many who have sought **light** and **truth**, but always only outside, where it was not. The spiritual ability to distinguish truth from illusion or to judge between different spiritual states.</note> of all truth, let them wait for this **birth** in themselves and in their interior, and take heed of it: thus will all the powers and the **outer man** <term>outer man</term> <note>The physical person and their sensory interaction with the world.</note> be enlightened. For as soon as God has touched the interior with truth, the light casts itself into the powers, and the person then understands more than anyone could teach them. Therefore the Prophet says: “I have known more than all who ever taught me.” <note>Likely referring to Psalm 119:99: "I have more insight than all my teachers, for I meditate on your statutes."</note>

The deepest, most unified point of the soul where it is essentially one with God.</note> and not in the powers, how does it concern the powers? What is their service here, that they should exert themselves and help to celebrate? Why is that necessary, since nothing happens in the powers? That is a well-posed question. But observe the following distinction. Every **creature** performs its work for the sake of a **purpose** <term>purpose</term> <note>Original: *Zweck*. Eckhart is here using the Scholastic principle *Finis est primus in intentione, ultimus in executione* (The end is first in intention, last in execution).</note>. The purpose is always the first in intention and the last in the work. Therefore, God intends with all

## Master Eckhart.

[gives] his works a spiritual purpose, that is: himself, and desires to lead the soul with all its **powers** <term>powers</term> <note>Original: *Kräften*. In medieval psychology, these are the soul's faculties, such as sight, hearing, memory, and will.</note> to their purpose, which is: to **God** himself. This is why God performs all his works; this is why the **Father** begets his **Son** in the soul, so that all the powers of the soul may reach their ultimate purpose. He strives for everything that is within the soul and invites it all to his banquet and to his court.

But now, the soul has divided and scattered its powers outwardly, each to its own task: the power of sight to the eye, the power of hearing to the ear, the power of taste to the tongue; and for this reason, her works are all the less capable of acting inwardly, for any divided power is imperfect. Therefore, if the soul wishes to act powerfully within, she must call all her powers home again and gather them from all scattered things into one inward action.

**Saint Augustine** <note>original: "Sankt Augustin".</note> says: "The soul is more present where it loves than where it gives life to the body." 

A **parable**: There was once a pagan master <note>This likely refers to the legendary death of the Greek mathematician Archimedes, who was famously killed by a Roman soldier while focused on his geometric drawings.</note> who had devoted himself to the **art of calculation** <term>art of calculation</term> <note>Original: *Rechenkunst*. Refers here to mathematics or geometry.</note>; he sat before his measuring rods, counting them and pursuing his science. Then someone came and drew his sword—not knowing that he was the master—and said: "Speak quickly, tell me your name, or I will kill you." The master was so deeply turned inward that he [did not perceive] the enemy...

<summary>Eckhart explains that the soul's sensory powers must be withdrawn from the external world and "called home" to focus on the inner birth of God. He illustrates this intense inward focus using a quote from St. Augustine and the story of a mathematician who was so absorbed in his work that he ignored a death threat.</summary>
<keywords>powers of the soul, inwardness, Saint Augustine, parable of the master, Archimedes, divine birth, focus, spiritual purpose</keywords>

## On Ignorance.

...did not see nor hear, nor could he notice what the enemy wanted. And when the enemy had called out long and loud, and the Master still did not speak, the enemy struck off his head. This was done to acquire a natural art. How much more, then, should we withdraw from all things and gather all our **powers** to behold and recognize the one, boundless, uncreated, eternal **truth**! To this end, gather all your **reason** <term>reason</term> <note>Original: *Vernunft*. In Eckhart's philosophy, this is the highest faculty of the soul, capable of touching the Divine.</note> and all your reflection: turn them toward the depths where this treasure lies hidden. Know that if this is to happen, you must drop all other works and you must come into an **ignorance** <term>ignorance</term> <note>Original: *Unwissen*. Also known as "unknowing." This is not a lack of intelligence, but a deliberate clearing of the mind of worldly concepts to allow for spiritual experience.</note> if you wish to find this.

A question arises again. Would it not be more appropriate for each power to keep to its own work, and for none to hinder the other in their tasks, and for them also not to hinder God in His work? In me, there can be a kind of **creaturely knowledge** <term>creaturely knowledge</term> <note>Original: *kreatürliches Wissen*. Knowledge obtained through the senses or directed toward created things, which can act as a distraction from God.</note> that hinders nothing, just as God knows all things without hindrance, as is the case with the **blessed** <term>the blessed</term> <note>Original: *die Seligen*. Those in heaven who enjoy the direct vision of God.</note>. Now, pay attention to the following distinction. The blessed see an image in God, and in that image they recognize all things; indeed, God Himself sees only within Himself and recognizes all things within Himself. He does not need to [turn] from one thing to another...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence as God's method of knowing is contrasted with human cognition.</note>

# Meister Eckhart.

...turn, as we must. If it were so arranged in this life that we had a mirror always before us, in which we saw and recognized all things in a single image in an instant, then our **acting** and **knowing** would be no hindrance to us. But since we must turn from one thing to another, we cannot occupy ourselves with one without hindering the other. 

For the **soul** <term>soul</term> <note>Original: *Seele*. In Eckhart’s thought, the soul is the seat of both worldly perception and divine union.</note> is so entirely bound to its **powers** <term>powers</term> <note>Original: *Kräfte*. These are the faculties of the soul—such as the senses, memory, will, and intellect—which "flow" out into the world.</note> that it flows with them wherever they go. In all the works they perform, the soul must be present with its **attention** <term>attention</term> <note>Original: *Aufmerksamkeit*. Eckhart teaches that the soul's energy is finite; if it is focused on the outside world, it cannot be focused on God.</note>; otherwise, they would achieve nothing at all with their activity. 

If the soul flows with its attention toward external works, it must necessarily be that much weaker in its inner work. For for this **birth** <term>birth</term> <note>Original: *Geburt*. The "Eternal Birth" is the central theme of Eckhart’s sermons, describing how God is continually born within the "ground" of the human soul.</note>, God desires and must have a **detached** <term>detached</term> <note>Original: *ledige*. Meaning unencumbered, empty, or free from worldly attachments.</note>, unburdened, and free soul, in which nothing may be except Him alone, and which waits for nothing and no one but Him alone. 

This is what Christ meant when he said: “Whoever loves anything other than me, and is fond of father and mother and these other things, is not worthy of me. I have not come to the earth to bring peace, but a sword, so that I may cut away all things, and [sever] the brother, the child, the mother, the friend...” <note>A reference to Matthew 10:34-37. Eckhart interprets this "sword" not as a weapon of war, but as a spiritual tool for "detachment" from earthly relationships and ego.</note>

<summary>Eckhart explains that because human attention is finite, we must withdraw from external activities and family ties to create a "detached" space for God to be born within the soul. He interprets Christ's "sword" as the spiritual necessity of cutting away worldly distractions.</summary>

<header>On Unknowing.</header>

...separate from you, those who are truly your enemies." For what is dear to you is truly your enemy. If your eye wants to see all things, and your ear to hear all things, and your heart to remember all things, then truly your **soul** <term>soul</term> <note>Original: *Seele*. Eckhart views the soul as a finite vessel; if filled with worldly sights and sounds, it has no room for the divine.</note> must be scattered by all these things.

Therefore, a **Master** <note>Original: *Meister*. Likely referring to an authority such as Augustine or Dionysius the Areopagite, whose "negative theology" deeply influenced Eckhart.</note> says: If a person wishes to perform an **inward work** <term>inward work</term> <note>Original: *inwendiges Werk*. The mystical practice of turning one's attention away from the physical world toward the spiritual "ground" of the soul.</note>, he must pull all his powers into himself, as if into a corner of his soul, and must hide himself from all **images and forms** <note>Original: *Bildern und Formen*. In medieval psychology, these are the mental representations of physical objects that the mind "grasps" to understand the world.</note>, and then he can work. There he must come into a **forgetting** and into an **unknowing** <term>unknowing</term> <note>Original: *Unwissen*. This is not a lack of intelligence, but a deliberate "unlearning" of worldly concepts to experience God, who is beyond human categories.</note>. It must be in a **stillness** <term>stillness</term> and in a **silence** <term>silence</term> where this **Word** <note>Original: *Wort*. Referring to the *Logos* or the presence of God speaking within the soul.</note> is to be heard. One can approach this Word with nothing better than with stillness and with silence: then one can hear it and then one understands it fully in the unknowing. When one knows nothing, then it shows and reveals itself.

Now you might say: "Sir, you place all our salvation in an unknowing. That sounds like a deficiency. God created man that he might know; where there is unknowing, there is negation and emptiness. Man is—this must be true—an animal, an ape, a fool, so long as he persists in unknowing." 

But knowledge should transform itself into a **super-form** <term>super-form</term> <note>Original: *Ueberform*. A state of being or understanding that transcends the limits of human logic and the physical senses.</note>, and this unknowing should not come from a lack of knowledge, but rather: from knowledge...

<header>Meister Eckhart.</header>

...one should come into an **unknowing** <term>unknowing</term> <note>Original: *Unwissen*. In Eckhart’s theology, this is a state where the mind is cleared of all earthly concepts to make room for God.</note>. Then we shall become knowing of the **divine unknowing** <note>Original: *göttliches Unwissen*. A reference to "negative theology," which suggests God is best understood by acknowledging what we cannot know about Him.</note>, and then our unknowing is ennobled and adorned with **supernatural knowledge**. And here, where we conduct ourselves receptively, we are more perfect than when we were active. Therefore a **Master** <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or a similar scholastic authority on the hierarchy of the senses.</note> said that the power of **hearing** stands on a much higher level than the power of **seeing**, for one learns more wisdom through hearing than through seeing, and lives more in wisdom here. It is told of a **pagan master** <note>Eckhart often used anecdotes from "pagan" (Classical Greek or Roman) philosophers to illustrate spiritual truths that are universal to human nature.</note> that as he lay dying, his disciples spoke in his presence of much art and great knowledge. At this, even as he was dying, he raised his head and listened, saying: "Truly, I still wish to learn this art, so that I may apply it in eternity." Hearing brings more in, but seeing points more out. And therefore, in the **eternal life**, we shall be much more blessed in the power of hearing than in the power of seeing. For the work of hearing the **eternal Word** <note>Original: *ewiges Wort*. The Logos or the ongoing presence of God communicating within the soul.</note> is within me, and the work of seeing goes out from me; in hearing I am receptive, and in seeing I am active.

Our **blessedness** <term>blessedness</term> <note>Original: *Seligkeit*. A state of supreme spiritual happiness or union with God.</note>, however, does not lie in our works, but rather in our receiving God. For by as much as God stands higher than...

### Of Unknowing.

...the creature, the work of God stands just as much higher than mine. Indeed, out of boundless love, God has placed our **blessedness** <term>blessedness</term> <note>Original: *Seligkeit*. This refers to the state of ultimate spiritual happiness or union with God.</note> in a state of receiving, in that we receive more than we act, and we take by far more than we give. Every gift prepares the receptivity for a new, and indeed for a larger gift; every divine gift expands the capacity and the desire for a greater reception. And therefore some **Masters** <note>Likely referring to scholastic theologians like Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great, whom Eckhart often synthesized.</note> say that in this, the soul is equal to God. For as boundless as God is in giving, so boundless is the soul in perceiving or receiving. And just as God is almighty in his working, so the soul is an **abyss of receiving** <note>Original: *Abgrund des Nehmens*. The "Abgrund" or "Abyss" is a central concept in Eckhart’s mysticism, describing the bottomless depth of the soul that matches the bottomless depth of God.</note>, and therefore she is **transformed** <term>transformed</term> <note>Original: *überformt*, literally "over-formed." This suggests the soul is reshaped into the likeness of God through this divine encounter.</note> with God and in God. God shall work and the soul shall receive; He shall recognize and love Himself within her; she shall recognize with His knowledge and shall love with His love. Therefore, she is much more blessed by what is His than by what is her own, and her blessedness rests more in His working than in hers.

His disciples asked **Saint Dionysius** <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century Christian philosopher whose "Mystical Theology" deeply influenced Eckhart’s ideas on unknowing.</note> why they were all overtaken in perfection by **Timothy**? <note>Dionysius’s student, to whom the treatise *On Divine Names* was addressed.</note> Then Dionysius spoke: "Timothy is a **God-receiving** man." For Eckhart, spiritual perfection is found in the "unknowing" state described on the previous page, where the soul is emptied of its own ego to be filled by God.</note>, and your **not-doing** <term>not-doing</term> <note>Original: *Nichttun*. ## On the Darkness. <note>Original: *Von der Dunkelheit*. In mystical theology, "darkness" does not imply evil, but rather the "divine dark"—a state where the human mind can no longer perceive God through logic or senses, requiring a "blind" leap of faith into the unknown.</note>

We read in the Gospel that when our Lord was twelve years old, he went with Mary and Joseph to Jerusalem to the Temple, and as they departed from there, Jesus remained in the Temple without them knowing it. And when they arrived home and missed him, they sought him among their acquaintances and strangers, and among their relatives and in the crowd, and found him nowhere. They had lost him in the crowd and therefore had to go back to where they had come from; and when they returned to the beginning, into the Temple, there they found him.

This is the truth; if you wish to find this **noble birth** <term>noble birth</term> <note>Original: *edle Geburt*. This is Eckhart's central concept of the "Birth of the Word in the Soul," where God is continuously born within the person who has made space for Him.</note>, then you must leave behind the entire "crowd" and must return to the beginning and into the **primordial ground** <term>primordial ground</term> <note>Original: *Urgrund*. A landmark term in German mysticism referring to the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God and the deepest, uncreated part of the human soul.</note> from which you came forth. All the powers of the soul and their works are merely "the crowd"; memory, intellect, and will all multiply themselves <note>Eckhart views the "crowd" as a metaphor for the distractions of the world and the various functions of our own minds, which pull us away from the singular, simple presence of God.</note>, and therefore you must leave them all...

leave behind: sensuality, mental images <note>Original: *Vorstellungen*. In Eckhart’s mystical philosophy, these are the "pictures" or concepts the mind creates. Therefore, we have a question regarding this: whether a person can find this birth in certain things that are indeed divine, but brought in from the outside through the senses—such as certain conceptions of God, for example, that God is good, wise, merciful, or something similar, which reason can derive and which is also divine: whether one can find this birth in all of this? In truth, no! Although all of that is good and divine, it has nonetheless all been brought in from the outside through the senses. Everything must well up from within, from God, if this birth is to shine in as its own and in purity; and all your work must "lie down" <note>Meaning your own ego-driven activities must cease.</note>, and all your powers must serve His powers and not your own.

...you must be brought to that point. And this cannot be changed. Just as matter does not rest until it is filled with all forms <note>Eckhart uses an idea from Aristotelian philosophy: that "prime matter" has a natural, restless hunger to be defined and shaped by a "form."</note>, so too reason never rests until it is filled with everything that is possible within it.

A pagan master <note>Original: *ein heidnischer Meister*. But certainly! Human consciousness overtakes even them in its course. If it remained effective in its own power and kept itself uncompromised and undivided by lower and coarse things, it would fly higher than the highest heaven and never cease; it would arrive at the Very Highest and there be fed and guided by the very best Good, which is God.

For Eckhart, the "wilderness" or "desert" is a metaphor for the soul when it is stripped of all created images and distractions, making it a place where God can speak.</note> within you and an ignorance of all things, the closer you come to Him. Medieval authors often cited scripture from memory or used common liturgical attributions of the time.</note>:

> “I will lead my beloved into the wilderness and speak to her in her heart.”

### On Darkness.

...spoken only in eternity, where man is a wilderness <note>Original: *Wüste*. For Eckhart, this is a positive state of spiritual emptiness where the soul is free from worldly distractions.</note> and is alienated from himself and from all multiplicity <term>multiplicity</term> <note>Original: *Mannigfaltigkeit*. In mystical theology, this refers to the many distracting things of the material world, which stand in contrast to the "Oneness" of God.</note>. The Prophet longed for this wilderness and this estrangement when he said:

> "Oh, who will give me wings like those of a dove, so that I might fly to where I find rest?" <note>A reference to Psalm 55:6.</note>

Where does one find rest and repose? Truly, it is there where one is stripped, cleared, and alienated from all created things. In this sense, David says:

> "I would rather choose to be rejected and despised in the house of my God, than to have great honor and wealth in the tabernacles of sinners." <note>A reference to Psalm 84:10.</note>

Now you might say: "Truly, sir, must it always and necessarily be so, that one is alienated and emptied of all things, both outwardly and inwardly—even of one's own [mental] powers and their works? Must all of that be cast down? That is a difficult state to be in, if God leaves a person so without a dwelling-place, and if God stretches a person's abandonment so far that He is not present within him—neither shining, nor speaking, nor acting—as you teach and suggest here. If a person stands thus in pure nothingness, is it not better that he do something to drive away this darkness and alienation? For example, that he should pray or read or hear a sermon, or perform other deeds which are, after all, virtues..."

<summary>Eckhart describes the soul's need to become a "wilderness" free from worldly distractions to find rest in God, then addresses a hypothetical objection about whether such a state of "nothingness" is too difficult for a person to endure.</summary>
<keywords>wilderness, multiplicity, alienation, spiritual rest, David, darkness, nothingness, virtues, Meister Eckhart</keywords>

# Meister Eckhart.

...with which one should help oneself?" — No, you should know this in truth: to remain completely and profoundly still, and entirely and utterly empty, is your absolute best. Take note of this. In Eckhart's thought, the soul's "readiness" or "receptivity" is itself a gift from God, not an independent human achievement.</note> unless God is there first. A common mystical term for the infusion of grace or the Divine nature into the human soul.</note> were divided between you and Him—which is indeed possible—you must know that God *must* act and pour Himself in as soon as He finds you ready. 

You must not imagine that God is like a carpenter, who works or does not work as he pleases; it is in a carpenter's will to do or to leave off as he has the desire. It is not so with God: rather, when God finds you ready, He *must* act and pour Himself into you, just as when the air is clear and pure, the sun must pour itself out and cannot restrain itself from doing so. Truly, it would be a very great defect in God if He did not perform great works in you and pour great goodness into you as soon as He finds you free and stripped <note>Original: *entblösst*. This implies being "naked" or divested of all worldly attachments and self-will.</note> [of all things].

> “I stand at the door and knock and wait; whoever lets me in, with him I will dine.” <note>original: "Ich stehe vor der Tür und klopfe und warte, wer mich einlässt, mit dem will ich schmausen." A paraphrase of Revelation 3:20.</note>
You do not need to seek Him here or there: He is no further away than the door of the heart. There He stands and longs and waits to find someone ready to open to Him and let Him in. You do not need to call to Him from afar: the waiting for you to open is harder for Him than it is for you. He needs you a thousand times more than you need Him: the opening and the entering take only a moment.

As it pleases Him, He reveals Himself, and He can hide Himself whenever He wills. You must know this: God cannot leave anything empty or hollow; that anything, even the smallest thing, should be empty or hollow

### Meister Eckhart.

# On Darkness. 41

...all its relatives and its whole lineage: there it is completely at home. In order to come to the aid of the spirit in this foreign land and to weaken the flesh somewhat in this struggle—so that the body does not overcome the spirit—one applies the bridle of **penitential exercises** <term>penitential exercises</term> <note>Original: *Bussübungen*. These were physical disciplines like fasting or labor intended to subdue bodily desires.</note> and oppresses it so that the spirit can defend itself. Since one does this to the body so that it may be a prisoner, then—if you wish to capture and burden it a thousand times better—put upon it the bridle of **love**. With love, you overcome it most quickly, and with love, you burden it most heavily.

And for this reason, God pursues us with nothing so much as with love. For it is exactly the same with love as it is with a fisherman’s hook. The fisherman cannot catch the fish if it does not take the hook. When it snaps at the hook, then the fisherman is sure of it: whichever way the fish then turns, here or there, the fisherman has it quite securely.

So I also speak of love: whoever is captured by it has the strongest of all bonds and yet a **sweet burden** <note>This is a reference to Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>. Whoever has taken this sweet burden upon themselves achieves more and goes further with it than with all the **penance** <term>penance</term> <note>Original: *Busse*.</note> and **severity** <note>Original: *Strenge*. Refers to rigid ascetic discipline.</note> that humans could ever practice. He can also gently and patiently [bear] all...

## Of Constant Joy.

The soul has something within itself, a **little spark of reason** <term>spark of reason</term> <note>Original: *Fünklein der Vernünftigkeit*. This is Meister Eckhart’s famous concept of the "soul-spark" (*scintilla animae*), an eternal, indestructible part of the human spirit that remains in direct contact with God.</note>, which never goes out; and into this little spark, one places the image of the soul as if into the highest part of consciousness. There is also a **knowing** <term>knowing</term> <note>Original: *Erkennen*. Eckhart distinguishes between the "knowing" of the senses and intellect, which deals with separate things, and a higher, unified knowing.</note> in our souls that pursues external things—namely, sensory and intellectual knowing—which takes place through images and language; and this hides the spark from us.

How are we **children of God**? <note>Original: *Söhne Gottes* (Sons of God). Eckhart uses this to describe the "birth of the Word" in the soul, where the individual becomes one with the divine nature.</note> It is because we have *one* **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>Original: *Wesen*. This signifies a shared reality or "being" with God, suggesting that at our deepest level, we are not separate from the Divine.</note> with Him. However, what we understand by being children of God is to be understood through both outer understanding and inner understanding. Inner knowing is that which is rationally grounded upon the essence of our soul. Yet it is not the essence of the soul itself; rather, it is rooted within it and is something of the life of the soul. We say that understanding is something living within the soul—that is, a **rational life**,

And if His knowing is thus mine, and if His substance is His knowing and His nature and His essence, then it follows that His essence and His substance and His nature are mine. And if His substance, His essence, and His nature are mine, then I am the **Son of God**. Behold, brothers, what love God has bestowed upon us, that we should be called the Son of God and be His own.

Pay attention to how we become sons of God: it is when we possess the same essence that the Son has. ### Of Constant Joy.

...that we can be placed into the very same **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>Original: *Wesen*. In Eckhart’s thought, this is the fundamental reality shared by God and the soul in its highest state.</note> that he himself is. Therefore, if I reach the point where I transform myself into nothing and transform nothing into myself, and carry out and cast out whatever is in me, I can be transported into the pure essence of the Spirit. There, everything that is a **likeness** <term>likeness</term> <note>Original: *Gleichnis*. For Eckhart, a "likeness" is an image, symbol, or concept that stands *between* the soul and God; to reach God, one must move beyond all "pictures" or comparisons.</note> must be driven out, so that I may be transformed into God and become one with him—one substance, one essence, one nature, and the **Son of God**. 

And when that has happened, then nothing in God remains hidden that does not become manifest and does not become mine. For then I become wise and powerful and entirely like him, and one and the same with him. Then Zion becomes a "seer of truth," a true **Israel**, which means "a man who sees God" <note>Original: *ein sehender Mann: Gott*. This reflects a common medieval etymology of the name Israel: *vir videns Deum* (the man who sees God).</note>; for to him nothing in the **Godhead** <term>Godhead</term> <note>Original: *Gottheit*. This refers to the absolute, undifferentiated divine nature, which Eckhart views as the "ground" deeper than the personal distinctions of Father, Son, and Spirit.</note> is hidden. 

There, man is led into God. But so that nothing remains hidden from me and everything becomes manifest, no likeness and no image may remain within me; for no image can open the Godhead or his essence to us. If any image remained in you, or any likeness, you would never become one with God. Therefore, in order for you to be one with God, nothing must be **formed within** <note>Original: *eingebildet*. Today this word means "imagined," but here it refers to the soul being "shaped" or "in-formed" by internal mental images.</note> or **formed without** <note>Original: *ausgebildet*. Here meaning "shaped" or "modeled" by external concepts or forms.</note>; that is to say, everything hidden within you must be opened and cast out.

# Meister Eckhart.

You should not doubt this: if you are sad for yourself or for your friend, then the child is not born, though it is close to birth. But it is perfectly born when a person feels no suffering in their heart for any thing: then the person possesses the **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>Original: *Wesen*. The fundamental, unchanging reality of a thing.</note> and the nature and the substance and the wisdom and the joy and everything that God has. Then this essence of the Son of God becomes ours and is in us, and we enter into this essence of God.

Eckhart interprets "denying oneself" as the total removal of the ego.</note>. This means: Cast out all heartache, so that in your heart there is nothing but constant joy. Then the child is born. # Of Constant Joy. 47

# Of the City of the Soul.

"Jesus entered into a certain town and a certain woman received him, etc." (Luke 10:38). <note>original: "Intravit Jesus in quoddam castellum et mulier quaedam excepit illum etc."</note> I have just spoken a short word in Latin that stands in the Gospel, which in English means: “Our Lord Jesus Christ went into a little town and was received by a **virgin** <term>virgin</term> <note>Original: *Jungfrau*. In Eckhart’s mystical theology, "virginity" represents a soul that is pure, empty, and detached from all created things.</note> who was a woman.”

Truly, now pay close attention to this word. It must necessarily be that the person by whom Jesus was received was a virgin. “Virgin” describes a person who is **free** <term>free</term> <note>Original: *ledig*. This can also be translated as "empty," "void," or "unencumbered." It refers to a state of absolute detachment where the soul is no longer "filled" with worldly concerns.</note> of all foreign **images** <term>images</term> <note>Original: *Bilder*. Eckhart uses this to mean any mental concepts, sensory perceptions, or attachments that distract the soul from God’s pure essence.</note>, as free as they were when they **did not yet exist** <note>Eckhart frequently refers to the state of the soul as it existed eternally in the mind of God before it was created as a finite being.</note>. See, now one might ask: a person who is born and has progressed to a **rational life** <note>Meaning a life guided by the human intellect and reason (*Vernunft*).</note>—how can they be as free of all images as they were when they did not yet exist? For they clearly know many things, and all of these are "images." How then can they be free?

<vocab>Soul, Virgin, Images, Gospel, Jesus, Luke 10:38, Little Town, Reason</vocab>

<summary>Meister Eckhart begins a sermon on Luke 10:38, interpreting the "virgin" who receives Jesus as a soul that has achieved total detachment from mental concepts and worldly images. He poses the central question of how a person living a rational life can return to the state of "emptiness" they possessed before creation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Sermon 2, "Of the City of the Soul." # Of the City of the Soul.

4

Now pay attention to the distinction to which I want to point you. If I were so rational that all the images that humans have ever received, and which are in God himself, stood rationally within me—and specifically, that I had grasped them in action and in omission without attachment <note>original: *Eigenschaft*. In Eckhart’s usage, this means "possessiveness" or "self-interest." To act without "property" or "attachment" is to act purely for God's sake.</note>, without "before" and without "after," but rather that they stood free and void in this present **moment** <term>moment</term> <note>original: *Nu*. This is Eckhart’s famous term for the "Eternal Now," a state outside of linear time where the soul meets the Divine.</note> according to the dearest will of God, so as to fulfill it without ceasing—then I would truly be a virgin, unhindered by all images, and truly as I was when I was not yet created <note>Eckhart often speaks of our "uncreated" existence, referring to the way we existed as ideas in the mind of God before our physical birth.</note>. Just as the masters say that like and like alone are a matter of unity, so also must a person be chaste and a virgin if they wish to receive the chaste Jesus.

I say further, that there is a **power in the soul** <term>power</term> <note>original: *Kraft*. This refers to the higher faculties of the soul, likely the "intellect" or "spark," which Eckhart views as the site of mystical union.</note> that touches neither time nor flesh; it flows from the spirit and remains in the spirit and is entirely spiritual. In this power, God is altogether greening and blooming in all joy and in all honor, as he is in himself. There is such heartfelt joy and such incomprehensibly great joy that no one can say enough about it. For the eternal Father begets his eternal Son in this power without ceasing, so that this power helps to beget the Son of the Father and [to beget] itself [as] the same...

### Of the City of the Soul.

**Now** <term>Now</term> <note>original: *Nu*. This refers back to the "Eternal Now" discussed on the previous page—a timeless state of being.</note>, always newly greening and without ceasing. Such divine glory is in this **power** <term>power</term> <note>original: *Kraft*. Here, Eckhart refers to the highest faculty of the human soul, often called the "intellect" or "spark," where the soul and God are one.</note>.

There is yet another power, which is also incorporeal: it flows out from the spirit and remains in the spirit and is entirely spiritual. In this power, God is ceaselessly glowing and burning with all his wealth, with all his sweetness, and with all his delight. Truly, in this power there is such great joy and such great, measureless delight that no one can speak or proclaim it truly enough. But I say, if there were a single person who, for one moment, in truth and reason, beheld the delight and the joy herein: all that he could suffer and whatever God willed him to suffer, it would all be little and even void to him; indeed, I say: it would be to him altogether a joy and a benefit.

I have sometimes said there is a power in the spirit that alone is free. At times I have said it is a **tabernacle** <note>original: *Hütte*. One of Eckhart's most famous terms (*Seelenfünklein*), describing the uncreated core of the soul that remains in contact with God.</note>. But I say now: it is neither this nor that. It is not a "something" at all; it is higher above this and that than the heavens are above the earth. Therefore, I name it now in a nobler way than I named it before, and yet

...can behold in any **mode** <term>mode</term> <note>original: *Weise*. For Eckhart, "modes" are the specific ways or characteristics through which things are defined. In this respect, the soul is equal to God and in no other way. What I have told you is true: for this, I present the truth as my witness and my soul as a pledge. That we may be such a city, where Jesus enters and is received and remains eternally in us in the manner I have described, may God grant. Amen.

## On the Nameless God.

Our Lord said: "Woman, the hour is coming and is already here, when the true worshipers will worship the Father in spirit and in truth, for the Father seeks such as these." <note>This is a translation of John 4:23. Eckhart frequently used this passage to argue that "true worship" does not happen in a physical location, but in the "spirit"—the inner depths of the soul.</note>

Now pay attention to the first little word where he says: "The hour is coming and is already here." Whoever wishes to worship the Father must transport themselves into **eternity** <term>eternity</term> <note>original: *Ewigkeit*. For Eckhart, eternity is not an endless duration of time, but a "now" that lacks a before or an after.</note> with their desire and with their confidence. There is a **highest part of the soul** <note>original: *obersten Teil der Seele*. Often referred to as the "spark" or "ground," this is the aspect of human nature that Eckhart believes remains uncreated and eternally connected to God.</note> that stands above time and knows nothing of time nor of the body. Everything that ever happened a thousand years ago—the day that was a thousand years ago—is no further away in eternity than this hour in which I stand right now; and the day that will come in a thousand years, or as far as you can count, is no further away in eternity than this hour in which I now stand.

Now he says: "They worship the Father."

# On the Nameless God. 55

Alas, how many there are who worship the **creature** <term>creature</term> <note>original: *Kreatur*. In Eckhart’s time, this referred to anything created—people, animals, or objects—as opposed to the Creator.</note> and concern themselves with it; these are truly foolish people. As soon as you worship God for the sake of the creature, you are praying for your own harm. For as long as the creature is merely a creature, it carries within itself bitterness, harm, evil, and hardship. Therefore, it is only right that people suffer hardship and bitterness from it. Why? Because that is exactly what they asked for.

All things that exist within time have a "why." Consider someone who asks a person: "Why do you eat?" "So that I have strength." "Why do you sleep?" "For the same reason." And so it is with all things that exist within time. But if one were to ask a good person: "Why do you love God?" "I do not know—for God's sake." "Why do you love the truth?" "For the truth's sake." "Why do you love justice?" "For justice's sake." "Why do you love goodness?" "For goodness' sake." "Why do you live?" "Truly, I do not know! I just like to live." <note>This reflects Eckhart's famous teaching on "living without a why" (*sunder warumbe*). He argues that just as God acts without an external purpose, the perfected soul should act out of its own inner nature rather than for a reward.</note>

The **Masters** <note>original: *Die Meister*. Likely referring to the great Scholastic philosophers like Aristotle or Thomas Aquinas.</note> say that the soul has **two faces**. The upper face gazes at God at all times, and the lower face looks down a little and informs the senses; and the uppermost face is the highest part of the soul, which stands in

The second thing that most of all makes a person the Son is **equanimity**. <term>Equanimity</term> <note>original: *Gleichmut*. Anyone who would take that away from God would be taking away His own essence and His own life. 

That we may thus truly become the Son, may that truth of which I have spoken help us. Amen.

## Of the Innermost Ground.

A master says: “God has become a man, through which the entire human race is exalted and honored. We may well rejoice in this, that Christ, our brother, by his own power has ascended above all the choirs of angels and sits at the right hand of the Father.” <note>A reference to the traditional Christian doctrine of the Ascension.</note> This master spoke quite well; but truly, I do not make much of it. How would it help me if I had a brother who was a rich man and I were a poor one, or if he were wise and I a fool? I say something else and something more urgent: God has not only become man, but he has taken on human nature <term>human nature</term> <note>original: *menschliche Natur*. Eckhart is making a radical distinction: if God only became the individual person Jesus, the rest of humanity remains separate. By taking on the universal "nature" of humanity, God unites with the essence of every human soul.</note>.

The masters <note>Eckhart typically uses "the masters" to refer to the Scholastic theologians of the universities, such as Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great.</note> usually say that all people are equally noble by nature. But I say truly: all the good that all the saints have possessed, and Mary the Mother of God, and Christ according to his humanity <term>humanity</term> <note>original: *Menschtum*. This refers to the human state or the quality of being human that Christ shared with us.</note>, that is mine—

<vocab>God, Man, Human race, Christ, Angel, Nature, Humanity, Mary, Saints</vocab>

<summary>Eckhart begins a new sermon by arguing that the significance of the Incarnation lies not just in the historical person of Jesus, but in God taking on the universal nature of all humanity. He suggests that this shared nature gives every individual access to the same spiritual "riches" possessed by Christ and the saints.</summary>
<keywords>Incarnation, human nature, spiritual equality, nobility of the soul, Christology, Middle High German mysticism</keywords>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Meister Eckhart.</header>

...intrinsic to this nature. Where the Father begets his Son in the innermost ground, there this nature has an in-soaring <note>original: *Hineinschweben*. A mystical term suggesting a fluid, effortless entry or permeation of the soul into the divine essence.</note>. This same nature is one and simple. Here something may well look out and hang upon it, and that is the "one Nothing."

I speak of another and of a weightier matter. Whoever is to stand in the nakedness of this nature without mediation <note>original: *ohne Mittel*. This refers to "unmediated" contact with God, without the help of images, saints, or even thoughts.</note> must have gone out of all personhood <term>personhood</term> <note>original: *Person*. In Eckhart’s usage, this refers to the individual ego or the "social self" that distinguishes one person from another.</note>, so that they wish as much good to a person who is beyond the sea, whom they have never seen face to face, as to the one who is with them and is their dear friend. As long as you wish more good for your own person than for the person you have never seen, so long are you truly in the wrong, and you have never for a single moment gazed into this simple ground. To be sure, you have seen the truth in an abstracted image as in a parable <note>original: *Gleichnis*. A metaphor or likeness that points to truth but is not the truth itself.</note>, but it was not the best way. Secondly, you should be pure of heart, and only that heart is pure which has annihilated all createdness <term>createdness</term> <note>original: *Erschaffenheit*. The quality of being a finite, created thing; to "annihilate" it means to strip away one's attachment to the material world.</note>. Thirdly, you should be free of the "Nothing."

There is a question: what burns in hell? The masters <note>The Scholastic theologians of the medieval universities.</note> usually say: it is self-will <term>self-will</term> <note>original: *Eigenwille*. The individual's ego-driven desires that resist the will of God.</note> that does this. But I say truly: it is the "Nothing" that burns in hell. A parable: take a burning coal and lay it upon my hand.

<summary>Eckhart explains that reaching the "innermost ground" requires total detachment from one's individual ego and a universal love for all humanity. He concludes with a radical teaching that hell is not a physical fire, but the "Nothingness" of a soul clinging to its own will.</summary>

### On the Innermost Ground.

If I said that the coal burned my hand, I would be doing it a great injustice. Should I say what actually burns me? It is "Nothingness" <term>Nothingness</term> <note>original: *Nichts*. Here, Eckhart uses "Nothing" to describe a lack or deficiency. In his analogy, the hand burns because it lacks the "nature of fire." In a spiritual sense, the soul suffers because it lacks the "nature of God."</note> that does this, because the coal has something in itself which my hand does not have. See, it is this very "Nothing" that burns me. For if my hand had everything within it that the coal is and can do, it would possess a complete fire-nature. If someone then took all the fire that ever burned and poured it onto my hand, it could not cause me pain.

In the same way, I say: Because God and all those who stand before the face of God in true blessedness <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*. The state of divine joy or beatitude found in union with God.</note> have something within them which those who are separated from God do not have, this "Nothing" alone torments the souls who are in hell more than self-will <term>self-will</term> <note>original: *Eigenwille*. The individual's insistence on their own ego and desires, which Eckhart views as the primary barrier to God.</note> or any physical fire. I say truly: to the extent that "Nothing" clings to you, to that extent you are imperfect. Therefore, if you wish to be perfect, you must be free of this "Nothing."

Therefore, there is a short saying: "God has sent his only-begotten Son into the world" <note>A reference to 1 John 4:9. Eckhart frequently reinterprets biblical events as internal, psychological, and mystical realities rather than just historical ones.</note>; you should not understand this to mean the *outer* world, as when he ate and drank with us; rather, you should understand it as the *inner* world. Just as surely as the Father, in his simple nature, begets the Son naturally, so surely does he beget him in the innermost part of the spirit, and that is the *inner* world. Here, God’s ground <term>ground</term> <note>original: *Grund*. The 'Seelengrund' is the most essential, divine point of the soul where it is indistinguishable from God's own essence.</note> is my ground, and my ground is God's

<vocab>Nothingness, God, Blessedness, Self-will, Only-begotten Son, Outer world, Inner world, God's ground</vocab>

<summary>Eckhart uses the analogy of a burning coal to explain that spiritual suffering is caused by a lack of divine nature ("Nothingness"). He concludes that the union of God and the soul occurs in the "inner world," where the divine and human "ground" become one.</summary>
