# Meister Eckhart.

<term>Ground</term> <note>original: *Grund*. In Eckhart’s theology, the "Ground" is the deepest part of the soul where the human essence and the divine essence are one and the same.</note>. Here I live outside of my own self, just as God lives outside of His own. To the person who has looked into this ground for even a moment, a thousand pounds of beaten red gold are worth no more than a counterfeit penny. Out of this innermost ground, you should perform all your works **without a "Why."** <note>original: *ohne ein Warum*. This is one of Eckhart's most famous concepts—the idea that the soul should act spontaneously and out of its own nature, rather than for the sake of a future reward like heaven.</note> I say truly: as long as you perform your works for the sake of the kingdom of heaven, or for God's sake, or for your own eternal <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*.</note> from the outside, for so long you are truly in the wrong. You may certainly be permitted to go on that way, but it is not the best. 

For truly, if you believe that you come closer to God through <term>inwardness</term> <note>original: *Innigkeit*. A deep, heartfelt devotion or internal spiritual focus.</note>, through devotion, through compliance, or special arrangements than you do at the hearth or in the stable, you are doing nothing other than if you took God, wrapped a cloak around His head, and shoved Him under a bench. For whoever seeks God in a <term>way</term> <note>original: *Weise*. This refers to a specific method, ritual, or mode of behavior. Eckhart teaches that God is "wayless" and found beyond specific religious techniques.</note>, takes the way and leaves God, who is hidden within that way. 

But whoever seeks God **without a way**, takes Him as He is in Himself; and such a person lives with the **Son**, and he is **life** itself. If someone asked life for a thousand years: "Why do you live?" and if it were to answer, it would say nothing other than: "I live because I live." This is because,

<summary>Eckhart describes the "Ground" of the soul as a place beyond selfish desire, where one must act "without a why" rather than for rewards. He argues that God is found just as easily in daily chores (the stable) as in private devotion if one abandons specific religious "ways."</summary>
<keywords>Ground, without a why, spontaneity, religious practice, union with God, inwardness, the Son, life itself</keywords>

### On the Innermost Ground.

...that life lives out of its own <term>ground</term> <note>original: *Grund*. The soul's deepest point where it meets God.</note> and springs from its own essence: therefore it lives **without a "Why,"** <note>original: *ohne Warum*. This is Eckhart's core ethical teaching: that a person should act out of their own nature without seeking a reward or a reason, much like a rose blooms simply because it blooms.</note> in that it lives for its own sake. Now, if someone were to ask a true person who acts out of their own ground: "Why do you perform your works?"—if they were to answer correctly, they would say nothing other than: "I work because I work."

Where the <term>creature</term> <note>original: *Kreatur*. Referring to the created, finite world and the human ego, as opposed to the uncreated God.</note> ends, there God begins to be. Now, God desires nothing else from you than that you should go out of yourself, in your creaturely way, and let God be God in you. Even the smallest creaturely <term>image</term> <note>original: *Bild*. Any mental concept, attachment, or worldly distraction that occupies the mind.</note> that forms within you is as large as God. Why? Because it robs you of an entire God. For where this image enters, God and His whole <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: *Gottheit*. The absolute, essence-filled nature of the Divine that exists beyond the three persons of the Trinity.</note> must give way. But where this image departs, God enters in.

God desires so powerfully that you go out of yourself, in your creaturely way, as if all His <term>bliss</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*. Divine happiness or salvation.</note> depended on it. Truly, dear human, what harm does it do you to grant God that He be God within you? Go out of your self for God’s sake, and God will go out of His for yours. When these two go out, what remains is a simple One. In this One, the Father begets His Son in the innermost wellspring. There blossoms...

<summary>Eckhart explains that to experience God, one must act "without a why" and empty the soul of all "creaturely images." This state of emptiness allows for a mystical union where God and the soul become "one" and the divine birth occurs.</summary>

<keywords>Ground, without a why, detachment, Godhead, divine birth, creature, bliss, images</keywords>

...the Holy Spirit, and there springs forth in God a <term>will</term> <note>original: *Wille*. In Eckhart’s thought, the will is the power of the soul that can either attach itself to worldly things or turn back toward its divine source.</note> that belongs to the soul. And as long as the will stands untouched by all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: *Kreaturen*. Meaning all finite, created things or beings in the world.</note> and by all <term>createdness</term> <note>original: *Erschaffenheit*. The state of being a limited, created thing rather than the eternal Creator.</note>, so long is the will free. Christ says: "No one comes into heaven, except for the one who has come from heaven." All things are created out of <term>nothing</term> <note>original: *Nichts*. A central concept in medieval philosophy: if God is "Being," then anything not God is "non-being" or nothingness unless sustained by Him.</note>; therefore, their actual origin is nothing. Insofar as this noble will inclines toward creatures, it flows away with these creatures into their nothingness. Now the question is, whether this will flows away in such a manner that it can never again return? The masters usually say that it never returns, insofar as it has flowed away in time. But I say: if this will turns itself for a moment away from itself and from all createdness back toward its origin, then the will stands there in a true, free manner and is free; and in this moment, all lost time is restored. People often say to me: "Pray for me." Then I think: Why do you go out? Why do you not stay within yourselves and reach into your own treasure? For you carry all truth essentially within you. That we may so truly remain in him and possess all truth <term>without mediation</term> <note>original: *ohne Mittel*. This means experiencing God directly, without the need for mental images, rituals, or "middle-men."</note> and undivided in true <term>bliss</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*. Divine happiness or the state of being one with God.</note>—may God grant this. Amen.

<summary>Eckhart explains that the soul's will remains free only when it avoids attachment to created things, which lead to "nothingness." He concludes by urging listeners to seek God within themselves rather than through external prayers or mediators.</summary>

## Of the Fullness of Time

"In time, the angel Gabriel was sent from God." <note>Luke 1:26</note> In what time? In the sixth month, when John leaped in his mother's womb. <note>Referring to John the Baptist's reaction to the presence of Mary, who was pregnant with Jesus.</note> Now, if someone were to ask me: Why do we pray, or why do we fast, or why do we perform all our works? I would answer: For this reason, that God may be <term>born in our soul</term> <note>original: *Gottesgeburt in der Seele*. This is the central pillar of Eckhart's mysticism—the idea that the Divine Word is eternally born within the human soul just as it is within the Trinity.</note>. Why is all Scripture written, and why did God create the angelic nature and all the world? For this reason alone: that God may be born in the soul. The nature of all grain intends wheat; the nature of all treasure intends gold; all birth intends man. As a master says, there is no animal that does not have something in common with man in time.

Saint Paul says: "In the <term>fullness of time</term> <note>original: *Vollendung der Zeit*. Referring to Galatians 4:4.</note>, God sent His Son." Saint Augustine was asked what that might be, the "fullness of time"? The fullness of time is when the day is no more: then the day is fulfilled. It

To receive the Creator, the soul must be empty of all "created" attachments.</note>. It is a necessary truth that time cannot touch God or the soul. If time could touch the soul, she would not be the soul. If God could be touched by time, He would not be God.

Another [definition of the] <term>fullness of time</term> <note>original: *Vollendung der Zeit*. Also translated as the "perfection" or "completion" of time.</note>! Whoever possessed the art and the power to gather time and everything that has ever happened in six thousand years, or will yet happen until the end of the world, and if they could draw all of that into a <term>present Now</term> <note>original: *gegenwärtiges Nu*. That is the "Now" of eternity, where the soul recognizes all things in God as new and as fresh, and with the same joy that I feel in the present moment. 

The smallest power in my soul is wider than the vast heavens. I speak of <term>reason</term> <note>original: *Vernunft*. For Eckhart, reason/intellect is the highest faculty of the soul, capable of transcending physical boundaries.</note>, in which there is expanse upon expanse; through it, I am as near to a place a thousand miles away as I am to the place where I stand right now. The masters say that the multitude of angels is beyond counting; their number cannot be grasped by the mind. But for one who could distinguish without number and without multitude, for that person a hundred would be as one.

## A Second Sermon on the Nameless God.

When the soul enters the nameless city, there she finds rest; where all things are God in God, there she rests. This city of the soul, which is God, is unnamed. I say that God is unspoken. It seemed to one of our oldest masters—who found the truth long, long before <insert>the manifestation of</insert> the birth of God, and before the Christian faith existed as it does now—that everything he might say about things carried something foreign and untrue within it; therefore, he wished to remain silent. 

He did not even want to say: "Give me bread," or "give me something to drink." He refused to speak of things for this reason: because he could not speak of them as purely as they had sprung from the <term>First Cause</term> <note>original: *Ursache*. In medieval philosophy, this refers to God as the ultimate origin from which all existence flows. The master mentioned is likely the Greek philosopher Cratylus, who famously chose to only move his finger rather than speak, believing language too unstable to capture reality.</note>; therefore, he preferred to remain silent, and indicated his basic needs only with signs of his fingers. Since he could not speak even of created things, it is fitting... 

<summary>This page introduces the concept of God as a "Nameless City" and discusses the inadequacy of language. It recounts a story of an ancient philosopher who practiced total silence because words could not capture the pure essence of things as they exist in God.</summary>

<keywords>Nameless God, Silence, First Cause, Mystical Theology, Soul's Rest, Ineffability</keywords>

## Concerning Good Gifts.

I am often accustomed to saying a small word, and it is also true: every day we call out and cry in the Our Father <note>original: "Paternoster"; the central prayer of the Christian liturgy</note>: Lord, thy will be done! But when His will actually happens, we want to become angry and do not surrender ourselves to His will. Whatever He does ought to seem the best to us and please us above all else. Those who take it for the best in this way remain always in complete peace. 

But you sometimes say: "Ah, if only it had turned out differently, it would have been better," or "If it hadn't happened this way, perhaps it would have turned out better." As long as you think this, you will never gain peace. You should take everything for the very best.

I once said: whatever can truly be expressed in words <note>original: "gewortet", a mystical term referring to the process of inner truth taking on the "form of a word"</note> must come from within and proceed from its own inner form; it must not enter from the outside. This truly lives in the innermost part of the soul. There, all things are to you—

<summary>This page exhorts the reader to find peace by unconditionally accepting God's will and suggests that true spiritual expression must arise from the inner soul rather than external stimuli.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Will, inner peace, Our Father, Paternoster, soul, spiritual form, acceptance</keywords>

# Concerning Good Gifts.

...present and living and seeking inwardly, and they are in the best and highest state. Why do you not feel this? Because you are not at home there. The higher in rank a thing is, the more universal it is. I share my senses with the animals and life with the trees. Being <note>original: "Das Sein"; in medieval philosophy, this refers to the most fundamental state of existence which connects all things to God</note> is even deeper within me; this I have in common with all creatures <note>original: "Kreaturen"; this refers to all created things in the universe, including inanimate objects</note>. The heavens are more than everything else that exists alongside them; therefore, they are also higher in rank. Love stands high in rank because it is universal. It seems difficult that our Lord has commanded that one should love their fellow Christian as themselves. The common man usually understands this to mean that one should love others in the same sense in which one loves oneself. No, it should not be so. One should love them just as much as oneself, and that is not difficult. If you would take good note of it: it is worth more as a reward than as a commandment. The commandment seems hard, but the reward is desirable. Whoever loves God as they should and must (whether they want to or not), and as all creatures love Him, must love their fellow human as themselves and rejoice in their joys and honors and strive for them as for their own honor, and for what belongs to the stranger as for what is their own. And thus, such a person is always in joy, in honor, and in benefit, so—

<summary>The author argues that "Being" is the most fundamental and universal gift shared by all creation, and that true love for God makes the commandment to love one's neighbor an effortless source of joy rather than a burden.</summary>

...he is completely as if in the Kingdom of Heaven <note>original: "Himmelreich"</note> and so he possesses greater joy than if he rejoiced in his own good alone.

And know in truth: if your own honor matters more to you than that of another, then it is wrong. Know that if you seek your own interest, you will never find God, because you are not seeking God purely. You are seeking something *with* God, and you act exactly as if someone made a candle out of God to search for something; once the object is found, the candle is thrown away. This is what you do: whatever you seek alongside God—be it profit, reward, inwardness <note>original: "Innerlichkeit"; referring to a private spiritual feeling or state</note>, or whatever it may be—is actually nothing. You are seeking nothing, and therefore you find nothing. All creatures <note>original: "Kreaturen"; refers to all created things in the universe, including objects, plants, and animals</note> are pure nothingness. I do not say that they are insignificant or "a little bit" of something: they are nothing at all. Whoever possesses no <term>Being</term> <note>original: "Sein"; in medieval philosophy, this refers to the fundamental essence of existence. Eckhart argues that true "Being" belongs to God alone, while created things only "exist" because God is currently sustaining them</note> is nothing. All creatures have no Being of their own, for their existence depends entirely on the presence of God. If God turned away for a single moment, they would vanish into nothingness. 

I have often said, and it remains true: If someone took the whole world plus God, they would have no more than if they had God alone. All creatures without God have no more value than a gnat would have without God—exactly the same, neither less nor more.

Truly, now pay heed to a true word.

# On Good Gifts.

If a person gave a thousand pounds of gold so that churches and monasteries could be built with it, that would be a great thing. Yet, the person who could regard a thousand pounds as nothing would have given much more; they would have done far more than the other. When God created all creatures <note>original: "Kreaturen"; in this context, it refers to all created things in the universe—stars, stones, plants, and animals—as opposed to the Creator</note>, they were so pitiful and so narrow that he could not move within them. However, he made the soul so much like himself—even exactly the same—so that he could give himself to the soul; for anything else he might give her, she does not value. God must give me himself as my own, just as he belongs to himself, or nothing is mine and nothing tastes good to me <note>The word "taste" refers to the mystical concept of *sapientia*, derived from the Latin *sapere*, which means both "to taste" and "to be wise." It suggests a direct, experiential knowledge of God</note>. Whoever wishes to receive him so completely must have surrendered themselves entirely and departed from their own self.

I was once asked what the Father in heaven was doing? I replied: He is giving birth to his Son, and this work is so delightful to him and pleases him so well that he does nothing else, and from them both the Holy Spirit blossoms. When the Father gives birth to his Son in me, then I am that Son and no other; among humans, there is one person here and another there, but there <note>Referring to the spiritual union of the soul with God</note> I am the same and no other.

God’s nature is to give, and his very essence depends on his giving to us, provided we are humble. If we are not, then we receive

## On Inexpressible Things.

“Do not fear those who want to kill you physically, for they cannot kill the soul,” <note>original: "Fürchtet nicht, die euch körperlich töten wollen..."; a paraphrase of Matthew 10:28</note> for spirit does not kill spirit. Spirit gives life to the spirit. Those who want to kill you are blood and flesh, and those die together. The noblest thing in man is the blood, provided it is of good will. But the worst thing in man is the blood, if it is of evil will. If the blood triumphs over the flesh, then man is humble, patient, and chaste, and possesses all virtue within himself. But if the flesh triumphs over the blood, then man becomes arrogant, angry, and unchaste, and possesses every vice <note>original: "Untugend"; literally "un-virtue," meaning a lack of moral excellence or a bad habit</note> within himself.

Now pay attention, I will now say what I have never said before <note>This is a characteristic rhetorical device used by Meister Eckhart to signal a particularly profound or provocative mystical insight</note>. When God created the heavens, the earth, and all creatures, God did not "work"; he had nothing to work upon; in him there was also no "work" <note>Eckhart distinguishes between "doing" or "working" (which implies effort or a change in state) and the pure, effortless flow of the Divine Being</note>. Then God spoke: “Let us make one who is our equal.” <note>original: "Wir machen einen Gleichen"; a mystical interpretation of Genesis 1:26, "Let us make man in our image." Eckhart emphasizes the equality of the soul's essence with God</note> Creating is an easy thing, which

one does, when and how one wants. But what I make, I make myself out of myself and in myself and impress my image <note>original: "Bild"; in Eckhart’s thought, this refers to the *imago Dei* or the divine spark within the soul</note> completely and entirely into it.

When God made man, he worked in the soul his work of likeness, his active and his everlasting work. That work was so great that it was nothing other than the soul: it was the work of God. God’s nature, his essence, and his Godhead <term>Godhead: (German: *Gottheit*) In Eckhart's theology, this refers to the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God, beyond the Trinity of Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</term> depend on this: that he must work in the soul. God’s blessing, God’s blessing! When God works in the soul, then he loves his work. The work is love, and love is God. God loves himself and his nature, his essence, and his Godhead. In the love with which God loves me, he loves all creatures. Not as creatures does he love them, but rather the creatures as God. With the love with which God loves himself, he loves all things.

Now I want to say what I have never said before <note>Again, Eckhart uses this phrase to emphasize a radical mystical truth: the total collapse of the distinction between God and the soul in the act of divine self-knowledge</note>. God perceives and tastes himself. With the taste with which God tastes himself, he tastes all creatures—not as creatures, but the creatures as God. In the taste with which God tastes himself, he tastes all things. Now pay attention. All creatures take their course toward their highest perfection—

<summary>Eckhart describes the soul as God's most significant work, asserting that God's love for the soul is identical to His love for Himself. He introduces the mystical concept of "tasting" or experiencing God, suggesting that God experiences all of creation through His own self-perception.</summary>

The sun casts its bright radiance upon all creatures, and whatever the sun casts its radiance upon, that it draws into itself, and yet it does not lose its radiant nature. All creatures give up their lives for the sake of their essence. Wait for what you all are doing. Now I return to my inner and outer person. I contemplate the lilies of the field and their bright radiance and their color and all their petals. ->Meister Eckhart.<-

[The outer] person tastes nothing as a creature <term>Creature: (German: *Kreatur*) In Eckhart’s vocabulary, this refers to any created thing or being, which is distinct from the uncreated, eternal nature of God.</term>, but as a gift of God. And my innermost person tastes them not as a gift of God, but as ever and eternal. I take a basin with water and place a mirror within it and set it under the wheel of the sun; then the sun casts its bright radiance out of the wheel and out of the ground <term>Ground: (German: *Grund*) A vital term for Eckhart, meaning the absolute depth or essence of a thing—the point where the human soul and God are one.</term> of the sun, and yet it does not pass away. The reflection of the mirror in the sun is in the sun. It is the sun, and yet it is what it is. So it is with God. God is with His nature, His essence, and His Godhead in the soul, and yet He is not the soul. The reflection of the soul is in God. It is God, and yet she <note>In German, soul (*Seele*) is feminine; Eckhart often speaks of the soul as "she."</note> is what she is. God there becomes all creatures—God’s speaking there becomes God.

When I stood in the ground, in the bottom, in the flow, and in the source of the Godhead, no one asked me where I was going or what I was doing: there was no one there to ask me. When I flowed out, all creatures spoke "God." If one asked me: "Brother Eckhart, when did you go out of the house?" <note>The "house" is a metaphor for the absolute unity of the divine essence from which the soul "proceeds" into created existence.</note> I was already inside. In this way, all creatures speak of God. And why do they speak nothing of the Godhead? Everything that is in the Godhead is one, and of that, nothing can be spoken. God acts, the Godhead acts <note>The text ends abruptly here; in Eckhart's typical theology, he usually distinguishes that while God acts as Creator, the Godhead remains in "non-action" or stillness.</note>

He describes the "Ground" of the Godhead as a place of absolute unity where even the distinction between the seeker and the sought vanishes.</summary>

...**not**, she has nothing to perform, for in her there is no work. God and Godhead are as different as acting and non-acting <term>Acting and Non-acting: (German: *Wirken und Nichtwirken*) This distinguishes between God the Creator, who interacts with the world, and the Godhead, which remains in eternal, silent rest.</term>. When I return to God, I do not take on a form; thus my breakthrough <note>original: *Mündung*. Literally "mouth" or "estuary." Following the river imagery, Eckhart uses this to describe the soul’s "out-flowing" return into the source of the Divine.</note> is much higher than my origin. I alone bring all creatures out of their own understanding <term>Understanding: (German: *Vernunft*) Not merely logic, but the highest faculty of the soul capable of perceiving divine truth.</term> into my understanding, so that they are one in me. When I enter the ground, the bottom, the flow, and the source of the Godhead, no one asks me where I come from or where I have been. There, no one missed me; there, all things cease.

Whoever has understood this sermon, I wish them well of it. Had there been no person here, I would have had to preach it to this wooden post. There are some poor folk who go home and say: "I will sit on my chair, eat my bread, and serve God." But I say in truth, these people must remain gone astray, and they can never achieve or attain what those others achieve who follow God in poverty <term>Poverty: (German: *Armut*) In Eckhart's mysticism, "spiritual poverty" means being empty of self-will, knowledge, and desire.</term> and nakedness <term>Nakedness: (German: *Entblösstheit*) The state of being stripped of all worldly attachments and mental images to face God directly.</term>. Amen.

## Of the Suffering of God.

A teacher says:
> You rich God, how well it is for me, when my love bears fruit for you!

Our Lord speaks to every loving soul:
> "I became human for you; if you do not become gods for me <term>Become gods: (German: *Götter seid*) This refers to the concept of deification or 'theosis'—a central theme in Rhenish mysticism where the soul, through grace, participates in the divine nature of God.</term>, then you do me an injustice. Within my divine nature, I dwelt in your human nature, so that no one recognized my divine power and men saw me walking just like any other man. In the same way, you should shelter yourselves with your human nature within my divine nature, so that no one may recognize your human weakness in you, and that your life may be so entirely divine that nothing is recognized in you but God."

And this does not happen through our adopting sweet words and spiritual gestures, or by standing in the odor of sanctity <term>Odor of Sanctity: (German: *Geruch der Heiligkeit*) A traditional hagiographic term implying that a person’s holiness is so profound it manifests as a metaphorical or literal sweet fragrance.</term>, or by our names being carried far and wide, or by being loved by the "Friends of God" <term>Friends of God: (German: *Gottes Freunde*) A 14th-century circle of mystics and pious laypeople in the Rhineland who sought a direct, personal experience of God's presence.</term>, or that—

It warns that true holiness is an internal state rather than an outward reputation or performance of piety.</summary>

...we are so pampered and coddled by God that it seems to us as if God has forgotten all other creatures except for us alone, and we imagine that whatever we desire from God must happen immediately. No, not so! That is not what God demands of us; it is quite a different matter.

He wants us to be found free and unmoved <term>Unmoved: (German: *unbewegt*) A core concept in Meister Eckhart’s philosophy, referring to "detachment" (*Gelassenheit*), where the soul remains steady and peaceful regardless of external circumstances or internal emotions.</term> when people say behind our backs that we are false and untruthful, or whatever else they might say to strip us of our good reputation. And he wants us to remain unmoved even when, while we do the very best in all our works that we can devise, people turn those works into the very worst thing they can imagine. He wants us to endure this not only from fellow human beings but also from God himself, so that he withdraws the comfort of his presence and acts just as if a wall were erected between us and him. ...behaves toward us as if he closed his eyes to us, so that he wishes neither to see nor hear us, and he leaves us to stand alone in the struggle with our needs, just as Christ was forsaken by his Father: see, then we should hide ourselves in his divine nature, so that we might stand so unshaken in our desolation, helping ourselves with nothing else than the word that Christ spoke: "Father, let all your will be accomplished in me."

God is a being of such a nature that he is best recognized through **Nothing**. Why through **Nothing**? Because one sets aside all <term>means: (German: *Mittel*) The intermediaries, images, or concepts that stand between the soul and God. Eckhart argues that to experience God directly, one must move beyond all created things and even holy concepts.</term>. It is not enough merely to renounce the world and possess virtue; rather, I must also leave virtue behind if I wish to see God immediately <note>original: *unmittelbar* – meaning "without means" or "without an intermediary."</note>; not in the sense that I renounce virtue, but rather that virtue should dwell within me essentially, and I should dwell *above* virtue. When a person’s thoughts can no longer touch any *thing*, only then do they touch God. A <note>pagan master: Likely a reference to Aristotle, whom Eckhart often cites to provide a philosophical foundation for his mystical claims.</note> says that nature can do nothing beyond nature. Therefore, God cannot be recognized by any creature. If he is to be recognized, it must happen in a light that is above nature.

## On the Unity of Things.

As I was walking here today, I pondered how I might preach to you so reasonably that you would understand me well, and I thought of a **parable** <note>original: "Gleichnis." In this context, it functions as a philosophical analogy to explain the nature of perception and unity.</note>. If you were able to understand this properly, you would understand my meaning and the **ground** <note>original: "Grund." This is a central term in Eckhart’s mysticism, referring to the deepest, uncreated essence of both the soul and God where they are indistinguishable.</note> of all my views that I have always preached.

The parable was about my eyes and a piece of wood. When my eye is opened, it is my eye. When it is closed, it is the same eye; nothing is taken from the wood nor added to it because of the seeing. Now pay close attention. If it happens that my eye is in itself one and unified, and is opened and cast upon the wood with a **gaze** <note>original: "Ansehen." This refers to the active power of sight or the act of looking.</note>, then each remains what it is, and yet in the **actuality** <note>original: "Wirksamkeit." This suggests the "working" or the "operation" of the senses in the moment of perception.</note> of the gaze they become as one, so that one might say: "Eye-wood," and the wood is my eye. If, however, the wood were without

<vocab>Unity, Parable, Eye, Wood, Actuality, Gaze</vocab>

<summary>Meister Eckhart introduces a philosophical analogy using the act of sight to explain how two distinct things—an eye and a piece of wood—become a single unit during the act of perception.</summary>
<keywords>unity, perception, the Ground, analogy, epistemology, Meister Eckhart, oneness</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of the eye and the wood from the previous page, expanding on how the "actuality" of a faculty creates a deeper unity than mere physical presence. It transitions from physical perception to the mystical "light" in the soul and the concept of the birth of God within the human essence.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

# Meister Eckhart.

...matter and entirely spiritual, like the sight of my eyes; thus one could say in truth that in the **actuality** <term>Wirksamkeit: The active operation or "working" of a faculty in the moment it functions.</term> of my sight, the wood and my eye consist of one **essence** <term>Wesen: The fundamental nature or being of a thing.</term>. If this is true of physical things, it is much more true of spiritual things. You should know, my eye has much more unity with the eyes of a sheep that is across the sea and which I have never seen, than with my ears, with which it is nevertheless one in essence <note>Meaning they are both part of the same physical body.</note>; and this is because the sheep’s eye has the same actuality as my eye. Therefore, I attribute to them more unity in their activity than to my eyes and ears, for these are different in their activity.

I have sometimes spoken of a **light** <note>This refers to the "spark of the soul" (Seelenfünklein), which Eckhart believed was a divine, uncreated element within every human being.</note> that is in the soul and that is uncreated and uncreatable. I am accustomed to always touch upon this very light in my preaching; and this light perceives God immediately and without veils, pure as He is in Himself. This perception takes place in the actuality of the **inward-birthing** <term>Hineingebärung: The mystical process of God being born in the soul, and the soul being born back into God.</term>. Thus I can truly say that this light has more unity with God than with any other power <note>Referring to the various faculties of the soul, such as memory, intellect, or will.</note>, with which it is nonetheless one in essence. For you should know, this light in the essence of my soul is no higher in rank than the lowest or most common...

<vocab>Meister Eckhart, Actuality, Essence, Unity, Light, Soul, Uncreated, Inward-birthing, God</vocab>

<summary>Eckhart explains that things are more unified by their shared function (actuality) than by their physical proximity, using the example of eyes and ears. He then applies this to the "uncreated light" of the soul, which achieves a perfect unity with God through the mystical "inward-birthing."</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, divine light, soul's spark, uncreated essence, mystical union, inward-birthing, perception and unity</keywords>

<page-num>89</page-num>

# On the Unity of Things.

...power <note>Referring to the soul's lower faculties.</note> that can be afflicted by hunger or thirst, frost or heat, and this is because the essence is simple. If one therefore considers the powers within the essence, they are all one and equal in rank; but if one considers them in their works, then one is much nobler and higher than the other.

Therefore I say: if a person turns away from themselves and from all created things, then to the extent that you do this, you will be united and blessed in the **spark of the soul** <term>Fünklein der Seele: A central concept in Eckhart’s mysticism, referring to the innermost, uncreated part of the human spirit that is identical with God.</term>, which has never touched time or space. This spark withdraws from all creatures and desires only God as He is in Himself. It is not satisfied with the Father or the Son or the Holy Spirit, nor with the three persons insofar as each stands for itself in its own attribute <note>Eckhart suggests that the soul's core seeks something even deeper than the Trinity of Persons—it seeks the unified source from which they emerge.</note>. I say truly, this very light is not satisfied with the particularity of the fruitful nature of the divine nature. I will say even more, which sounds even more wonderful: I say in good truth, this light is not satisfied with the simple, motionless divine **essence** <term>Wesen: Here meaning the Godhead as an unchanging state of being.</term>, which neither gives nor takes; rather, it wants to know where this essence comes from. It wants to go into the simple **ground** <term>Grund: The absolute, undifferentiated foundation of reality where the distinction between "God" and "soul" disappears.</term>, into the **silent desert** <note>original: "stille Wüste." A metaphor for the Godhead, signifying a state of absolute stillness and lack of "features" or "distinctions."</note>, where nothing distinct—neither Father, nor Son, nor Holy Spirit—has ever penetrated; into the inner—

<vocab>Spark of the soul, God, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, silent desert, ground, divine essence</vocab>

## How Jesus Pulled on the Cord.

"No one can come to me, unless my Father draws him" (John 6:44) <note>original: "Nemo potest ad me venire, nisi pater meus traxerit eum"</note>. These words our Lord Jesus Christ spoke with his sweet mouth in the Gospel, and they mean: "No one can come to me, except for the one whom my Father draws."

Now we should know that before our Lord Jesus Christ was born, the heavenly Father pulled with all his might for five thousand two hundred years <note>Medieval scholars, following certain chronologies of the Bible, estimated the time between the Creation of the world and the birth of Christ to be approximately 5,200 years.</note>, without being able to pull a single person into the kingdom of heaven. When the Son saw that the Father had exerted himself and pulled so powerfully and yet had achieved nothing, he said to the Father: "I will pull them with the cords of Adam" <term>Cords of Adam: A reference to Hosea 11:4. Preachers often interpreted these "human cords" as the humanity of Christ, suggesting that humans are more easily drawn to God through Christ's relatable human nature than through the Father's overwhelming divine power.</term>; just as if he were saying: I see well, Father, that with all your power you can achieve nothing; therefore, with my wisdom, I will pull them with the cords of Adam. Because of this, the Son descended from the

## Concerning the Knowledge of God.

Our dear Lord says that the Kingdom of God is near to us. Truly, the Kingdom of God is within us, and Saint Paul says that our salvation <note>Saint Paul refers here to Romans 13:11: "For now is our salvation nearer than when we believed."</note> is nearer to us than we believe. Now you should know how the Kingdom of God is near to us. Regarding this, we must observe the meaning very carefully. For if I were a king and did not know it myself, I would not be a king. But if I had the firm conviction that I were a king, and all people thought and believed it along with me, then I would be a king and all the king’s wealth would be mine. In the same way, our blessedness <note>original: "Seligkeit." This term denotes a state of supreme happiness and spiritual fulfillment in God.</note> also depends on this: that one recognizes and knows the highest good, which is God himself. I have a power in my soul that is entirely receptive to God. I am as certain of this as I am that I live: that nothing is as near to me as God. God is nearer to me than I am to myself; my very being <note>original: "Wesen." This refers to one's fundamental essence or core existence.</note> depends on it,

# Concerning the Knowledge of God.
### 7

...that God is near and present to me. He is just as near to a stone and a piece of wood, but they do not know it. If the wood knew God and recognized how near He is to it—just as the highest angel recognizes it—the wood would be as blessed <note>original: "selig." In this mystical context, blessedness refers to the state of divine union and perfect happiness.</note> as the highest angel. And for this reason, a human being is more blessed than a piece of wood: because he recognizes God and knows how near God is to him. His blessedness does not come from the fact that God is in him and is so near to him, nor that he possesses God, but rather from this: that he recognizes God and how near He is, and that he is one who knows and loves God. Such a person shall recognize that the Kingdom of God is near.

When I think of the Kingdom of God, a deep silence falls upon me because of its greatness; for the Kingdom of God is God himself with all His riches. The Kingdom of God is no small thing: if one were to think of all the worlds that God could create, that is still not the Kingdom of God. The soul in which the Kingdom of God shines and which recognizes the Kingdom of God does not need preaching or teaching; she is taught by Him and comforted by the promise of eternal life. He who knows and recognizes how near the Kingdom of God is to him can say with Jacob: "God is in this place, and I did not know it." <note>original: "Gott ist an diesem Ort und ich wusste es nicht." This is a reference to Genesis 28:16, where Jacob awakes from his dream of the ladder to heaven.</note>

God is equally near in all creatures <term>creatures (Kreaturen): in medieval theology, this refers to any created thing, whether animate or inanimate.</term>. The wise man says: "God has spread His nets and snares..."

<summary>This page explains that while God is equally present in all things, only humans have the capacity to be "blessed" through the conscious recognition and love of that presence. It describes the Kingdom of God as the internal realization of God's infinite nature within the soul.</summary>

### On the Knowledge of God.
->7*<-

once and for all died to the world, detached, and raised above it. Heaven is pure and clear without any stains; neither time nor space touches heaven. All physical things have no place within it. It is also not within time; its revolution is incredibly swift, its course is without time, yet from its course, time originates <note>In medieval cosmology, the movement of the celestial spheres was thought to create the measure of time for the earthly realm. By placing God "above" this, Eckhart emphasizes His eternal nature.</note>. Nothing hinders the soul so much in the knowledge of God as time and space. Time and space are fragments <note>original: "Stücke." This suggests pieces or parts of a whole, implying a fractured reality compared to the divine unity.</note> and God is one. Therefore, if the soul is to know God, it must know Him above time and above space; for God is neither this nor that, like these things of multiplicity <term>multiplicity (Mannigfaltigkeit): the state of being varied, complex, or many; for Eckhart, this is the opposite of God’s absolute, simple oneness</term>; for God is one.

If the soul is to know God, it must have no fellowship with nothingness. Whoever sees God recognizes that all creatures are nothing. When one creature is compared with another, it seems beautiful and is "something"; but if one wishes to compare it with God, it is nothing.

I say more: if the soul is to know God, it must also forget itself and must lose itself; for as long as it sees and knows itself, it does not see or know God. When it loses itself for God's sake and leaves all things behind, it finds itself again in God, because it knows God, and then

If I wish to know the highest Good and eternal goodness, truly, I must know it as it is good in itself, not as goodness is divided <note>Eckhart suggests that in the physical world, "goodness" is seen only in fragments (e.g., a good meal, a good person), but in God, goodness is an undivided, absolute whole.</note>. If I wish to know the true essence <term>essence (Wesen): the fundamental nature or "being" of something; its most basic reality</term>, I must know it as being is in itself—that is, in God—and not as it is divided among created beings.

In God alone is the whole of the divine essence. In a single human being, the whole of humanity is not present, for one human is not all humans. But in God, the soul recognizes the whole of humanity and all things in the Highest, for she recognizes them in their essence. A person who lives in a beautifully painted house knows much more about it than another who never went inside and yet wanted to speak much about it <note>Eckhart frequently uses the "house" analogy to distinguish between intellectual study and direct spiritual experience.</note>. Therefore, it is as certain to me as I live and God lives: if the soul wishes to know God, she must know Him beyond time and space. And such a soul knows God and knows how near the Kingdom of God is—that is, God with all His richness. ...she expands herself, so that she might receive much and He might give much to her.

No one should think that it is difficult to arrive at this, although it sounds difficult and is indeed truly difficult in the beginning, in the parting from and dying to all things <note>This refers to "Gelassenheit" or detachment—the process of letting go of worldly desires and the ego to make room for the divine.</note>. But once one enters into it, no life is easier, more joyful, or more lovely; for God takes great pains to be with man at all times, and teaches him so that He may bring him to Himself, if only man is willing to follow Him. No person ever desired anything as much as God desires to bring man to the point of knowing Him. God is always ready, but we are very unready; God is near to us, but we are far from Him; God is inside, but we are outside; God is at home, but we are in a foreign land. The Prophet <note>Likely referring to the Psalmist (Psalm 18:19), whom Eckhart often cites as "The Prophet."</note> says: “God leads the righteous through a narrow path into the broad street, so that they may come into the width and the breadth,” that is: into true freedom of the spirit <term>freedom of the spirit: a state where the soul is no longer bound by earthly attachments or the "self," acting instead out of pure divine love</term>, which has become one spirit with God. May God grant that we all follow Him, so that He may bring us into Himself. Amen.

## On Poverty.

Blessedness <note>original: "Seligkeit" – This term refers to the state of supreme happiness or the divine joy found in the presence of God.</note> opened its mouth of wisdom and spoke: "Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven." All angels and all saints and everything that was ever born must remain silent when this eternal wisdom of the Father speaks; for all the wisdom of angels and of all creatures is purely nothing before the wisdom of God, which is fathomless <term>fathomless: the original German "grundlos" refers to God being without a "ground" or limit, a key concept in German mysticism suggesting that God's essence is an infinite abyss.</term>. This wisdom has said that the poor are blessed. 

Now, there are two kinds of poverty. One is an external poverty, and it is good and much to be praised in the person who practices it voluntarily for the sake of our Lord Jesus Christ, because He Himself practiced it while on earth. Of this poverty I shall say nothing more. But there is another kind of poverty, an inward poverty, through which this word of our Lord is to be understood when He says: "Blessed are the poor in spirit, or in the spirit."

<vocab>Poverty, Blessedness, Wisdom, Kingdom of Heaven, Jesus Christ, External Poverty, Inward Poverty, Poor in Spirit</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a discourse on the Beatitude "Blessed are the poor in spirit," distinguishing between external, physical poverty and a deeper, internal spiritual poverty. The author asserts that divine wisdom far surpasses any human or angelic understanding.</summary>
<keywords>poverty of spirit, external poverty, inward poverty, divine wisdom, Beatitudes, Meister Eckhart, kingdom of heaven</keywords>

### On Poverty.

Now I ask you to be such that you might understand this discourse, for I tell you by the eternal truth: if you are not equal to the truth <note>The "truth" here refers to the specific mystical realization of the Godhead that the author is about to describe.</note> of which we are now speaking, you cannot understand me. Several people have asked me what poverty is. To this, we wish to respond.

Bishop Albert <note>original: "Bischof Albrecht" — Referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), the famous Dominican scholar, scientist, and teacher of Thomas Aquinas.</note> says that he is a poor person for whom all things that God ever created do not suffice; and that is well said. But we say it even better and take poverty in a higher sense. **A poor person is one who wants nothing, knows nothing, and has nothing.** I wish to speak about these three points.

Firstly, then, he is called a poor person who **wants nothing**. Some people do not rightly understand this meaning; these are the people who cling painfully to penances <note>original: "Pönitenzien" — formal, often public acts of atonement prescribed by the church.</note> and outward acts of contrition <note>original: "Bussübungen" — physical or ritualistic practices like fasting or self-denial used to show remorse.</note>—may God have mercy that such people are held in great esteem!—and yet they recognize so little of the divine truth. These people are called holy according to outward appearances, but inside they are asses, for they do not understand how to discern the divine truth. These people say that a poor person is one who wants nothing. They interpret this to mean that a person should be such that they no longer fulfill their own will in any thing, but rather strive 

<summary>The author defines true spiritual poverty as a state of wanting, knowing, and having nothing, contrasting this internal state with superficial, outward religious practices. He critiques those who focus only on external penances without understanding deeper divine truth.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual poverty, Albertus Magnus, will, divine truth, penance, detachment, interiority</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the sermon on poverty from page 103, specifically elaborating on the first point of spiritual poverty: "wanting nothing." It clarifies the distinction between merely following God's will and achieving a mystical state of total self-emptying.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

->Meister Eckhart<-

...should <note>The text continues from the previous page, finishing the thought that these people believe their goal should be</note> follow the most beloved will of God. These people are not in a bad state, for their intention is good; therefore we should praise them; may God and His mercy preserve them. But I say in good truth that they are not truly poor people, nor are they to be equated with poor people. They are highly regarded in the eyes of people who understand nothing better. But I say that they are asses <note>Eckhart frequently used the term "asses" to describe those who, despite their piety, lacked deep mystical insight and remained stuck in literal or external religious practices.</note> who understand nothing of divine truth. With their good intentions they may perhaps attain the kingdom of heaven, but of this poverty of which I now wish to speak, they know nothing.

If someone now asks me what then a poor person is, who "wants nothing," I answer and speak thus: As long as a person has that which is in their will, and as long as it is their will to fulfill the most beloved will of God, that person does not have the poverty of which we wish to speak; for this person has a will with which they want to satisfy God's will, and that is not right. For if a person truly wants to be poor, they should be as empty of their **created will** <note>original: "geschaffenen Willens" — This refers to the individual, human will that belongs to us as created beings, as opposed to the uncreated essence of God.</note> as they were when they did not yet exist. And I tell you by the eternal truth: as long as you have the will to fulfill God's will, and long for eternity and for God, for so long you are...

<summary>Meister Eckhart argues that true spiritual poverty requires more than just following God's will; it requires a complete letting go of the individual "created will" entirely. He sharply distinguishes between conventional piety and the radical mystical state of "wanting nothing."</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, poverty of spirit, divine truth, created will, kingdom of heaven, mysticism, detachment</keywords>

# On Poverty.

...not truly poor; for a poor person is one 
who wants nothing, knows nothing, and 
desires nothing.

When I stood in my **first cause** <note>original: "ersten Ursache" — In Eckhart’s thought, this refers to the "Godhead" or the state of being before the soul was "created" into a separate existence.</note>, I 
had no God and belonged to myself; 
I wanted nothing, I desired nothing, for I 
was a mere **Being** <note>original: "blosses Sein"</note> and a knower of myself 
according to divine truth; there I wanted 
myself and wanted no other thing; what 
I wanted, that I was, and what I was, that 
I wanted, and here I stood free of God and 
of all things. But when I went out from my 
**free will** <note>This "going out" refers to the process of creation, where the soul moves from its eternal essence into a temporal, created form.</note> and received my created 
being, then I obtained a "God"; for before 
there were creatures, God was not "God"; 
He was what He was. When creatures 
came to be and began their created 
existence, then God was not "God" in 
Himself, but rather He was "God" in 
the creatures. Now we say that God, 
insofar as He is "God," is not the final 
goal of the creature, nor does He possess 
as much fullness as the least creature 
has in God. And if it were so, that a 
fly had **reason** <note>original: "Vernunft"</note> and could rationally 
seek the eternal abyss of divine being 
from which it came, we say that 
God, with all that He is as "God," 
could not satisfy the fly nor suffice 
for it. Therefore we pray that we 
may be **rid of God** <note>The sentence cuts off here; Eckhart is leading to his famous prayer: "I pray to God that He may quit me of God," meaning to move past the concept of a "creator God" into the essential Godhead.</note>...

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Meister Eckhart’s sermon on spiritual poverty. Having previously defined the first requirement of poverty as "wanting nothing," he now describes the state of the soul in eternity and introduces the second requirement: "knowing nothing."</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

...become and perceive the truth and become partakers of eternity, where the highest angels and souls exist in the same way in that state where I stood and willed what I was, and was what I willed. So shall a person be **poor of will** and will and desire as little as they willed and desired when they did not yet exist <note>Eckhart frequently refers to the state of the soul before it was "created" into time, existing only as a thought or essence within God.</note>. And in this manner, the person who wants nothing is poor.

Secondly, he is a poor person who **knows nothing**. We have sometimes said that a person should live as if they did not live for themselves, nor for the truth, nor for God. But now we say it differently and wish to say further: the person who is to possess this poverty must possess everything they were when they did not "live" in any way—neither for themselves, nor for truth, nor for God. Rather, they should be so **quit and free** <note>original: "quitt und ledig" — A legal and mystical term meaning to be entirely discharged of all attachments or obligations.</note> of all knowledge that not even the **knowing of God** is alive within them; for when the person stood in the eternal nature of God, nothing else lived in them; what lived there was they themselves. Therefore we say that a person should be as emptied of their own knowledge as they were when they were not, and let God act as He will, and stand as free as when they first came from God.

Now the question is: upon what does the **essence of the soul** <note>original: "Seelheit" — A term referring to the essential nature or "soul-ness" of the human being.</note> primarily depend? Some masters have said it depends upon **desire**. Others say it...

<summary>Eckhart explains that true spiritual poverty requires being as free of will and knowledge as the soul was before its creation, existing only in God's eternal essence. He begins to debate the philosophical foundations of the soul's nature, contrasting desire with other faculties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 106 regarding the second requirement of true spiritual poverty: "knowing nothing." Having just asked what the essence of the soul depends on, Eckhart now challenges the traditional theological views of his time.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>

# On Poverty

...depends on knowledge and on desire. But we say that it depends neither on knowledge nor on desire, but rather there is a **Something in the soul** from which knowledge and desire flow; this "Something" itself does not know and does not desire in the same way as the powers of the soul <note>Eckhart distinguishes between the "powers" (the active parts of the mind like memory, will, and intellect) and the "ground" or essence of the soul which remains still.</note>. Whoever recognizes this, recognizes that upon which the **essence of the soul** <note>original: "Seelheit" — The innermost core or "soul-ness" of a human being.</note> depends. This "Something" has neither a "before" nor an "after," and it does not wait for anything to be added, for it can neither gain nor lose. Therefore, every possibility of acting within itself is completely taken away from it; rather, it is always the same "Self" that consumes itself in the manner of God. 

Thus, I mean that a person should stand **quit and free** <note>original: "quitt und ledig" — A legal-mystical term meaning to be entirely discharged of all attachments, mental images, or obligations.</note> such that they neither know nor recognize what God is working within them; only then can a person call poverty their own. The masters say that God is **Being** <term>Wesen: In traditional medieval philosophy, "Being" (esse) was the primary definition of God. Eckhart provocatively rejects this to suggest God is beyond all human categories.</term>, and indeed a rational being, and that He knows all things. But I say: God is neither Being, nor **Reason** <term>Vernunft: The faculty of intellect or understanding.</term>, nor does He know anything—neither this nor that. Therefore, God is stripped of all things, and for that very reason, He **is** all things. 

Now, whoever wishes to be **poor in spirit** must be poor of all their own knowledge, as one who knows nothing and no thing—neither God, nor creature, nor themselves. On the contrary, it is not the case that a person should even desire to know or recognize the way of God.

<summary>Eckhart argues that true poverty of spirit requires abandoning all intellectual concepts of God, even the idea of God as "Being" or "Reason." He points to a "Something" in the soul that exists beyond knowledge and desire, where the soul is most like God.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual poverty, essence of the soul, apophatic theology, God as non-being, quit and free, poor in spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the second requirement of poverty (knowing nothing) to the third and final requirement: "having nothing." It concludes the recap of the first two stages—being free of the will and being free of knowledge—before introducing a radical shift in Eckhart's teaching regarding the soul as a "dwelling place" for God.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

# Master Eckhart.

In the manner that I have spoken, a person can be poor in their own knowledge.

Thirdly, he is a poor person who **has nothing**. Many people have said that perfection consists in having none of the physical things of this earth, and in a certain sense that is true, if one does it with [conscious] intent. But this is not the sense that I mean. I said before that he is a poor person who does not [merely] want to fulfill the will of God, but wants to live so that he is as **quit and free** <note>original: "ledig" — meaning discharged from all attachments or debts.</note> of his own will and of God’s will as he was when he did not [yet] exist. Of this poverty we say that it is the **primordial poverty**. Secondly, we say that he is a poor person who does not know the works of God within himself. Whoever stands as free of knowledge and understanding as God stands free of all things—that is the most manifest poverty. But the third poverty of which I wish to speak is the deepest: namely, that a person **has nothing**.

Now pay close attention; I have often said, and great masters <note>Eckhart likely refers to scholastic authorities like Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great.</note> say it too, that a person should be so quit of all things and all works, both inwardly and outwardly, that they could be a **dwelling-place** <term>Eigenstätte: Literally a "private place" or "proper place." In mystical theology, this usually refers to the soul being prepared as a temple or vessel for God's activity.</term> for God, in which God could work. 

But now we declare it differently. If the case is such that the person [is] free of all things...

<summary>Eckhart defines the three levels of spiritual poverty: being free of one's own will, being free of knowledge, and—most radically—having nothing at all. He begins to challenge the idea that the soul should even be a "place" for God to work.</summary>
<keywords>poverty, will of God, primordial poverty, knowledge, dwelling-place, detachment, Master Eckhart</keywords>

<page-num>17.</page-num>

## Of God and the World.

The very best thing <note>This refers to the "Summum Bonum" or the highest good in medieval theology.</note> that God ever did for humanity was that he became human <note>The Incarnation: the Christian belief that God took on human flesh in the person of Jesus.</note>. Concerning this, I wish to tell a story that truly belongs here. There was a rich man and a rich woman; then a misfortune befell the woman so that she lost an eye, at which she was very sorrowful. Then the husband <note>The original "Herr" can mean Lord, Master, or Husband; here it functions as both a literal husband in the story and an allegory for God.</note> came to her and said: “Lady, why are you so sorrowful? You should not be sorrowful because you have lost your eye.” Then she said: “My lord, I am not sorrowful because I lost my eye; I am sorrowful because it seems to me that you must now love me less.” Then he said: “Lady, I love you.” Not long after that, he struck out one of his own eyes and came to the woman and said: “Lady, so that you may now believe that I love you, I have made myself equal <term>Gleichheit: Equality or likeness. In Eckhart’s thought, union with God requires a state of "likeness" or "equality" between the soul and God.</term> to you: I now also have only one eye.”

<vocab>God, Story, Eye, Love, Equality</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a section on God's relationship with the world, using a parable of a man who blinds himself in one eye to match his wife's loss. The story serves as an allegory for the Incarnation, illustrating that God became human to prove His love and achieve "equality" with humanity.</summary>

<keywords>Incarnation, divine love, equality, allegory, Meister Eckhart, compassion</keywords>

<page-num>113</page-num>

# Of God and the World.

->8<-

The masters <note>Likely referring to scholastic authorities like Aristotle or Thomas Aquinas, whom Eckhart frequently cites to ground his mystical claims.</note> say: all creatures work toward this—that they might give birth and wish to make themselves equal to the Father. Another master says: Every acting cause acts solely for the sake of its purpose, so that it may find rest and peace in its purpose. This refers to the human person, who could hardly believe that God loved him so much, until God finally struck out one of his own eyes and assumed human nature <note>This refers back to the parable on the previous page where a husband blinds himself to show love for his one-eyed wife; here it is explicitly linked to the Incarnation.</note>. This is "become flesh" <note>A reference to John 1:14: "And the Word was made flesh."</note>.

**In the beginning** <note>original: "In principio"</note>. **A child is born to us, a son is given to us** <note>A reference to Isaiah 9:6, traditionally read in the Middle Ages as a prophecy of the birth of Christ.</note>. A master says: All creatures work according to their primary purity and their very greatest perfection. This is what God has done. He created the soul according to the highest perfection and has poured into it all his clarity in its pure primacy, and yet he has remained unmixed <note>This suggests that while God's light fills the soul, God’s essence does not lose its distinct identity or become "polluted" by the soul’s created nature.</note>.

Now pay attention! I said recently in a certain place: When God created all creatures, should he not have previously created something that was uncreated, which carried the images of all creatures within itself? That is the **Spark** <term>Funke: The "Seelenfünklein" or Spark of the Soul. In Eckhart's theology, this is the highest, uncreated part of the human spirit that remains eternally in contact with God.</term>, which is so close to God that it is a single, undivided "One," and it carries the image <note>original: "Bild"</note> of all creatures without image and beyond image within itself.

A question was discussed yesterday among great scholars. I wonder, I said, that no one can fathom the very least word...

<summary>This page explains the Incarnation as God's way of proving his love to humanity, making himself "equal" to us. It also introduces the "Spark," an uncreated element of the soul that contains the true, non-material essence of all things.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the soul's relationship to God. Following the previous page’s discussion on the Incarnation ("the Word became flesh") and the "primary purity" of the soul, Eckhart now moves into a first-person exploration of the "Eternal Birth"—the idea that the human soul is not just a creation, but is eternally begotten by God the Father.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

# Meister Eckhart.

and if you ask me whether I—if I am an only Son whom the Heavenly Father has eternally begotten—have then eternally been the Son, I answer: yes and no. Yes, a Son: in that the Father has eternally begotten me; and no, not a Son: according to the state of being unborn <term>Unborn-ness: refers to the pre-existent state of the soul within God’s essence before it is "born" into a distinct identity or creaturely form.</term>. **In the beginning** <note>original: "In principio"</note>. Here it is given to us to understand that we are an only Son, whom the Father has eternally begotten out of the hidden intellect <term>Intellect (Verstand): for Eckhart, this is the highest power of the soul, the "spark" where the human and divine meet.</term> of the eternal hiddenness, while he remained in the first beginning of the pure primacy, which is a fullness of all purity. Here I have eternally rested and slept in the hidden knowledge of the eternal Father, remaining within, unspoken. Out of pure clarity, he has eternally begotten me as his only-begotten Son, into the very image of his eternal Fatherhood, so that I might be a father and give birth to him from whom I am born <note>This is one of Eckhart’s most radical claims: that the soul is so united with God that it participates in God's own creative and generative activity.</note>. 

In the same way as if someone stood before a high mountain and cried: “Are you there?” and the sound and the echo called back: “Are you there?” Or if he said: “Come out!” and the sound answered: “Come out!” Indeed, if one were to see the wood in that light <note>To see "wood in the light" refers to perceiving the spiritual essence of a material object as it exists in the mind of God.</note>, there would arise an angel and a rational being—and not merely rational, it would become pure Reason, in that pure primacy which is a fulfillment of all purity. This is how God acts: he begets his only-begotten Son

<summary>Meister Eckhart explains the paradoxical nature of the soul as both eternally "unborn" in God and eternally "begotten" as God's Son. He uses the analogy of an echo to describe how the soul reflects God's own voice and activity back to Him.</summary>

# Of God and the World.

I thought (it was many years ago) that if I were asked why every grass spider <note>original: "Grasspinne." Then I said: it is more wonderful that all grass spiders are so much alike; and I said: just as all angels are "all-at-one" in their pure primacy <term>Primacy (Erstheit): the original, purest state of a being as it exists in the mind of God before being created in the physical world.</term>, so all grass spiders are "all-at-one" in their pure primacy, and all things are "all-at-one" <term>All-at-one (alleins): a key mystical term describing a state where all distinctions between individual things vanish, and they are experienced as a single unity in God.</term>.

I sometimes thought, while walking outdoors, that a person might in time reach a point where he can compel God. If I were up here and said to him, “Come up here!” that would be difficult. But if I said, “Sit down here!” that would be easy. God does the same. When a person humbles himself, God, in his goodness, cannot restrain himself; he must stoop <note>The text cuts off here, but the implication in Eckhart’s theology is that God’s nature is so moved by true humility that He is "forced" by His own goodness to descend into the humble soul.</note>

### Meister Eckhart.

**In the beginning** <note>original: "In principio." These are the opening words of the Gospel of John. Eckhart uses this Latin phrase to explore the concept of the divine source.</note> signifies as much as a beginning of all being. There is also an end to all being, for the first beginning exists for the sake of the final end. Indeed, God himself does not rest where he is the first beginning; rather, he rests where he is a purpose and an end and a repose for all being—not that this being would be annihilated there, but rather it is there brought to completion in its highest perfection. What is the final end? It is the hiddenness of the darkness of the eternal <term>Godhead</term> <note>In Eckhart's teaching, the "Godhead" (Gottheit) is the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God, which is even more fundamental than the Three Persons of the Trinity.</note> and is unknown, and was never known, and will never be known. God remains therein unknown to himself, and the light of the eternal Father has shone therein eternally, and the darkness does not comprehend the light. That we may come to this truth, may the truth of which we have spoken help us.
Amen.

## On the Renewal of the Spirit.

"You shall be renewed in your spirit, which is called <term>mind</term> <note>original: "mens." In medieval scholasticism, *mens* refers to the highest part of the soul, the seat of consciousness and the image of God.</note>," which means a consciousness. So speaks Saint Paul. Now [Saint] Augustine says that in the first part of the soul, which is called <term>mind</term> or consciousness, there was created along with the essence of the soul a power that the masters call a container or shrine of spiritual forms or formless images. This power makes the soul like the Father through his outflowing Godhead, from which he has poured the whole treasure of his divine essence into the Son and into the Holy Spirit with personal distinction, just as the power of memory in the soul pours out the treasure of images to the soul's other powers. Now, when the soul beholds any kind of imagery with this power—whether it be the image of an angel or its own image—it is quite deficient. If it beholds God as God is, or as he is an image, or as he is Three <note>A reference to the Trinity.</note>, it is [still] deficient. But when all

A term from mystical theology describing a reality that exists beyond or above the categories of created existence.</note>, receptive, resting in itself. Now Saint Paul says: "You shall be renewed in your spirit." <note>Ephesians 4:23.</note> Renewal affects all creatures beneath God; but no renewal affects God, for He is entirely eternity. What is eternity? Pay attention. The characteristic of eternity is that [present] existence and youth are one within it, for eternity would not be eternal if it could become "new" and was not always [the same]. 

Now I say: renewal affects the soul insofar as it is called "soul," for it is called "soul" because it gives life to the body and is a <term>form of the body</term> <note>Following the Aristotelian idea that the soul is the animating principle or "form" that gives the physical body its specific nature.</note>. God is nameless, for no one can speak or understand anything of Him. ### On the Renewal of the Spirit.

[pagan] master <note>original: "heid-nischer Meister." Likely a reference to Proclus or another Neoplatonic philosopher whose works influenced medieval mysticism.</note>: What we understand or say about the **first cause** <term>First Cause</term> <note>A philosophical term for God as the ultimate source and origin of all existence.</note>, we are more that ourselves than it is the first cause, for it is above all speaking and understanding. If I say now: "God is good," it is not true; rather, I am good, God is not good. I say even more: I am better than God, for what is good can become better; what can become better can become the very best. Now, God is not "good," therefore He cannot become better. And if He thus cannot become better, He also cannot become the very best, for these three are far from God: good, better, and best, for He is above all.

If I say further: "God is wise," it is not true; I am wiser than He. If I say further: "God is a being," it is not true; He is a **transcendent being** and a <term>super-essential nothingness</term> <note>original: "überwesende Nichtheit." This refers to the "Cloud of Unknowing" or apophatic theology, where God is described by what He is not, because He is so far beyond human categories of "being" that He appears as a "nothingness" to the mind.</note>. Therefore **Saint Augustine** says: "The most beautiful thing that man can say of God is that he can remain silent out of the fullness of wisdom." Therefore, be silent and do not prattle about God, for by prattling about Him, you lie and thus commit sin. If you wish to be without sin and perfect, then do not prattle about God. You should also understand nothing under the category of God, for God is above all understanding. A master says: "If I had a god whom I could understand, I would never take him for God—" <note>The sentence ends mid-thought and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, the author uses provocative "negative theology" to argue that God transcends all human language and categories like "goodness" or "wisdom." He suggests that true spiritual perfection is found in silent wonder rather than trying to define the divine with limited human concepts.</summary>

## Of Nature.

Our masters <note>Eckhart often refers to "masters" to cite the authority of Scholastic philosophers or Church Fathers, bridging the gap between academic theology and mystical experience.</note> say that everything that is recognized or born is an image, and they say the following: When the Father is to beget his only-begotten Son, he must beget his image remaining within himself—the image in the ground <note>The "Ground" (German: *Grund*) is a central term for Eckhart, referring to the deepest, uncreated part of the soul where it is one with God.</note>, just as it has been in him from eternity, **of his form** <note>original: "formae illius"</note>, that is, his form remaining within himself. This is a teaching of nature, and it seems quite unreasonable to me that one must point out God with parables, using this or that comparison. Nevertheless, he is neither this nor that, and the Father is not satisfied with this; instead, he withdraws into the primality, into the innermost part, into the ground and into the core of Fatherhood where he has eternally been within, in himself in Fatherhood, and where he consumes himself as Father of himself in the uniquely One. Here, every little blade of grass and wood and stone and all things are one. This is

<summary>In this opening to a treatise on nature, Meister Eckhart describes the internal life of the Trinity, explaining how God the Father begets the Son as an internal "image" and withdraws into a primal unity where all created things—even grass and stones—are one.</summary>

<header>Of Nature. 127</header>

the very best, and I have become completely enamored of it. Therefore, nature places everything it can achieve into it; it casts everything into Fatherhood, so that it may be one and a Son, and outgrow everything else and remain alone in Fatherhood; and so that, if it cannot be fully within it, it might at least be a likeness of the One. Nature, which comes from God, seeks nothing that is outside of itself; indeed, nature, as it is in itself, has nothing to do with color <note>In medieval philosophy, "color" represents an accidental property—something external rather than the internal essence of a thing.</note>, for nature, which is of God, seeks nothing other than God’s peer.

I considered tonight that only like things can act upon one another. I cannot see any thing that is not like me, and I cannot recognize any thing that is not like me. God carries all things hidden within Himself, but not distinguished as "this" or "that," but rather as one in Unity. The eye also has color within itself; the eye receives color, whereas the ear does not <note>Eckhart is referencing the medieval theory of perception where "like is known by like." The eye can only see color because it possesses the capacity for "color-ness" within its own nature.</note>. The ear receives sound, and the tongue taste. Each has that with which it is one. Accordingly, the image of the soul and God’s image share one <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Wesen." This refers to the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing, suggesting the soul and God share the same underlying reality.</note>: since we are children of God. And even if I had neither eyes nor ears, I would still possess that essence.

I have often said: the shell must break—
