break, and what is within must come out: for if you wish to have the kernel, you must break the shell. And if you therefore wish to find nature naked, then all parables <note>The German *Gleichnisse* refers to parables, analogies, or mental images. Eckhart argues that even our holy images of God are just "shells" that must be discarded to experience God directly.</note> must break, and the further one steps inside, the closer one is to the essence <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Wesen." This refers to the core being or fundamental reality of a thing.</note>.

   A few years ago I was nothing; not long after, my father and my mother ate meat and bread and herbs that grew in the garden, and from that I became a human being. My father or my mother could not bring this about; rather, God made my body directly and created my soul according to the Most High. Accordingly, I possessed my own life <note>original: "possedi me." Latin: I possessed myself.</note>. This grain aims toward the rye; yet it is in the nature of rye that it can become wheat, therefore it does not rest until it attains this very nature. This grain of wheat has it in its nature that it can become all things; therefore it goes into itself and enters into death <note>A reference to John 12:24: "Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit."</note>, so that it might become all things. And this ore is copper; it has it in its nature that it can become gold; therefore it does not rest until it attains this very nature. Indeed, this wood has it in its nature that it can become a stone; I say even more, it can indeed become all things; it dissolves into a fire and allows itself to be burned, so that it...

# On Nature.

This refers to the core reality or fundamental "is-ness" of a thing, distinct from its outward physical appearance.</note> in their primality <term>primality</term> <note>original: *Erstheit*. A term used by Eckhart to describe the original, undivided state of being as it exists in God before it becomes "this" or "that" specific thing.</note>. ## Of God and Man

Preach the word. <note>original: *Praedica verbum*. A Latin command from 2 Timothy 4:2, serving as the foundational mission for the Dominican Order (the Order of Preachers), of which Eckhart was a member.</note> One reads a little word concerning my Lord Saint Dominic, and Saint Paul wrote it, and it says in English thus: “Speak it out, speak it forth, bring it forth, and give birth to the Word.” It is a wondrous thing that a thing flows out and yet remains within. That the Word flows out and yet remains within, that is very wonderful; that all creatures <term>creatures</term> <note>original: *Kreaturen*. In this context, it refers to all created beings—humans, animals, and objects—as distinct from the Creator.</note> flow out and yet remain within, that is very wonderful; that God has given and that God has promised to give, that is very wonderful and is incomprehensible and unbelievable. And that is right; if it were comprehensible and believable, it would not be right. God is in all things. The more He is in things, the more He is out of things; the more He is within, the more He is without. I have said it often before: God is creating all this world now, completely and entirely. Everything that God ever, six thousand <note>Referring to the medieval belief that the world was roughly 6,000 years old.</note>

# Of God and Man.

...has a twofold meaning. ## Of Death.

One reads of the holy <term>martyrs</term> <note>Those who bear witness to their faith through suffering or death.</note>, whom we commemorate today, that they died by the sword. # Of Death.

**Master:** Nothing can touch heaven. This means that the human being is a heavenly human, for whom all things are so insignificant that they cannot touch him. The <term>martyrs</term> are dead; they have lost a life and have received a being. I am certain that if a soul recognized the smallest thing that has "being," it would not want to turn away from it for a single moment. # What is God?

What is God and what is the temple of God? Twenty-four masters <note>Eckhart is referencing the "Book of the Twenty-Four Philosophers" (*Liber XXIV philosophorum*), a popular medieval text that provided twenty-four diverse, metaphorical definitions of God.</note> came together and wanted to say what God was, and they could not. Afterward, they came back at a suitable time, and each of them brought his own saying with him; of these, I will now take two or three. The first spoke: "God is something compared to whom all changeable and temporal things are nothing, and everything that has an <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*. This refers to the core nature or reality of a thing.</note> is from him and is small compared to him." The second spoke: "God is something that is beyond being, and which in itself needs no one, yet all things have need of him." The third spoke: "God is an <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *Vernünftigkeit*. This denotes a divine capacity for reason, consciousness, or the act of knowing.</note> that wishes to know itself."

I will set aside the first and the third and speak of the second: that God is something that must necessarily be beyond being. Whatever possesses an essence, time, or space, that does not belong to God; he is beyond them. What he is in all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: *Kreaturen*. In this context, it refers to all "created things"—humans, animals, and the physical world—as distinct from the uncreated God.</note>...

One master says: God is something that acts eternally undivided within himself, who needs no one's help and no tool, and who remains within himself; he needs nothing, yet all things need him and strive toward him as their final end. This refers to the core nature or reality of a thing.</note>. But God acts beyond essence in the vastness where he can move; he works essence into <term>non-essence</term> <note>original: *Unwesen*. Here, Eckhart uses the term not to mean "nuisance" as in modern German, but to describe a state of being that is prior to or beyond any defined form or existence.</note>; before any essence existed, God was acting. ### What is God.

...say, it is just as wrong to call God a <term>being</term> <note>original: *Wesen*. In this context, Eckhart is arguing that God is "super-essential," existing beyond the category of "a being" among other beings.</note> as if I were to call the sun pale or black. God is neither this nor that. And a Saint says: If anyone imagines that they have known God—if they have known anything, then they have known *s o m e t h i n g*, and thus they have not known God.

Lesser masters <note>original: *Kleine Meister*. This likely refers to junior faculty in the medieval university system or scholastic theologians who focused on technical distinctions rather than mystical union.</note> read in the schools that all beings are divided in two ways, and they completely deny these ways to God. Neither of these ways touches God, yet He lacks neither of them. The first, which has the most being and in which all things take on their existence, is <term>substance</term> <note>original: *Substanz*. In medieval philosophy, this is the primary category of being that exists in itself rather than in another.</note>, and the last, which carries the least being within itself, is called <term>relation</term> <note>original: *relatio*. This refers to how one thing stands in regard to another; Eckhart is arguing that in God, even the "weakest" category of logic is as "great" as the strongest.</note>; in God, this is equal to the very greatest thing that has the most being; they share an identical image in God. In God, the images of all things are equal; but they are unequal to the images of the things themselves. The highest angel, the soul, and the gnat have an equal image in God. God is neither being nor goodness. Goodness clings to being and is no broader than being, for if there were no being, there would be no goodness, and being is even purer than goodness. In God, there is neither goodness, nor something better, nor the very best. Whoever said that God is "good" would do Him as much wrong as one who [called] the sun black...

->Gott, Wesen, Substanz, relatio, Güte, Seele, Heiliger<-

<summary>Eckhart argues for a "negative theology" where God is so far beyond human comprehension that even positive terms like "good" or "being" are inaccuracies. He suggests that within the divine mind, all creatures—from angels to gnats—exist as equal images.</summary>
<keywords>Apophatic theology, Divine Essence, Substance, Relation, Scholasticism, Equality of Images, Goodness</keywords>

# Meister Eckhart.

...would be called. Now God says: no one is good but God alone. What is good? That which shares itself with the universal. They call a person good who is useful to the community. Therefore a pagan master <note>original: *heidnischer Meister*. Eckhart often refers to Aristotle or other Greek philosophers this way.</note> says that a hermit is neither good nor evil (in this sense), because he is not useful to the community or to other people. God is the most universal. No created thing shares from what is its own, because all creatures are nothing in themselves. Whatever they share, they have received from another. They also do not give of their own selves. The sun gives its light and yet remains where it is; fire gives its heat and yet remains fire; but God shares His own because He is, in Himself, what He is; and in all the gifts He gives, He always gives Himself first. He gives Himself as God, exactly as He is, in all His gifts, insofar as it is possible for someone to receive Him.

If we take God in His <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*. Here referring to God’s existence as the ground of all things.</note>, we take Him in His <term>outer court</term> <note>original: *Vorburg*. A metaphor comparing God's being to a castle; the outer court is the part accessible to others, while the inner sanctum remains hidden.</note>; for essence is His outer court, where He dwells. Where is He then in His temple? This is the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *Vernünftigkeit*. In Eckhart’s thought, the "intellect" or "reason" is a higher faculty than "being" because it is where the soul most closely mirrors God.</note>, where He shines forth holily, as the other master said: that God is an intellect who lives in His knowledge alone and remains in Himself alone,

<note>Meister Eckhart, God, Essence, Outer Court, Intellect, Knowledge</note>

<summary>Eckhart defines God's goodness as His total self-sharing and distinguishes between God's "essence" (His outer presence) and His "intellect" (His true inner temple).</summary>

## On Personal <term>Essence</term><note>original: *Wesen*. This term can mean "being," "essence," or "nature." In this context, it refers to the core reality of a person in relation to God.</note>.

God is love, and whoever dwells in love dwells in God and God in him <note>A direct reference to the New Testament, 1 John 4:16.</note>. God dwells in the soul with everything that He is, and so does every creature. Therefore: where the soul is, there is God, for the soul is in God. For this reason, the soul is also where God is, unless the Scriptures lie. Where my soul is, there is God, and where God is, there too is my soul; and this is as true as God is God.

Not only by nature, but <term>above nature</term> <note>original: *über Natur*. This refers to the "supernatural" state of grace where the soul exceeds its natural human limitations to participate in divine life.</note>, my soul rejoices in all the joy and all the <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*. Often translated as beatitude, referring to the ultimate state of happiness found in the presence of God.</note> that God Himself enjoys in His divine nature, whether it be pleasing or painful to God, for of these there is only one, and where one is, there is all, and where all is, there is one. This is a certain truth. Where the soul is, there is God, and where God is, there is the soul. And if I were to say that it were not so, I would be speaking falsely.

# On Personal <term>Essence</term><note>original: *Wesen*. As noted previously, this refers to the core reality or "being" of a thing. Here, it describes the soul's ultimate state where it is indistinguishable from God's own being.</note>.

Truly, now pay attention to a small word, which I hold very dear; for I reflect on how one He is with me, as if He had forgotten all other creatures and were nothing more than I alone. Now, pray for those who have been recommended to me! Those who pray for a "part" of God, or for God <note>That is, praying for God as if He were an object or a merchant who gives gifts, rather than seeking union with Him.</note>, they pray wrongly; if I pray for nothing, then I pray rightly, and the prayer is right and is powerful. Whoever prays for anything else worships an <term>idol</term><note>original: *Abgott*. Literally "off-god." The author suggests that treating God as a means to an end—like asking for favors—turns God into a false idol of our own desires.</note>, and one could say it was pure heresy. I never pray so well as when I pray for nothing and for no one, neither for Henry nor for Conrad <note>Heinrich and Konrad were common names used in medieval German texts as placeholders, similar to "John Doe" or "any generic person."</note>.

The true worshippers worship God in truth and in the spirit [namely, in the Holy Spirit]; what God is in power, that we are in the <term>image</term><note>original: *Bilde*. Based on the Genesis concept that humans are made in the "image of God," the author argues that in our deepest core, we reflect God's own nature.</note>. There we know as we are known, and love as we are loved. This is also without <term>work</term><note>original: *Werk*. This refers to outward actions or the distinct functions of the mind. In the highest state of mystical union, "working" ceases because the soul simply "is."</note>, for the soul is there one with the image and acts in power as power; she is also one with the [Divine] Persons and exists in the power of the Father, and in the wisdom of the Son, and in the goodness of the Holy Spirit. This is all still "work" within the Persons; the essence above that, however, is without work. Rather, there everything is one—essence and work—where the soul is in God. Indeed, where the Persons reach into the essence, there work and essence are one; there she loves the Persons, insofar as they—

This is the highest perfection of the spirit that one can attain in this life according to the nature of the spirit. # On the Personal Essence.

...this personal essence, the God-man, grows far beyond and soars above the outer man, so far that the outer man can no longer follow it. If a person remains standing within themselves, they may well receive the influence of grace from the personal essence in many ways—sweetness, comfort, and inwardness—and that is good, but it is not the highest state. If he thus remains within himself in the <term>shelter</term><note>original: *Unterkunft*. This implies a self-made dwelling or a state of being "at home" in one's own ego or created nature, rather than in God.</note> of his own self, he indeed receives comfort from grace and through the working of grace, but that is not what is best for him. For then the inner man would have to bend outward, according to his spiritual nature, from that <term>ground</term><note>original: *Grund*. A central term in German mysticism referring to the deepest, uncreated part of the human soul where it meets God.</note> in which he is one, and he would have to turn toward the grace-filled essence from which he receives grace.

Therefore, the spirit can never become perfect in this way. Body and soul are only perfected when the inner man, in the manner of the spirit, escapes his own essence toward that place where he is fundamentally *one* ground; and likewise, the outer man must also be deprived of his own shelter and merge entirely into the eternal personal essence, which is one and the same personal essence. Now, there are two essences here. One essence in the Godhead is the bare <term>substantial essence</term><note>original: *substanzliche Wesen*. This refers to God's pure, undifferentiated being.</note>; the other is the personal essence, and yet it is an <term>under-ground</term><note>original: *Untergrund*. A foundational layer or substratum. Here, it suggests that Christ’s personhood serves as the support or base for all reality.</note>; for the same foundation—the personality of Christ—is also the foundation...

## On the Stages of the Soul.

Whoever desires to reach their highest level and the contemplation of the supreme Good—which is God himself—must possess a knowledge of themselves <note>original: "Kennen seiner selbst"; self-knowledge was considered the first step toward divine union in medieval mysticism.</note> and of the things that are above them, ascending to the highest point; in this way, one arrives at the highest, purest knowledge. Therefore, dear soul <note>original: "lieber Mensch" (dear human/person). The author uses this to address the reader directly.</note>, learn to know yourself, for that is better for you than if you understood the power of all created things. To understand how you should know yourself, observe two ways.

First, you must observe whether your outer senses <term>outer senses: the five physical faculties of sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch</term> are well-ordered in their proper place. See, now take note of the condition of our outer senses. The eyes are always just as ready to see evil as they are to see good. Likewise, the ears are ready to hear the good, and the other senses can perceive in the same manner. Therefore, you should compel yourself toward good things with zeal and great earnestness. So much for the outer senses.

Now hear of the inner senses <note>The "inner senses" traditionally included faculties like imagination, memory, and common sense, which process information from the physical world for the soul.</note>, which

...are the faculties of a higher level that exist within the soul, categorized as the lower and the higher. Now, learn about the lower faculties. These serve as intermediaries between the higher faculties and the outer senses. Because they are situated so close to the outer senses, whatever the eye sees and the ear hears, they immediately transmit to the desire <term>desire: here referring to the "appetitive" faculty (Begehren), the part of the soul that reacts to and wants things perceived by the senses</term>. If the matter is well-ordered <note>i.e., if the sensory input is virtuous or correctly processed</note>, the desire then passes it to a second faculty called contemplation <note>original: "Anschauung"; this refers to the soul’s ability to look at and process an internal image.</note>. This faculty gazes upon it and carries it further to a third, which is called rationality. In this manner, the perception becomes increasingly purified before it reaches the higher faculties. The power of the soul stands at such a high level that it eventually perceives the object without any likeness or image <note>A central theme in Eckhart’s mysticism: to reach God, the soul must eventually strip away all "images" or mental pictures of worldly things.</note> and carries it up into the highest faculties. There, it is preserved in the memory, understood in the intellect, and fulfilled in the will. These are the higher faculties of the soul, and they exist as one in nature. Whatever the soul performs, this simple nature also performs through these faculties.

Now, take note of how the soul reaches its highest stage and its greatest perfection. A master says: God is carried and transposed into the soul. From this, a divine spring of love <note>original: "Liebesquell"</note> arises within the soul, which carries the soul back to God. Behold, you shall learn how this occurs. A saint says: Everything one can say about God

# On the Stages of the Soul.

...can be spoken of, that is not God. And another saint says: Everything that one can speak of God, that is God. And finally, a great master says that they both speak the truth <note>This refers to the tension between "Apophatic" theology (saying what God is not) and "Kataphatic" theology (saying what God is). Eckhart suggests that both perspectives are true in the light of divine mystery.</note>. Just as these three saints speak, so I say the following: When the soul, through its intellect <term>intellect: the faculty of understanding (Verstand), which Eckhart sees as a bridge to divine reason</term>, understands something of the divine intellect, it is then immediately handed over to the will. Then the will takes it into itself and becomes one with it, and only then does the will bring and transpose it into the memory. In this way, God is carried and moved into the soul. Truly, now hear of the divine fountain of love. It overflows within the soul, so that the highest powers <note>The spiritual faculties: intellect, will, and memory</note> pour into the lower ones, and these in turn pour into the outer person <note>The physical, sensory self</note> and raise him out of all lowliness, so that he desires to perform nothing but spiritual works. Just as the spirit acts according to divine works, so the outer person must act according to the spirit.

O wonder of wonders, when I think of the union that the soul has with God! He makes the soul joyfully blissful, flowing out of itself, for all "named" things <note>Defined, worldly things that can be grasped by language</note> are not enough for it. And since the soul itself has a "named" nature, it is not even enough for itself. The divine fountain of love flows upon the soul and draws it out of itself into the unnamed essence in...

...their first origin, which is God alone. Although the creature <note>In mystical writing, "the creature" refers to any created being—human, animal, or angelic—as distinct from the Creator.</note> has given Him names, He is nevertheless in Himself an unnamed essence. Thus the soul comes into its highest perfection.

Truly, friends of the heart, now hear further regarding the stages of the soul. Saint Augustine says: exactly as it is with God, so it is also with the soul. See how it is formed after the image of the Holy Trinity; you shall learn this through the interpretation of God.

God is threefold in persons and is simple <term>simple: in medieval theology, "simplicity" refers to being undivided and without parts, a state of absolute unity.</term> in nature. God is also in all places and in each place God is present at once. This means as much as if all places were one place of God. So it is also with the soul. God has providence <term>providence: the protective care and foresight of God over the universe.</term> over all things and forms all things in His providence. All this is natural to God. So it is also with the soul. It is also threefold in powers <note>Referencing the traditional three faculties of the soul: memory, intellect, and will.</note> and simple in nature. The soul is also in all limbs and in each limb it is present at once. Therefore, all limbs are one place of the soul. It also has providence and forms the things that are possible for it. Of everything that one can say of God, the soul has some likeness.

Now I will speak of a pure knowledge of God. I have you in mind, brother and sister <note>Eckhart frequently addressed audiences of both monks and nuns, particularly within the Dominican Order.</note>, because you are God’s very best friends.

### On the Stages of the Soul.

...and [is] most intimate to him of all those listening here. The flowing within the Godhead <term>Godhead: in mystical theology, the "Godhead" (Gottheit) refers to the absolute, essence of God which is the source of the three Persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</term> is a unity of the three persons without distinction. In this same flow, the Father flows into the Son, and the Son flows back into the Father, and they both flow into the Holy Spirit, and the Holy Spirit flows back into them both. Therefore, the Father speaks His Son and speaks Himself in His Son to all creatures; everything is within this flowing. Where the Father turns back into Himself, there He speaks Himself within Himself. In this manner, the flow has flowed back into itself, as Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite (5th-6th century), a theologian who wrote extensively about the "mystical union" with God.</note> says. Therefore, this flowing within the Godhead is a speaking without words and without sound, a hearing without ears, a seeing without eyes. Thus each person speaks Himself in the other without words in the flow. Therefore, it is a flow without flowing <note>original: "Fluss ohne Fliessen." This paradox describes a state of eternal, dynamic movement that is simultaneously at rest.</note>. 

Receive here a parable of the noble soul: it has something within itself that is especially like this flow. Where the highest powers <note>Referencing the traditional "faculties" of the soul: memory, intellect, and will.</note> and nature possess *one* single property, there each flows into the other and speaks without word or sound. Blessed be the soul that comes to the contemplation of the eternal light.

Now one might say: “This is all beautiful and well spoken. Friend of the heart, how does it happen now that I [attain] to the stage—

The text concludes by beginning a practical inquiry into how a person can reach this high level of spiritual contemplation.</summary>

# Meister Eckhart.

...long, about which you have written? Behold, you must know: God is what He is, and what He is, is mine, and what is mine, I love, and what I love, loves me and draws me to itself, and what has drawn me, to that I belong more than to myself. Behold, therefore love God, then you will become God with God. I will say no more of this.

Those who have renounced themselves and follow God in true nakedness <term>nakedness: (Entblösstheit) in Eckhart's teaching, this refers to a state of total spiritual stripping or detachment from worldly desires and the self, leaving the soul "bare" before God.</term>, how could God fail them? He must indeed pour His grace into the soul that has so annihilated <note>original: "vernichtet." In medieval mysticism, this "annihilation" is not the destruction of the soul’s existence, but the total surrender of the ego and self-will to make room for the Divine.</note> itself in love. He pours His grace into it and fills it and gives Himself to it in grace.

There God adorns the soul with Himself, just as gold is adorned with precious stones. Then He brings the soul into the contemplation of His Godhead. This happens in eternity and not in time. Yet the soul has a foretaste in time, because this holy life has been spoken of here. This has happened so that you may know that no one can reach the highest stage of knowledge and life without pursuing voluntary poverty and being like the poor. This is the very best for all people. Now we praise God for the sake of His eternal goodness,

<summary>This page concludes a sermon on the soul's union with God, explaining that through the "annihilation" of the self and the pursuit of spiritual poverty, the soul is filled with divine grace and brought into the eternal contemplation of the Godhead.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, grace, soul, detachment, Godhead, spiritual poverty, annihilation of the self, contemplation</keywords>

# Of the Stages of the Soul.

...and ask Him that He may finally receive us into His presence. May the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit help us to this end <note>The "end" refers to the state of mystical union and spiritual "nakedness" described in the preceding passages, where the soul is finally stripped of self-will to be filled by God.</note>. Amen.

## Dialogue between Sister Catherine and the Father Confessor.

The father confessor <note>Historically, this refers to a priest acting as a spiritual director for a nun or "beguine" (a lay religious woman).</note> often visits his daughter <note>In this context, "daughter" refers to his spiritual ward, Sister Catherine.</note> and asks: "Tell me, how are you now?" — She says: "I am faring poorly; heaven and earth are too narrow for me." — He begs her to tell him something. She says: "I do not know anything so clear that I could speak of it." — He says: "Do it for the sake of God; tell me one word." — With much pleading, he wins a small word from her. Then she spoke to him so wonderfully and such deep sayings concerning the **naked discovery** <note>The term "naked" (*nackt*) in mystical writing refers to a state stripped of all mental images, concepts, and self-will, allowing for a direct encounter with God.</note> of divine truth, that he says: "Do you know, this is unknown to all people; and had I not been such a great scholar that I had found it myself in the **science of God** <term>Gotteswissenschaft: The formal, academic study of theology as practiced in medieval universities.</term>, it would be unknown to me as well." — She says: "I grant you that poorly; I wish you had found it through living <note>Catherine is contrasting the "book learning" of the scholar with the "lived experience" of the mystic. She suggests that true spiritual knowledge comes from one's way of life, not just study.</note>." — He says: "You should know that

But it is not long before she comes back to the gate, asks for her worthy father confessor, and says: "Lord, rejoice with me: I have become God." <note>This "becoming God" refers to the mystical concept of *theosis* or deification—the total union of the soul with the Divine, where the individual self is said to be "transformed" into God.</note> — He says: "God be praised! The confessor said: "I do not believe she is dead." — Know that if the confessor had not been there, they would have buried her. They tried everything they knew, but they could not determine whether the soul was still in the body. They said: "She is certainly dead." — The confessor said: "No, certainly not." — On the third day, the daughter came to herself again. She said: "Alas,"

### Meister Eckhart.

"Poor me, am I back here again?" — The father confessor was there at once and spoke to her, saying: "Let me enjoy the divine word and make known to me what you have found." — She spoke: "God knows well, I cannot. What I have found, no one can put into words." — He said: "Do you have everything you want now?" — She said: "Yes, I am confirmed." <note>The German *bewähret* suggests she has been spiritually tested and has now attained a state of certainty or completion.</note> — He said: "Know that I love to hear this speech, dear daughter, speak further." — She said: "Where I stand, no <term>creature</term> <note>original: *Kreatur*. In mystical thought, this refers to any created being or thing, which must be "transcended" to reach the uncreated God.</note> can reach in a creaturely way." — He said: "Explain it to me better." — She said: "I am there where I was before I was created; there is purely God and God. There are neither angels nor saints, nor choirs, nor heavens. Some people speak of eight heavens and of nine <term>choirs</term> <note>Referring to the traditional celestial hierarchy of angels (such as Seraphim and Cherubim) described by Dionysius the Areopagite.</note>; there is nothing of that where I am. You should know that everything that is expressed in words and presented to people with images is nothing but a means to lure them toward God. Know that in God there is nothing but God; know that no soul can enter into God until it becomes as much God as it was God before it was created." <note>This reflects a core theme in Eckhart’s philosophy: the "spark of the soul" is uncreated and eternal, and salvation is the return to this original state of being within God.</note> — He said: "Dear daughter, you speak truly. Now, for God’s sake, do so and give me your next counsel: how can I come to possess this good?" — She said: "I give you faithful counsel. You well know,"

She explains that the soul must return to its uncreated state—becoming "as much God" as it was before creation—to truly enter the Divine.</summary>

...that all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: *Kreaturen*. In this mystical tradition, this refers to all created things, which are distinct from the uncreated God.</note> are created from Nothing and must become Nothing again before they return to their origin. — He said: "That is true." — She said: "Then enough has been said to you. Examine this: what is Nothing?" — He said: "I know what Nothing is, and I know well what is less than Nothing. You should understand it like this: all transitory things are nothing before God. Therefore, whoever occupies themselves with the transitory is less than Nothing." — "Why?" — "Because he is the servant of the transitory. Nothing is Nothing. Whoever serves Nothing is less than Nothing." — She said: "That is true. Direct yourselves accordingly if you wish to come to your Good <note>Here "your Good" refers to the ultimate spiritual goal or the divine essence.</note>, and you should <term>annihilate</term> <note>original: *vernichten*. A Master <note>Likely referring to Meister Eckhart himself, to whom this text is traditionally attributed.</note> says: 'Whoever loves God as his God and worships God as his God and is satisfied with that—to me, that is an unbelieving person.'" — She said: "Blessed be the Master who ever spoke this; he recognized the truth. You should know that whoever is satisfied with what can be put into words—'God' is a word, 'Kingdom of Heaven' is a word—whoever does not wish to go further with the powers of the soul, with knowledge and with love, than has ever been captured in words, should rightfully be called an unbeliever."

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a confessor and a woman identified in the tradition as "Sister Catherine." Following the previous page’s discussion on "Nothingness," Catherine now explains how the soul transcends language and its own faculties to return to its divine source.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

What one puts into words is grasped by the lowest senses or <term>powers of the soul</term> <note>original: *Kräfte der Seele*. Medieval psychology divided the soul into "lower" powers (like memory and desire) and "higher" powers (like intellect and will). Here, Eckhart suggests even the higher powers must eventually be left behind.</note>. The highest powers of the soul do not content themselves with this: they press ever further forward until they reach the **Origin**, from which the soul flowed. But you should know that the *power* of the soul cannot enter the Origin. When the soul stands before the Origin in its majesty above all created things, all powers remain outside. 

You should understand it this way: the soul is naked and stripped of all things that bear a name; thus it stands "one in one," so that it moves forward into the bare **Godhead** <note>original: *Gottheit*. In Eckhart’s thought, the "Godhead" is the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God, which is deeper and more mysterious than the "Person" of God the Father, Son, or Spirit.</note>, like oil on a cloth that keeps spreading: so the soul runs further and flows ever forward, for as long as God has ordained that it must give essence to the body in time. Know that as long as a good person lives on earth, so long does their soul have progress in eternity. This is why good people love life. As the good ascend, so the wicked, who are in error, descend.

— Truly, dear daughter, now explain to me: one speaks of **Hell** and of **Purgatory** and of the **Kingdom of Heaven**, and we read much about them. But we also read that God is in all things and all things are in God.

— She said: "I will gladly tell you that, as far as I can put it into words. Hell is nothing..."

<summary>This page describes the soul's mystical ascent beyond language and its own faculties toward its "Origin" in the Godhead, using the metaphor of oil spreading on cloth. It begins a dialogue regarding the nature of Hell, Purgatory, and Heaven in relation to God's omnipresence.</summary>

<keywords>Soul, Origin, Godhead, Eternity, Hell, Purgatory, Kingdom of Heaven, Meister Eckhart, Sister Catherine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the confessor and "Sister Catherine" (Schwester Kathrei). Having discussed the soul's return to the Godhead on the previous page, Catherine now addresses the continuity of the soul's essence between this life and the next, challenging traditional views on Purgatory and Judgment.</meta>

## Sister Catherine and the Confessor.

...as one **essence** <term>Wesen: a central term in German mysticism meaning "being," "essence," or "inner nature." It suggests the core reality of a person that exists beyond their physical form.</term>. Whatever a person's essence is here, that remains their essence for eternity, just as they are found within it. A great many people believe they possess a creaturely essence here and shall possess a divine essence there. This cannot be. Know that many people are deceived in this regard. **Purgatory** is a temporary thing, like a penance; it comes to an end. People speak of the **Last Day**, saying that God shall then pass judgment. That is true. However, it is not as people imagine. Every human being judges themselves: as they appear there in their essence, so shall they remain eternally.

The daughter continued to speak, and in her speech she came upon the subject of God. She spoke so much of God that the confessor could only say: "Dear daughter, speak on." The daughter told him so much of the greatness of God, His power, and His **Providence** <note>original: *Vorsehung*. The theological concept of God's divine guidance and care for the universe.</note> that he lost all of his outward senses. He had to be carried into a quiet cell, where he lay for a long time before he came to himself again.

When he had recovered, he felt a great longing for the daughter to come to him. The daughter came to the confessor and said: "How is it with you now?"

He said: "Heartily well. Praised be God that He ever created you as a human being! You have shown me the way to my eternal **bliss** <note>original: *Seligkeit*. This refers to the state of perfect happiness or salvation in the presence of God.</note>."

# Meister Eckhart.

...shown; I have come to the **vision of God** <note>original: *Anschauung Gottes*. This refers to the "beatific vision," or the direct, intuitive perception of God's essence.</note>, and a true knowledge of all that I have heard from your mouth has been given to me. Truly, dear daughter, remember the love that you have from God, and help me with words and with works, so that I may attain a permanence where I now am. — She said: Know that this cannot be. You do not have the right nature for it. When your soul and your **powers** <term>Kräfte: The "faculties" of the soul, such as the intellect, will, and memory, which were thought to interact with the world and the divine.</term> walk up and down the path in the accustomed way—just as a retinue at a court comes and goes—and when you understand how to distinguish the heavenly retinue and everything God ever created as well as a man knows his own followers, then you shall examine the difference between **God** and the **Godhead** <note>A central theme in Eckhart’s circle: the distinction between "God" (the active, Trinity-based Creator) and the "Godhead" (the silent, undifferentiated, absolute essence from which all things flow).</note>. Only then shall you strive to be proven worthy. You should not go astray; you should seek recreation among the **creatures** <note>original: *Kreaturen*. Meaning all created things in the physical world.</note>, so that you take no harm from them and they also suffer no harm from you. By this, you shall lift up your powers, so that you do not fall into **frenzy** <note>original: *Raserei*. A state of religious mania or uncontrolled ecstasy that was considered a spiritual danger for those not yet fully grounded.</note>. You should do this as often as it takes until the powers of the soul are stirred, until you arrive at the knowledge of which we spoke before. —

->Praised and honored be the sweet name of our Lord Jesus Christ. Amen.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the narrative of "Sister Catherine" to a specific treatise titled "On Detachment," traditionally attributed to Meister Eckhart. While the previous page focused on Catherine's mystical ascent and the distinction between God and the Godhead, this page begins a formal inquiry into the "highest virtue" that allows the soul to return to its original state before creation.</meta>

# On Detachment.

I have read many writings by pagan masters <note>Likely referring to Greek and Roman philosophers like Aristotle, Plato, or Seneca, whose works influenced medieval mysticism.</note> and by prophets, and from the Old and New Testaments, and I have searched with earnestness and all diligence for what might be the best and highest virtue, through which man could most directly join himself to God, and through which man might be exactly like the image he was in God, in which there was no distinction between him and God before God created the **creatures** <note>original: *Kreaturen*. In this context, "creatures" refers to all created things, including people, animals, and objects, which are distinct from the uncreated Divine.</note>. And when I search through all the writings, as far as my reason is able to fathom and recognize, I find nothing else but pure **detachment** <term>Abgeschiedenheit: A central concept in German mysticism. It means "being away" or "separated" from the world; a state of internal stillness and release from all worldly desires and self-will.</term>, which is divested of all creatures. Therefore our Lord said to Martha: "one thing is necessary" <note>original Latin: *unum est necessarium*. A reference to the Gospel of Luke 10:42, often interpreted by mystics as the need for a single-minded focus on the Divine over worldly activity.</note>, which is as much as to say: whoever wishes to be untroubled and pure must have one thing, and that is detachment.

The teachers praise love very greatly, as

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on the virtue of "Detachment" (Abgeschiedenheit), arguing that it is the highest path to unity with God, surpassing even the wisdom of the prophets and philosophers.</summary>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the treatise "On Detachment" attributed to Meister Eckhart. The previous page ended with the statement that teachers highly praise the virtue of love; this page begins by providing an example of that praise via Saint Paul before arguing why detachment is, in Eckhart's view, a superior virtue.</meta>

### Meister Eckhart.

for example Saint Paul with the words: "Whatever I may practice, if I have not love, I have nothing at all." <note>A paraphrase of 1 Corinthians 13:3: "And though I bestow all my goods to feed the poor, and though I give my body to be burned, and have not charity [love], it profiteth me nothing."</note> I, however, praise **detachment** more than all love. First, because the good thing about love is that it compels me to love God. Now, it is much more valuable that I compel God to come to me than that I compel myself to go to God. And this is because my eternal **blessedness** <term>Seligkeit: The state of ultimate spiritual joy and union with the Divine; often translated as "salvation" or "beatitude."</term> depends on me and God being united; for God can adapt Himself to me and unite Himself with me more fittingly than I can with Him. That detachment compels God to come to me, I prove thus: every thing loves to be in its own natural, proper place. Now, God's natural, proper place is simplicity and purity; these come from detachment. Therefore, God must necessarily give Himself to a detached heart. — Secondly, I praise detachment more than love because love compels me to take everything upon myself for God's sake, whereas detachment compels me to be receptive to nothing but God. Now, it stands much higher to be receptive to nothing but God than to bear everything for God's sake. For in suffering, a person still has a regard for the **creature** <note>original: *Kreatur*. In medieval theology, this refers to any created thing (people, objects, events) that is not God. Eckhart argues that even suffering "for God" involves focusing on the thing causing the pain.</note> from which they must suffer. Detach-

<summary>Eckhart argues that detachment is superior to love because it draws God into the soul through purity and simplicity, whereas love merely draws the soul toward God. He suggests that being purely receptive to the Divine is a higher state than actively enduring suffering for God's sake.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Meister Eckhart’s argument for the superiority of detachment over other virtues. It completes the comparison with love and begins a comparison with humility, focusing on the concept of "nothingness" as the meeting point between the soul and God.</meta>

<header>On Detachment.</header>

...ment, on the other hand, is rid of every **creature** <note>original: *Kreatur*. In medieval theology, this refers to any created thing (people, objects, events) that is not God.</note>. That **detachment** is receptive to nothing but God, I prove thus: for whatever is to be received must be received somewhere. Now detachment is so close to **nothingness** that no thing is so delicate <note>original: *zierlich*. In this context, it implies a spiritual refinement or subtleness so pure that it borders on the non-material.</note> that it can be contained in detachment except for God alone. He is so simple and delicate that He may well dwell in the detached heart.

The masters also praise **humility** <term>Demut: A core medieval virtue involving the recognition of one's lowliness before God.</term> above many other virtues. I praise detachment above all humility, and for this reason: Humility can exist without detachment, but there can be no perfect detachment without perfect humility. For perfect humility aims at the **annihilation** <term>Vernichten: Literally "making into nothing"; the spiritual process of stripping away the ego to allow God to act through the soul.</term> of oneself; now detachment touches so closely upon **nothingness** <note>original: *Nichts*. In Eckhart’s theology, "Nothingness" represents a state free from the limitations of created things, which is where the soul meets the "no-thing-ness" of God.</note> that between it and nothingness, no thing can exist. Therefore, there can be no perfect detachment without humility, and two virtues are always better than one. The second reason why I prefer detachment to humility is that perfect humility bows itself beneath all creatures, and in doing so, the person goes out of himself toward the creatures. But detachment remains within itself. Now

<summary>Eckhart argues that detachment is superior to humility because it brings the soul closer to "nothingness," a state of purity where only God can dwell. He explains that while humility bows toward created things, detachment keeps the soul centered within itself and God.</summary>

<keywords>detachment, humility, nothingness, annihilation, Meister Eckhart, divinity, soul, creatures</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison of detachment (Abgeschiedenheit) with other virtues. Having previously compared it to love and humility, Eckhart now argues why detachment is superior even to "going-out" toward others or feeling compassion, concluding with a reference to the philosopher Avicenna.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

...but no "going-out" <note>original: *Hinausgehen*. Eckhart refers to the soul’s attention moving away from its center toward the external world.</note> can ever stand as high as remaining within oneself. **Perfect detachment** pays heed to nothing and inclines neither below nor above any **creature** <note>original: *Kreatur*. Any created being or thing, as distinct from the Creator.</note>; it wishes to be neither below nor above; it wishes to persist of its own accord, for the love of no one and the sorrow of no one, and wants neither equality nor inequality, nor to have this or that in common with any creature; it wants nothing other than to be alone. Therefore, it is never troubled by anything.

I also prefer **detachment** to all **compassion** <note>original: *Mitleid*. Literally "suffering-with."</note>, for compassion is nothing other than a person going out from themselves toward the infirmities <note>original: *Gebresten*. Weaknesses, illnesses, or spiritual failings.</note> of their fellow man and letting their heart be troubled by it. Detachment stands free of this and remains within itself and allows itself to be troubled by nothing. In short: when I consider all virtues, I find none so entirely without fault and so leading to God as detachment.

A master named **Avicenna** <term>Avicenna: The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose philosophical works deeply influenced medieval Christian scholasticism.</term> says: "The rank of the spirit that is detached is so high that everything it beholds is true, and whatever it desires is granted to it, and wherever it commands, it must be obeyed." And you should know this in truth: when the free spirit stands in true detachment, he compels...

<summary>Eckhart argues that detachment is superior to compassion because it keeps the soul centered and untroubled, rather than "going out" to dwell on human suffering. He cites Avicenna to demonstrate the immense spiritual power and authority of a truly detached mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the superiority of detachment (Abgeschiedenheit) as a path to the divine. Following the previous page's citation of Avicenna regarding the power of a detached spirit, Eckhart now explains how this state brings the soul into direct union with the very essence of God.</meta>

<header># On Detachment.</header>

...bring God to His essence; and if he <note>The human spirit in a state of detachment.</note> could be formless and without any specific state, he would take on God’s own property. But God can give this to no one but Himself; therefore, God can do nothing more for the **detached spirit** than to give Himself to it. And the person who stands in such complete **detachment** is so rapt into **eternity** that no transitory thing can move them, so that they feel nothing bodily and are said to be dead to the world, for they feel and taste nothing that is earthly. This is what **Saint Paul** meant when he spoke: "I live, and yet I do not live; Christ lives in me." <note>original: "Ich lebe und lebe doch nicht, Christus lebt in mir." A translation of Galatians 2:20.</note>

Now you might ask what detachment is, if it is so noble in itself? Now you shall learn that true detachment is nothing other than the spirit remaining as immovable against all circumstances—be it joy or sorrow, honor, shame, or disgrace—as a broad mountain against a light breeze. This immovable detachment brings a person into the greatest **likeness** with God. For that God is God, He owes to His immovable detachment, and from this He has His **purity**, His **simplicity**, and His **immutability** <term>immutability: The quality of being unchanging and eternal; a traditional attribute of God in medieval philosophy.</term>. Therefore, if a person wishes to become like God, as far as a **creature** <note>original: Kreatur. Referring to any created being as distinct from the Creator.</note> can have likeness with God, he must...

<summary>Eckhart defines detachment as an immovable state of spirit that mirrors God’s own nature of simplicity and constancy, citing Saint Paul to illustrate the death of the worldly self.</summary>
<keywords>detachment, essence of God, Saint Paul, immutability, divine likeness, simplicity, spiritual transformation</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the necessity of the person becoming like God through detachment. It explores the absolute immutability of the Divine essence.</meta>

### Meister Eckhart.

...be detached. And you should know: to be empty of all **creatures** <note>original: Kreaturen. In medieval theology, this refers to everything created by God, including humans, animals, and objects, as opposed to the Creator Himself.</note> is to be full of God, and to be full of all creatures is to be empty of God. You should further know that God has stood and still stands in this immovable **detachment** from before the world began; and you should know that when God created heaven and earth and all creatures, it affected His immovable detachment as little as if He had never created any creatures. 

I say even more: by all the prayers and good works that a person can perform in **time** <note>Referring to the earthly realm of change and history, contrasted with God's "eternity."</note>, God's detachment is moved as little as if no prayer or good work ever happened in time, and God becomes as little more gracious or inclined toward the person as if the prayer or the good works had not taken place. 

I say even more: when the Son in the **Godhead** <term>Godhead: (Gottheit) The essential, transcendent nature of God, which Eckhart often describes as being beyond the active roles of the Trinity.</term> willed to become human and did so, and suffered his **martyrdom**, it affected the immovable detachment of God as little as if He had never become human. 

Now you might say: "So I hear that all prayer and all good works are lost if God does not take notice of them, and that no one can move Him with them; and yet it is said that God wants to be petitioned for all things." Here you should pay close attention to me and understand me correctly (if it...

<summary>Eckhart argues that God’s essential detachment is absolute and unaffected by creation, human efforts, or the Incarnation, maintaining a state of perfect immutability. He begins to address the theological problem of why humans should pray if God cannot be moved or changed by their actions.</summary>

->Meister Eckhart.<-

came to pass. And when it happens that what He saw in eternity manifests in time, people imagine that God has taken on a new love; and when He is angry or does something good, it is we who are changed, while He remains unchangeable—just as the sunshine hurts diseased eyes and benefits healthy ones, yet remains in itself the same unchangeable radiance. 

God does not see time, and in His seeing, no renewal occurs. In this sense, Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar whose work *Sententiae* (referred to here as *The Supreme Good*) was a foundational text for medieval theology.</note> also speaks in the book *Of the Supreme Good*: "Many people ask what God did before He created heaven and earth, or from where the new will came into God that He should create **creatures**?" and he answered the following: "No new will ever arose in God, for although it is true that the creature did not exist for itself as it does now, it was nevertheless pre-worldly in God and His reason." 

God did not create heaven and earth in the transitory way we describe their coming into being; rather, all **creatures** <note>original: Kreaturen. This refers to all created things—human, animal, or inanimate—which exist as ideas in the divine mind before their physical creation.</note> are spoken in the **Eternal Word** <note>original: ewigen Worte. A reference to the "Logos" (John 1:1), the divine intellect through which the universe was brought into existence.</note>.

And yet much is said of her lamentation: how can all this be reconciled with...

<summary>Eckhart explains that while God appears to change in response to us, He remains immutable; all of creation existed eternally in His mind before time began. He concludes by raising the question of how Christ and Mary could experience deep suffering while maintaining divine detachment.</summary>

One is called the **outer man**, which is **sensuality** <term>sensuality: (Sinnlichkeit) In this context, it refers to the physical nature and the way we perceive the world through the five senses, rather than purely sexual desire.</term>; the five senses serve this person, yet he acts through the power of the soul. Now you should know that every person who loves God uses the powers of the soul in the outer man no more than the five senses strictly require; and the inwardness turns to the five senses only in so far as it serves as a guide and teacher to them, guarding them so that they do not use their objects in a beast-like way, as some people do who live for their physical lusts like animals that lack **reason** <note>original: Vernunft. A central term for Eckhart, referring to the intellectual power that allows humans to grasp spiritual truths.</note>. Such people should truly be called animals rather than humans. 

And the powers that the soul has beyond this, which she does not give to the five senses, she gives entirely to the inner man. When he has a high and noble object, the soul draws back to herself all the powers she had lent to the five senses, and this person is then said to be "out of his senses" and **rapt** <note>original: verzückt. A state of spiritual ecstasy or "rapture" where the soul’s attention is so focused on God that it loses awareness of the physical body.</note>, because his object is either a non-rational image or something rational without an image. <meta>This page continues the exploration of the "outer man" and "inner man," applying these categories to Christ and Mary, and introducing the famous analogy of the door and the hinge to explain how the soul remains still while the body acts.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

God desires that man loves him with all the powers of the soul. Therefore he said: "Love your God with your whole heart." Now there are some people who consume the powers of the soul entirely in the **outer man**. These are the people who direct all their senses and thoughts toward fleeting goods and know nothing of the **inner man**. Just as a good person sometimes deprives the outer man of all the powers of the soul when she <note>In German, "soul" (Seele) is a feminine noun, so Eckhart often refers to it as "she."</note> has a high task, so animalistic people deprive the inner man of all the soul's powers and use them for the outer man. 

Now you must know that the outer man can be in activity while the inner man stands entirely rid of the same and remains immovable. Now there was also an outer and an inner man in Christ, and likewise in Our Lady <note>original: "unserer Frau." A traditional title for the Virgin Mary.</note>, and everything that Christ and Our Lady ever spoke regarding external things, they did so as the outer man, while the inner man stood in an immovable <term>detachment: (Abgeschiedenheit) A core concept in Eckhart's philosophy, meaning a state of being completely "cut off" or "set apart" from worldly concerns and self-will to be perfectly open to God.</term>. 

Take an image for this: A door opens and closes on a **hinge**. Now I compare the outer board of the door to the outer man, and the hinge to the inner man. When the door opens and closes, the outer board moves back and forth, yet the hinge remains still <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, but the analogy implies that the hinge remains fixed and unmoved despite the door's movement.</note>

<summary>Eckhart distinguishes between the 'outer man' who interacts with the world and the 'inner man' who remains in a state of divine detachment. He uses the famous analogy of a door's hinge to illustrate how Christ and Mary could act in the world while their spirits remained unshakeable.</summary>

...immovable in one spot and is therefore not changed in the slightest. In the same way, it is also here <note>Referring to the inner man remaining still like the hinge while the outer man moves like the door.</note>.

Now I ask, what is the task of pure detachment? To this I answer, that neither this nor that is its task. It rests upon a mere nothing, for it rests upon the highest point in which God can act with all his power. Now God cannot work in all hearts despite all his will. For although God is almighty, he can only act when he finds readiness or potential <note>original: "Macht." In this context, it refers to the soul's capacity or "potentiality" to receive the divine.</note>. His working in humans is different than in stones; for this we find a parable in nature. 

If one heats an oven and places in it a dough of oats, and one of barley, and one of rye, and one of wheat, there is only one heat in the oven, and yet it does not act upon all the doughs equally; for one becomes a beautiful bread, another becomes coarse, and the third even coarser. The heat is not to blame for this, but rather the matter <note>The "matter" or "substance" of the different grains represents the different spiritual dispositions of people.</note>, which is unequal. Likewise, God does not act in all hearts equally, but according to how he finds readiness and receptivity. In those hearts where this or that thing resides, there can be something that hinders God from acting at the highest level. If, therefore, a heart is to have readiness for...

### On Detachment.

...we can take the word that Dionysius <note>original: "Dionysius." Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose mystical writings on the "Unknown God" were central to Eckhart’s thought.</note> speaks concerning Saint Paul’s word: “There are many who all run for the crown, and yet it is granted to only one.” <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 9:24.</note> All the powers of the soul run for the crown, and yet it is granted to the essence <note>original: "Wesen." In this context, it refers to the innermost core or 'ground' of the soul, distinct from its active faculties like memory or will.</note> alone. To this, Dionysius says: The race is nothing other than a turning away from all creatures and a uniting with uncreatedness. And when the soul attains this, she loses her name and draws God into herself, so that she becomes naught in herself, just as the sun draws in the morning dawn <note>original: "Morgenrot"—the red glow of sunrise.</note> so that it becomes naught. Nothing brings a person to this except pure detachment <term>Abgeschiedenheit: Often translated as 'detachment' or 'releasement,' this is the state of being completely free from self-will and worldly attachments, allowing God to be the sole actor in the soul.</term>.

Here the word spoken by Saint Augustine may also fit: The soul has a heavenly entrance into the divine nature, where all things become naught to her. On earth, this entrance is nothing other than pure detachment. And when detachment reaches its peak, it becomes unconscious through consciousness, and loveless through love, and dark through light <note>These paradoxes describe the "apophatic" or negative way to God, where the human way of knowing, loving, and seeing must fail because God transcends all human categories.</note>. Therefore we can also accept what a master says: Blessed are the poor in spirit, who have left all things to God, just as he had them when we were not. ## On the Supreme Joy.

Even if there were neither hell nor the kingdom of heaven, I would still want to love you, God, sweet Father, and your sublime nature, wherein the Trinity stands within the Unity. <note>The author expresses the ideal of "disinterested love"—loving God for His own sake, rather than out of fear of punishment (hell) or hope for reward (heaven).</note> Behold, now you may wish to hear of all the secrets of the sublime nature of the Trinity. There is a distinction; my soul perceives it in the reflection of the sublime Unity. This Unity shines into its own essence entirely without differentiation. Within it, she has enclosed all her unity, and yet with the distinction of the sublime Personality. The flow is primordial in which the unity lives; the simple One, which hovers within itself in dark silence, is without any need. <note>The "dark silence" is a common mystical term for the state of God that is beyond human words, concepts, or light.</note> No one can understand it, yet in its selfhood <term>Selbstheit: The state of being oneself; the pure, independent existence of the Divine.</term> it is manifest. ## **The Soul in Search of God.**

Those who serve God for the sake of a reward through **external works** <note>In this context, "external works" refers to outward religious acts like fasting, vocal prayer, or formal rituals performed specifically to "earn" a place in heaven.</note> shall be rewarded with created things, such as the kingdom of heaven and celestial matters. But those who serve God through **internal works**, they shall be rewarded with that which is **uncreated**, that is, with the works of the Holy Trinity!

Now, pay attention. If fire were to vanish, there would be no light; if earth were to vanish, there would be no life; if air were to vanish, there would be no love; if water were to vanish, there would be no space. <note>The author uses the four classical elements to illustrate how God transcends even the most fundamental components of reality.</note> Therefore, God is neither light, nor life, nor love, nor nature, nor spirit, nor appearance, nor anything that can be captured in words. It is God in God, and God has flowed forth from God, and God resides within Himself as God, and resides in all His creatures as God, and resides especially in a **noble soul** <term>edeln Seele: A central concept in German mysticism, referring to the soul that has detached itself from worldly images to become a "blank slate" for God’s presence.</term>. ## **The Soul in Search of God.**

[The Son is] all-wise, and the Holy Spirit is all-loving in the soul, and He loves all creatures with equal love. However, He reveals Himself to them unequally; and for this purpose the soul was created: that it should recognize things as they are, and should immerse itself in the purity of the **groundless well** <term>grundlosen Brunnens: A favorite metaphor of the Rhineland mystics, describing God as a "fountain" or "well" that is "groundless" because He has no beginning and is the foundation of all existence.</term> of divine nature. There, it should become as one with God, so that the soul itself could say that it *is* God. The soul should be so **detached** <term>abgezogen: Often translated as "withdrawn" or "abstracted," this refers to the mystical practice of stripping away all sensory perceptions and thoughts of physical things to reach a state of pure spiritual focus.</term> within itself that it cannot form images of any created or named things. It should be as stripped bare within itself as God is stripped of all names, and should rise above itself into its God, and regard itself as one with its God for the sake of its God; for God is neither white nor black, nor great nor small; He has neither space, nor past, nor future, and the soul is only equal to Him insofar as it can set itself above all **createdness**.

The soul is a creature that can receive all named things, but unnamed things it can only receive when it is received so deeply into God that it itself becomes **nameless**. And then no one can know whether God has seized the soul or the soul has seized God. **Dionysius** <note>This refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian whose works emphasized that God is "unknowable" and "beyond all names."</note> says that God has contained Himself within the soul and draws it so completely into Himself that it is no longer anything within itself but God. What the soul understands in the light, it loses in the darkness. And yet it strives toward the darkness, because the darkness seems more "passable" <note>original: "wegsamer." Where God has neither time nor essence, there He is unnamed.

### Meister Eckhart.

Now pay attention to when a human being is **every creature**. It is when they have the power of all things within them. When a person perceives all physical things with their external senses and then detaches themselves—yet remains within them without being touched by them—and when they perceive all spiritual things with their inner senses and then likewise detaches themselves and remains within them without being touched: only then is the human being every creature, and only then has that person come to their true nature and is ready to enter into God. 

The reason we do not find God is this: we seek Him through parables <note>original: "Gleichnissen." In Eckhart’s theology, this refers to images, analogies, or likenesses that the mind creates to represent God. He argues that since God is beyond all forms, these "likenesses" actually hinder true connection.</note>, even though He has no parable. Everything that Holy Scripture can provide is more *unlike* Him than *like* Him. Regarding this, Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD), an early Christian scholar and theologian often cited by Eckhart for his mystical views on the soul.</note> says that the soul's desire to search out God comes from its much "observing." If the soul knew herself, she would also know her God. That the soul forms an image of herself and an image of her God comes from the fact that she observes too much. When she sinks into the **Godhead** <term>Gottheit: For Eckhart, the "Godhead" is the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God, deeper even than the Trinity of Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</term>, all such observing is lost to her.

Regarding this, Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century mystic whose works on "negative theology"—the idea that we can only say what God is NOT—deeply influenced Eckhart’s thought.</note> says to Timothy: "My friend Timothy, if you become aware of the Spirit of Truth, do not follow after it with human senses, for it is very swift: it comes as a rushing sound." One should seek God with strangeness, with forgetfulness, and with un-

<summary>Eckhart explains that to enter God, one must become "every creature" by detaching from both physical and spiritual perceptions. He warns that God cannot be found through images or "observing," as God is ultimately unlike any human concept or parable.</summary>

Matter, form, intellect, and essence—all these she has lost in the **Unity**, for she has become nothing in herself. God performs all her works; He holds her in His essence and leads her by His power into the bare Godhead.

# On the Crossing to the Godhead.

As the sun shines, so the eye sees; then the eye is in the sun, and the sun is in the eye. Come now, my friend, pay attention to what I mean, for I hardly dare to write or speak my thought, because within the Persons <note>Referring to the three Persons of the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit). Eckhart often distinguishes between these "Persons" and the "Godhead" which lies behind them.</note> the divine nature is a mirror that language never reaches. Insofar as the soul can rise above language, so far she makes herself like the mirror. In the mirror, only what is alike is gathered.

When I, Lord, was in you, I was without need in my **nothingness** <term>Nichts: In Eckhart’s thought, "nothingness" is not a void but a state of being "without things"—stripping away all individual identity and desire to be perfectly empty for God.</term>, and your countenance—the fact that you looked at me—that made me needy. If it is a death when the soul is separated from God, then it is also a death that she flowed out of God <note>Eckhart suggests that the soul's original "flow" or creation into a separate person was itself a loss of its primal unity with the divine, a kind of "dying" into individuality.</note>, for every movement is a kind of dying. Therefore, we die from moment to moment, and the soul dies a total death into the wonder of the **Godhead** <term>Gottheit: The absolute essence of God, beyond all names or descriptions.</term>, since she [cannot reach] divine nature <note>The German text ends mid-word with "er-", likely a form of *erreichen* (to reach) or *erfassen* (to grasp), continuing on the next page.</note>...

# Of the Crossing to the Godhead.

...fire have been extinguished and have become nothing in him, a simple substance.

Blessed is the soul that leaps across to receive all things in the bare Godhead <note>The "bare" or "naked" Godhead (blosse Gottheit) refers to God's essence stripped of all names, images, or distinct attributes.</note>. The soul should be buried in the face of God; she should be drawn into the heavens where the three Persons dwell in the unity of their nature. This is the hidden Godhead about which one cannot speak. Blessed are those who make the **Crossing** <term>Ueberfahrt: A central concept in Eckhart’s mysticism describing the soul's transition from its created, limited self into the infinite, uncreated essence of God.</term>; to them, all things—which are otherwise unknown to all creatures—become known in truth.

The creature has an entrance into God, wherein its essence lies, and it acts in the power that moves it to come from nothing to something. Now Saint Paul and also Saint Augustine say: "How has it happened to me that I have become something from nothing, and God from a worm, and Creator from a creature?" <note>Eckhart frequently uses such "daring" language to describe the total transformation of the human through grace.</note> The soul should be so united in God that it seems to her there is nothing but God alone, and as if God never created any creature but her alone. The soul that performs this **Crossing** comes into a rest of all things. She is God, as he is in himself. Concerning this, Christ himself speaks: "I have been human for you, and if you are not God to me, you do me wrong." <note>original: "Ich bin euch Mensch gewesen, und wenn ihr mir nicht Gott seid, so tut ihr mir unrecht."</note> God became human so that we might become God. <meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the soul's "Crossing" into the Godhead. It explains how the soul must be hidden in God just as Christ’s divinity was hidden in his humanity, and explores the "nature of all natures."</meta>

<header>Meister Eckhart.</header>

...[just as the] divine nature was hidden in the human nature, so that one recognized nothing there but a human being. In the same way, the soul should hide herself in the divine nature, so that one can recognize nothing in her but God. God is not a "nature" in the way a creature is, who possesses one quality that another does not. For instance, if someone were both a baker and a brewer, one could not say he was a brewer alone, because he would also be a baker. In this way, God is the nature of all natures, because he contains the nature of all natures within himself, undivided. He is the light of all lights, he is the life of all the living, he is the essence <term>Wesen: Often translated as "being" or "essence," referring to the core reality or "is-ness" of a thing.</term> of all that has being, he is the speech of all who speak. Therefore, he is the nature of all natures.

Concerning this, Saint Dionysius <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 6th-century theologian who heavily influenced Eckhart. He taught "negative theology," which describes God by what He is NOT.</note> says: He cannot be called "a nature" for the reason that he is simple <note>In medieval philosophy, "simple" (einfach) means not made of parts; a pure, indivisible unity.</note> and nothing is like him. And further he says: One can only know God through unknowing <term>Unerkenntnis: Also known as "agnosia," this is the mystical state where the mind moves beyond intellectual concepts to encounter God directly in the "Cloud of Unknowing."</term>. When God enters the soul, he enters her with all things. However, while God possesses all things simply within himself, the soul possesses them through speech and with distinction: devils and angels and all things.

Thus the soul has the capacity to receive all things in God, and she recognizes what God is within them and what they are within God, and she soars up into simplicity, above all things, into unknowing. Concerning this, Saint Dionysius says that this is "dominion"—that one [rules] over ne—

->### Of the Crossing to the Godhead.<-

...rise above [other] things and those that are alongside them, and bring them into the highest [things]. For the Son—the Word—belongs to the Father; thus the Father reveals himself to the soul in the Word, because in his divine nature he has no form <note>Original: *Gestaltung*. In Eckhart’s thought, God’s essence is "without form" because he is not a limited "thing" like a creature.</note>. And in the same way, the soul speaks herself back into the Father in that same Word, because she has no form in her nothingness <note>Original: *Nichts*. This "nothingness" is not a lack of existence, but a state of being empty of all created images and ego.</note>; therefore, she leaves her “something” <note>Original: *Etwas*. This refers to the individual identity or the ego that separates the soul from God.</note> in the Word and casts herself, formless, into the Formless One. The Godhead is a naked, simple thing that holds the power of all things within itself, above the Persons <note>Referring to the three persons of the Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Eckhart often personifies the Godhead as a silent, underlying depth.</note> can give herself to no one, and no one can receive her so fully that she exists in them alone. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a primary influence on medieval mysticism.</note> says: The Godhead possesses all things. Therefore, the three Persons are in the Godhead, revealing the Godhead—each to the other and to the creature, insofar as the creature can receive it. The Father reveals the Godhead to himself and reveals it to his Son, and the Father and the Son reveal it to the Holy Spirit, and the three Persons reveal it to the creatures; and the Godhead plays with language, and before language, and above language, and language cannot grasp it. # Meister Eckhart.

->Of the Passage to the Godhead.<-

...has escaped the powers. Then the powers have lost the Godhead, and also their bare essence of the Godhead in the persons and in the powers; the powers follow into the essence, and they resist the tension <note>Original: *Sträuben*. In the Nothingness of the Godhead, the Father has his perfection, and the three persons their unity, and they give all creatures their perfection in their created "Something"; and the soul flows in her "Something" within the Nothingness of the Godhead through all things, yet she does not touch them in the "Something" of her essence.
