This does not mean the Godhead ceases to exist, but that it is "naughted"—rendered incomprehensible and "no-thing" to the soul's natural faculties.</note>, because the powers of the soul cannot grasp it.

## Concerning the Wrath of the Soul.

The loving soul becomes angry through its self-knowledge. She has received a countenance quite powerfully and is red and angry because of that which has remained above her, which stays unreachable in God: that she is not all that God is by nature, and that she does not possess all that God possesses by nature.

Now the masters <note>Original: *Meister*. Usually referring to established scholastic theologians or spiritual authorities like Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great.</note> say that this is also a fierce wrath <note>In mystical theology, "wrath" (*Zorn*) is not a sin, but an expression of the soul's intense, "jealous" longing for total union with God, frustrated by its status as a created being.</note>, when a friend wishes to possess his friend himself and all that he has. The soul says that her wrath is so boundless that it cannot be reconciled with her. The bond of love is all too strong for her. She speaks: "Ah, who can comfort me? My misfortune is far too great! If I were the Creator, simple and without beginning or end, and if I had created the creatures, and if He were the soul as I am, then I would want to step out of all this being and let her enter in to be God, and I would..."

<meta>This page contains a collection of theological fragments, likely attributed to the medieval mystic Meister Eckhart, exploring the relationship between the soul, nature, and the Divine.</meta>

## Fragments.

1. All creatures are a footprint of God.
2. God is not a destroyer of nature; rather, he fulfills <note>Or "perfects." The idea is that grace does not replace nature but brings it to its intended completion.</note> it.
3. Man cannot know what God is. One thing he does know: what God is not. <note>This refers to "apophatic theology" or the "way of negation," suggesting that God is so far beyond human language that we can only describe Him by what He is not.</note>
4. God loves my soul so powerfully that his being and his life depend on the fact that he must love me, whether it pleases him or not. Whoever would take from God the fact that he loves me, would take from him his divinity.
5. He who gives his will entirely to God captures and binds God, so that God can do nothing but what man wills.
6. Knowledge comes from comparison. Because the soul, then, has the potential to know all things, it never rests until it reaches the primary image <note>The "original image" or "archetype" in which all creation exists in unity.</note> where all things are one; and there it rests, which is in God. In God, no creature is of a different rank than another. The Masters <note>A common reference in medieval texts to established academic or spiritual authorities, such as the Church Fathers.</note> say: Being <term>Wesen: can be translated as "essence," "being," or "nature"</term> and knowledge are one and the same.

<summary>A series of mystical aphorisms exploring the intimate, reciprocal relationship between God and the human soul, emphasizing that divine love is an essential necessity of God's own nature.</summary>

### Meister Eckhart.

7. God is nowhere. All of creation <note>The original term "Kreatur" refers to all created things, including humans, animals, and inanimate objects.</note> is full of the least part of God, and his greatest part is nowhere.

8. If God were not in all things, nature would neither work nor desire anything in any thing; for whether it brings you joy or sorrow, and whether you know it or not: nature, in its innermost depths, seeks and intends God. Never would a person who is thirsty desire something to drink so much if there were not something of God in it. Nature intends neither food nor drink, nor clothes, nor comfort, nor anything else, if God were not in it, and it hunts and drills ever deeper to find God within them.

9. If the image that is formed according to God <note>This refers to the human soul, which according to Genesis is made in the "image and likeness" of God.</note> were to pass away, then the image of God would also pass away.

10. Reason <term>Vernunft: in Eckhart’s thought, this is the highest power of the soul, sometimes called the "intellect," which seeks to penetrate beyond appearances to the essence of things</term> is penetrating; it is not satisfied with goodness or wisdom or truth, and not even with God himself. It is a solid truth: it is as little satisfied with God as it is with a stone or a tree. <note>Eckhart suggests that the intellect seeks the "Godhead"—the nameless, essential source—rather than the human concept or name of "God."</note>

11. Just as it is true that God became man, so it is true that man has become God. <note>This refers to the mystical doctrine of "deification" or *theosis*, where the soul is unified with the Divine.</note>

12. It is God’s nature that he is without nature. <note>This echoes the "way of negation," suggesting that God is beyond all definitions, categories, or "natures" that apply to created things.</note>

# Fragments.

13. God can do what he wills; therefore, he has made you completely equal to himself and has made you an image of himself. I know and can go no further; let this discourse be at an end.

14. If I do not compel God to do everything that I want, then I am lacking either in humility or in longing. <note>Eckhart often used provocative language like "compelling" God to illustrate that when a person is perfectly aligned with God's will, God cannot help but act through them.</note>

15. Where does one see God? Where there is neither Yesterday nor Tomorrow, where there is a Today and a Now—there one sees God. What is God? One master speaks: If it must be necessary that I speak of God, then I say that God is something that no sense can grasp or attain; otherwise, I know nothing of him. Another master says: He who recognizes this of God—that he is unknown—he is the one who recognizes God. <note>This is a core principle of "apophatic" or negative theology: we understand God best by realizing what He is NOT, rather than what He is.</note> When I preach in Paris, I say, and I may well say it: all here in Paris with all their science <term>Wissenschaft: in this context, the formal, academic study of theology and philosophy at the university.</term> cannot grasp what God is in the smallest creature <note>The original term "Kreatur" refers to any created thing.</note>, even in a gnat. But I say now: the whole world cannot grasp it. Everything that one can think about God, that is not God at all. What God is in himself, to that no one can come, who

# Meister Eckhart.

...is not caught up into a light that is God himself. What God is to the angels is very far off and no one knows it. What God is in a God-loving soul, no one knows except the soul in which he is. What God is in these lower things, I know a little, but very weakly. Where God dwells in knowledge <term>knowledge: "Erkenntnis," here referring to a high spiritual or contemplative understanding</term>, there all natural sensuality <term>sensuality: "Sinnlichkeit," the physical perception and attachment to the material world</term> falls away. That we may be so caught up into a light that is God himself, in order to be blessed in it for eternity—may God grant this. Amen.

16. The word that Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian for Eckhart.</note> speaks: "What a man loves, that is the man," is to be understood in the following way: if he loves a stone, he is a stone; if he loves a man, he is a man; if he loves God—now, I do not dare to speak further, for if I say that he then is God, you might want to stone me. <note>Eckhart was frequently accused of heresy for suggesting "theosis" or divinization—the idea that the soul could become one with God's essence.</note>

17. To the righteous, justice is so serious that, if it were the case that God were not just, they would not care a bean <note>original: "nicht eine Bohne," a common idiom meaning "not in the slightest"</note> for God.

18. All love in this world is built upon self-love. If you had abandoned that, you would have abandoned the whole world. <note>This refers to the concept of "Gelassenheit" or detachment, a central theme in Eckhart's teaching.</note>

19. I was considering recently whether I should take or desire anything from God. I will...

# Fragments
<sig>14</sig>

...reflect very deeply, for if I were to take anything from God, I would be beneath God like a servant beneath his master through the act of giving. But we should not be like that in eternal life. <note>Eckhart suggests that a true union with God transcends the "master and servant" dynamic, where the soul is no longer a recipient of "gifts" but one with the Giver.</note>

20. Some simple people believe they should see God as if he were standing there and they were here. This is not so. God and I are one in knowing <term>knowing: "Erkennen," here referring to a mystical, participatory awareness rather than mere intellectual facts</term>. Therefore, if I take God into myself through love, I enter into God. We should be knowers of Him—I knowing Him just as He knows me, neither less nor more, but simply equal.

21. Love takes God as He is God; and from this name <note>Referring to the conceptual name "God."</note>, God falls away. Goodness and love never progress further. Love takes God under a skin, under a cloak. <note>This likely refers to love seeing God through the "garment" of his manifestations or through the physical world.</note> The intellect <term>intellect: "Verstand," the rational or analytical mind</term> does not do this: the intellect takes God as He is known within itself; yet there it can never grasp Him in the sea of His groundlessness <term>groundlessness: "Grundlosigkeit," the bottomless abyss or the absolute nature of the Divine that lacks any "ground" or limit</term>.

22. A master <note>Possibly a reference to Aristotle or a contemporary Scholastic philosopher.</note> who spoke most excellently about the soul says that all human science can never discover what the soul is. Supernatural science is required for that. The powers of the soul go out into its works. We know nothing of this—we may know a little, but as for what the soul is in its ground <term>ground: "Grund," the innermost core or essence of the soul</term>, no one knows anything.

...most excellent; all this is the same for this power <note>This "power" refers to the "spark of the soul" or the highest part of the intellect discussed in preceding sections.</note>. It takes all things beyond "here" and beyond "now." "Now" is time, and "here" is space.

24. I once reflected (it was not long ago): that I am a human being is something common to me and all other people; that I see and hear and eat and drink is something another animal does as well; but that *I am* <note>original: "dass ich bin." Eckhart distinguishes between shared biological traits and the pure fact of existence (Being), which he views as the point of contact with God.</note> belongs to no one else but me alone—neither to any human, nor to an angel, nor to God, except insofar as I am one with Him. Everything that God performs, He performs in the One, equal to Himself, and yet in the results of His works, they are entirely unequal to one another.

25. For the one who, while in time, has set their heart upon eternity, and in whom all temporal things are dead, there is the fulfillment of time. I once said: those who rejoice within time do not rejoice "at all times." Saint Paul says: "Rejoice in God at all times." <note>original: "Freuet euch in Gott allezeit." A reference to Philippians 4:4 (Gaudete in Domino semper).</note> He rejoices at all times who rejoices above time and without time. Three things hinder a person so that they cannot know God in any way. The first is **time**, the second is **corporality** <term>corporality: "Körperlichkeit," the state of being a physical body or being focused on material form</term>, and the third is **multiplicity** <term>multiplicity: "Mannigfaltigkeit," the state of being many things or variety, which contrasts with the Divine Unity</term>. As long as these three are within me, God is not in me and does not work in His own way within me. Saint Augustine says: it comes from the soul's greed that it wants to grasp and possess many things; thus, it reaches into time, into corporality, and into multi- <note>The text breaks off mid-word ("Mannigfaltigkeit"), likely continuing on the following page.</note>

<summary>Eckhart explores the distinction between shared human nature and the unique "is-ness" of the individual soul, concluding that one must transcend time, the body, and multiplicity to allow God to work within them.</summary>

...plicity <note>original: "faltigkeit," completing the word "Mannigfaltigkeit" (multiplicity) from the previous page.</note> and thereby loses exactly what it has. For as long as "more and more" is within you, God can never dwell or act within you. These things must always go out if God is to come in, unless you possess them in a higher and better way, so that from a multitude, "one" has become. The more multiplicity is then in you, the more unity there is, for the one is transformed into the other. I once said: Unity unites all multiplicity, but multiplicity does not unite unity. If we are lifted above all things, and everything within us is suspended <note>original: "aufgehoben." In Eckhart’s thought, this means individual, worldly distinctions are not simply destroyed, but are raised up and resolved into a higher spiritual unity.</note>, then nothing weighs us down. If I were purely God-seeking <note>original: "gottmeinend." This refers to a state where a person's intention or "meaning" is directed solely toward God without secondary motives.</note>, such that nothing were above me but God, then nothing would be difficult for me and I would not so easily become distressed.

26. In the ground of the soul <term>ground of the soul: "Seelengrund," the deepest, essential core of a person that remains untouched by external things and is the site of union with the Divine</term>, the power that works in the eyes is just as high in rank as the intellect, and there the foot and the eye are equally noble. What the soul is in its ground has never yet been discovered.

27. The masters say that human nature has nothing to do with time, and that it is entirely untouchable and much more intimate and closer to a person than they are to themselves. And for this reason, God took on human nature and joined it to His person. Then human nature became God, because He took on purely human nature...

<meta>This page continues fragment 27 from page 211, which discusses how the eternal Word (the Logos) took on universal human nature rather than a specific, individual person. It then proceeds through fragments 28, 29, and 30 regarding the nature of God, time, and the "One."</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>Master Eckhart.</header>

...and did not take on any individual man. <note>This completes the sentence from page 211: "The masters say that human nature has no [individual personality]..."</note> If you wish, therefore, to be Christ yourself and to be God, then depart from everything that the eternal Word did not take upon itself. The eternal Word did not take any individual man unto itself: therefore, depart from what is "individual person" in you and what you are uniquely, and conduct yourself purely according to universal human nature; then you are the same in the eternal Word as human nature is in Him. For your human nature and His have no difference: they are one; for what it is in Christ, that is what it is in you.

28. Nothing is so opposed to God as time. <note>Eckhart frequently taught that God exists in an "eternal now," and that human preoccupation with past and future prevents union with the Divine.</note>

29. "He had no name." In this way, the Trinity of the Godhead is without name; for all the names that the soul gives Him, she takes from her own intellect. Regarding this, a pagan master <note>Likely a reference to Proclus or the author of the *Liber de Causis*, though Eckhart often categorized Neoplatonic philosophers as "pagan masters."</note> says in the book called "Light of Lights": God is super-essential <term>super-essential: "überwesenhaft," meaning existing beyond or above the category of "being" or "substance" as understood by human logic</term>, beyond language, and incomprehensible in relation to what constitutes natural understanding.

30. A master says: "One" is a negative statement <note>original: "untersagendes Aussagen." This refers to apophatic or "negative" theology, which suggests that we can only say what God is NOT, rather than what He is, because God transcends all human categories.</note>. If I say: "God is good," then something is being added to Him. "One" is a negative statement and a restrictive desire. What does "One" mean? Something to which nothing is added. The soul receives the Godhead as it is purified within her, where nothing is added, where nothing is thought...

...is. <note>This completes the thought from page 212 regarding the definition of the One.</note> "One" is a denial of denials <term>denial of denials: Eckhart uses the German "Untersagen des Aussagens" to translate the Latin "negatio negationis." It means that God is so utterly one that He negates any possibility of being anything else.</term>. All creatures have some kind of denial within them; one states that it is not the other; an angel states that he is not another creature. But God has a denial of all denials; He is One and denies everything else, for nothing exists outside of God. All creatures are in God and are His very Godhead, and they wish to fulfill Him. He is a Father of all Godhead. It is called a Godhead <note>Eckhart distinguishes between "God," the active Creator, and the "Godhead," the silent, unchanging essence from which everything flows.</note> because nothing flows out from it, and nothing touches it anywhere, and no word is thought there. By saying something about God—if I say God is "goodness," I cannot truly express God—by saying something about Him, I understand something about Him which He is not; precisely that must fall away. God is One; He is a denial of denials.

31. One reason why it would be unworthy of me and distasteful to me to pray to God to make me healthy is that I neither want to nor should I ask the rich, loving, and generous God for such a trifle. Suppose I traveled a hundred or two hundred miles to the Pope, and when I came before him, I said:

> "O Lord and Holy Father, I have traveled two hundred miles at great expense on difficult roads, and have come here to ask you to give me a bean..."

32. Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD) was a Roman Stoic philosopher. If there were nothing new, nothing would ever become old.

33. Just as the Godhead <term>Godhead: Eckhart uses the term "Gottheit" to refer to the absolute, hidden essence of God, distinguished from "God" as the active Creator.</term> is in all things, it is the soul of all souls. The Godhead is the soul of the creature.

Transformations in His power. Concerning this, Saint Dionysius says: God is a mover of the soul. Therefore, form is a revelation of the essence <term>essence: "Wesen" in German, referring to the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing</term>. Concerning this, Saint Dionysius says that form is the "somethingness" of the essence. There is no matter without form. Therefore, the soul never rests until it enters into God, who is its primary form. There, the soul unites with God as food unites with a human: it becomes eye in the eyes and ear in the ears. Thus the soul becomes God in God: it unites itself with every divine power just as that power is in God, and God unites Himself in the soul just as every power is in the soul; and the two natures flow into one light, and the soul, being all-present <note>original: "allwesend," meaning present in all being or essence</note>, becomes nothing. What she is, she is in God. The divine powers draw her into themselves without looking, just as the sun draws all creatures to it without looking.

What God is in Himself, no one can comprehend. God is for Himself in all things; God is all things in all things, and God is to every single thing all things at once. Thus the soul should be. God is to no thing completely "nothing"; God is not completely nothing for Himself; rather, God is a "nothing" that cannot be captured in words <note>This refers to "apophatic theology," where God is called "Nothing" not because He doesn't exist, but because He is so far beyond human language and "things" that no word can describe Him.</note>. Here, all humans are one human, and this very human is Christ. Regarding this, a master says that the earth was never emptied of these people and will never be emptied, for the human being encloses heaven and earth within themselves. If the human being did not exist, then neither heaven nor earth would exist.

<page-num>217</page-num>
<header>Fragments.</header>

...it was not out of wickedness that he sinned; rather, he does it for the sake of his <term>passionate desire</term> <note>original: "Lustgier"; in mystical German, this refers to a deep, driving longing for satisfaction or joy, even when misdirected toward sin.</note>. One person strikes another dead; he does not do this to perform an evil act, but because it seems to him that as long as the other lives, he himself could never come to peace within himself. Therefore, he seeks delight in peace, for peace brings joy. In this way, every creature <term>hunts</term> <note>original: "jagt"; a characteristic Eckhartian term implying an active, intense, and natural pursuit of the divine source.</note> God with its love, for God is love. Thus, all creatures desire love. If a stone were rational, it would be compelled to hunt God with its love. If one asked a tree why it bears its fruit, and if it possessed reason, it would say: "I do this so that I may renew myself in the fruit; I do this to draw near to my <term>origin</term> <note>original: "Ursprung"; the source or "ground" from which all things flow and to which they seek to return.</note> once again; for to be near the origin is full of delight." God is the origin and is delight and love.

39. God is everywhere in the soul and she is everywhere in Him; thus God is a Whole, and she with Him is an <term>all in all</term> <note>original: "Alles in Allem"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 15:28, describing the ultimate state where God and creation are perfectly unified.</note>.

## Sayings.

1. Master Eckhart says: Whoever is at home in all places is worthy of God; and whoever remains one in all times, to them God is present; and in whomever all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "Kreaturen"; in Eckhart’s thought, this refers to all created things and worldly distractions that are not God.</note> have come to silence, in them God gives birth to His only-begotten Son.

2. Master Eckhart says: A <term>master of living</term> <note>original: "Lebemeister"; someone who teaches through the example of their life and spiritual experience.</note> would be more necessary than a thousand <term>masters of reading</term> <note>original: "Lesemeister"; a play on words referring to scholars or lecturers who teach only from books and theory.</note>; but to read and to live without God is something no one can achieve. If I wanted to seek a master of the Scriptures, I would look for him in Paris and in the high schools of advanced science <note>At this time, the University of Paris was the most prestigious center for theological study in the Western world.</note>. But if I wanted to ask about the perfect life, he could tell me nothing of it. Where should I go for that? Nowhere else than into a naked, <term>divested nature</term> <note>original: "entledigte Natur"; a state of the soul that has been stripped of all ego, attachments, and worldly images.</note>: that nature could reveal to me what I asked of it in reverence. People, what are you seeking in the dead bones? Why do you not seek the living salvation that can give you eternal life? For the dead have neither to give nor to take. And should

<summary>In these fragments, Meister Eckhart emphasizes that the "birth of God" occurs only when the soul is silenced and detached, and he famously argues that a single teacher who lives the truth is more valuable than many who merely study it.</summary>

# Sayings.

...an angel to seek God without God <note>This likely refers to seeking God's essence rather than just His gifts or external manifestations.</note>, he would seek Him nowhere else than in a <term>divested</term> <note>original: "entledigten"; meaning a soul that has been emptied of all specific things, images, or worldly attachments.</note>, naked, <term>detached</term> <note>original: "abgeschieden"; a central concept in Eckhart’s theology, referring to the state of being totally unmoved by worldly concerns and centered only on God.</note> creature. All perfection depends on this: that one can suffer poverty and misery and shame and adversity, and everything that can happen to you and oppress you, willingly, joyfully, freely, eagerly, and ready and unmoved, and remain in it until death without any "why" <note>Eckhart often teaches that we should live and love "without a why" (ohne Warum), acting from our inner essence rather than for the sake of a reward or a specific reason.</note>.

3. Master Eckhart said: For whom things are different in one circumstance than in another, and to whom God is dearer in one thing than in another, that person is ordinary and still far off and a child. But for whom God is the same in all things, he has become a man. But for whom all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "Kreaturen"; meaning all created, finite things as opposed to the infinite Creator.</note> are superfluous and foreign, he has arrived at the truth.

He was also asked: if a person wished to go out of themselves, should they still care for anything natural? Then he said: God’s burden is light and His yoke is gentle; He desires it nowhere but in the will; and what is a horror to the sluggish person is a heart’s joy to the <term>raptured</term> <note>original: "hingerissenen"; one who is spiritually caught up, ecstatic, or moved by divine grace.</note> one. No one is full of God except the one who is dead in the <term>ground</term> <note>original: "Grunde"; referring to the "ground of the soul," the deepest, most essential part of the human spirit where it is one with God.</note>.

4. God ordains nothing for us through which He does not lure us to Himself. I never want to thank God for loving me, because

Meister Eckhart.

...he cannot help it; his nature <note>Eckhart frequently argues that God’s essence is love and goodness, meaning God does not act out of a choice between options, but out of the necessity of His own perfect nature.</note> compels him to it; I will thank him for this, that in his goodness he cannot help it, that he must love me.

5. Master Eckhart said: I will never ask God to give himself to me; I will ask him to make me empty and pure. For if I were empty and pure, God would have to give himself to me out of his own nature and be enclosed within me. <note>The idea here is that God, like a vacuum being filled by air, must fill any soul that has been completely emptied of "self" and worldly attachments.</note>

6. Master Eckhart says: That we do not compel God to do what we wish is because we lack two things: <term>humility</term> <note>original: "Demut"; here meaning a groundedness in one’s own nothingness before God.</note> from the depth of the heart and powerful <term>desire</term> <note>original: "Begehren"; a spiritual longing or "will" that reaches toward the divine.</note>. I say this upon my life—God is capable of all things in his divine power, but he is not capable of this: that he should not grant the request of the person who possesses these two things. Therefore, do not occupy yourselves with small things, for you were not created for small things; for worldly honor is nothing but a transformation and a straying from <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit"; the state of eternal happiness or salvation in union with God.</note>.

7. 8. Brother Eckhart preached and said: Saint Peter said: I have left all things. Then Saint James spoke: we have all things <note>This likely refers to the apostolic paradox of having nothing yet possessing everything in God.</note>

### Sayings.

...given away. Then Saint John <note>Likely referring to Saint John the Evangelist, who was often invoked in mystical literature as a model of divine love and insight.</note> said: "We have nothing left at all." Then Brother Eckhart said: "When has one let go of all things? When one lets go of everything that the senses can grasp, and everything that one can speak, and everything that can create color <note>In this context, "color" refers to the outward appearances or the distinct qualities of worldly things that occupy the mind.</note>, and everything that one can hear—only then has one let go of all things. When one lets go of all things in this way, one is clarified and transfigured <note>original: "durchklärt und überklärt"; these terms suggest being thoroughly permeated by divine light and raised to a higher state of spiritual clarity.</note> by the **Godhead**." <note>original: "Gottheit"; Eckhart distinguishes the Godhead—the absolute, unchanging essence of God—from the active persons of the Trinity.</note>

9. Whoever wishes to become what they ought to be must let go of what they are now. When God created the angels, the first look they took was to see the Father’s <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Wesen"; the fundamental nature or "being" of God.</note> and how the Son grew out of the Father’s heart, just like a green sprout from a tree. They have held this joyful vision for more than six thousand years <note>Based on medieval biblical chronology, which often dated creation to approximately 4000-5000 BC.</note>, and what it truly is, they know no more today than they did at that moment when they were first created. And this comes from the greatness of <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Erkenntnis"; refers here to spiritual realization rather than mere data.</note>: for the more one knows, the less one understands. <note>This paradox is central to Eckhart’s "apophatic" or "negative" theology: the more the soul experiences the infinite nature of God, the more it realizes God is beyond all human understanding.</note>

10. And this is how a person who wishes to become perfect should direct their life. Concerning this, Master Eckhart speaks: The works that a person performs from within are joyful, both to the person and to God; they are gentle and are called **living works**. They are precious to God because...

# Meister Eckhart.

because he alone is the one who performs the works in the person that are performed from within. These works are also sweet and gentle to the person, for all those works are sweet and pleasurable to the person where body and soul become unified with one another. And that happens in all such works. These works are also called living works, for this is the difference between a dead animal and a living animal: that the dead animal can only be moved by an external motion—that is, when one pulls or carries it—and therefore all its works are dead works. But the living animal moves itself wherever it wishes, for its motion proceeds from within, and all its works are living works. In exactly the same way, all the works of people that take their origin from within, where God alone moves, and which proceed from the <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Wesen"; the "ground" or fundamental being where the soul and God are one.</note>, are called our works and divine works and useful works. But all those works that happen from an external cause and not from the inner essence, those are dead and are not divine works and are not our works. Master Eckhart also says that all those works that a person performs from within are **voluntary works** <note>original: "willkürliche Werke"; in medieval German, this refers to actions arising freely from the faculty of the will, rather than the modern sense of "arbitrary."</note>. Now, whatever is voluntary is pleasant, and therefore all works that happen from within are pleasant,

11. Master Eckhart said that no person in this life could reach a point where they should not also perform external works. For when a person gives themselves to the <term>contemplative life</term> <note>original: "beschaulichen Leben"; the medieval ideal of a life focused on prayer and internal meditation.</note>, they cannot contain themselves due to the great abundance; they must pour it out and must be active in the <term>active life</term> <note>original: "wirkenden Leben"; the life of practical service and labor in the world.</note>. Just as a person who has absolutely nothing can indeed be <term>generous</term> <note>original: "mild"; in this context, the virtue of liberality or spiritual charity.</note>, for they give with their will; however, if a person has great wealth and gives nothing, they cannot be called generous. And likewise, no one can possess a virtue if they do not devote themselves to that virtue whenever time and space permit. And for this reason, all those who give themselves to the contemplative life but not to external works, and who shut themselves off entirely from external work, are in error and are not on the right path. Thus I say, the person who is in the contemplative life certainly can and should free themselves from all external works as long as they are in <term>contemplation</term> <note>original: "Schauen"; a technical term for the mystical "beholding" or direct vision of God.</note>; but afterwards, they should devote themselves to external works, for no one can—

<summary>Eckhart argues that the contemplative life and the active life must coexist, as true spiritual abundance naturally overflows into outward acts of virtue. He warns that those who completely abandon external works in favor of isolation are in error.</summary>

### Meister Eckhart.

...constantly and continually to the <term>contemplative life</term> <note>original: "beschaulichen Leben"; the medieval ideal of a life focused on prayer and internal meditation.</note>, and the <term>active life</term> <note>original: "wirkende Leben"; the life of practical service and labor in the world.</note> becomes a dwelling-place of the life of beholding.

12. Master Eckhart and other masters as well say that two things are in God: essence and perception, which is called <term>relationship</term> <note>original Latin: "relatio"; in medieval theology, this refers to the "subsistent relations" (like Fatherhood or Sonship) that distinguish the persons of the Trinity while remaining one in essence.</note>. Now the masters say that the Father’s essence does not beget the Son within the <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "Gottheit"; often used by Eckhart to refer to the inner, undifferentiated essence of God as opposed to the Trinity of persons.</note>, for according to His essence, the Father sees nothing other than His own bare essence and beholds Himself therein with all His power; and there He beholds Himself purely without the Son and without the Holy Spirit, and sees there nothing but the unity of His very essence. 

But when the Father desires to have a beholding and a perception of Himself in another person, then the Father’s essence begets the Son in that perception; and because He is so well-pleased with Himself in that perception, and the beholding is so full of delight for Him, and because He has possessed all delight eternally, He must therefore have possessed this perception eternally. For this reason, then, the Son is eternal like the Father; and from the delight and the love which the Father and Son have for one another, the Holy Spirit has His origin. And because this love between Father and Son has been eternal, the Holy Spirit is just as eternal as the Father and the Son, and

13. Master Eckhart says: God is not only a Father of all things; He is much more also a Mother of all things. For He is a Father because He is a cause and a <term>Creator</term> <note>original: "Schöpfer"</note> of all things. However, He is also a Mother of all things, for when the <term>creature</term> <note>original: "Kreatur"; in this theological context, "creature" refers to anything created by God, including humans, animals, and objects.</note> receives its <term>being</term> <note>original: "Wesen"; can also be translated as "essence" or "nature."</note> from Him, He remains with the creature and maintains it in its being. For if God did not remain with and in the creature when it comes into its being, it would necessarily soon fall away from its being. For whatever falls out of God falls from its being into a <term>nothingness</term> <note>original: "Nichtheit"; a central concept in Eckhart’s philosophy, suggesting that without God's sustaining presence, a thing ceases to exist entirely.</note>. It is not so with other causes, for they may well depart from the things they have caused once those things come into their being. When a house comes into its being, the carpenter leaves; this is because the carpenter is not the complete and total cause of the house, but rather he takes the material from nature. God, on the other hand, gives the creature absolutely everything that it is—both <term>form</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, "form" is the organizing principle or essence that makes a thing what it is.</note> and <term>matter</term> <note>The physical substance of a thing.</note>—and therefore He must remain present, because otherwise the creature would soon fall away from its being.

15. Master Eckhart said: How does He come, who is unchangeable, and how does He come, who is in all places? To whom does He come, who is in all hearts? # Sayings.

no one finds who seeks it in external things. As Mary Magdalene renounced all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "Kreatur"; in Eckhart’s vocabulary, this refers to any created, finite thing as opposed to the infinite Creator.</note> and entered into her heart, she found our Lord. God is pure and clear; therefore, I can find God nowhere but in what is pure. The innermost part of my soul, however, is clearer and purer than any creature; therefore, I find God most surely in my innermost self.

### 17. ###

That God is at rest is what sets all things in motion. There is something so full of delight that it causes all things to run, so that they might return to that from which they came, and which yet remains immovable within itself; and the higher the level a thing is on, the more delightfully it runs. <note>Eckhart is referencing the philosophical concept of the "Unmoved Mover"—God remains still while His beauty and perfection draw all of creation back toward Him.</note>

### 18. ###

God can no more tolerate <term>likenesses</term> <note>original: "Gleichnisse"; here meaning images, analogies, or any representation that is merely "like" God rather than being God.</note> than He can tolerate not being God. Likeness is that which is not of God. In the <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "Gottheit"; the absolute, undifferentiated essence of God.</note> and in eternity there is <term>Oneness</term> <note>original: "Einssein".</note>, but likeness is not Oneness. If I am one, then I am not "like." Likeness is not the form of essence in unity; unity gives me Oneness in the unity, not "likeness." <note>Eckhart distinguishes between being "like" God (which implies a distance or a copy) and being "one" with God (which is the total disappearance of distinction in mystical union).</note>

### 19. ###

What can be sweeter than to have a friend with whom you can speak about everything in your heart as if with yourself? This is true.

20. What is God’s speaking? The Father looks upon Himself in a <term>simple knowledge</term> <note>original: "einfachen Erkenntnis"; in medieval philosophy, "simple" means unified and without parts or complexity.</note> and looks into the simple purity of His essence; there He sees all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "Kreaturen"; all created things.</note> as they are formed. Then He speaks Himself; this Word is clear understanding, and that is the Son.

21. When one says "human," one understands by this a person; when one says "humanity," one means the nature of all humans. The Masters <note>A common reference by Eckhart to scholastic authorities like Aristotle or Thomas Aquinas.</note> ask what "nature" is. It is a thing that can receive essence. Therefore, God united <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "Menschtum"; the universal essence of being human, as opposed to a specific individual person.</note> with Himself, not a specific human being. I say: Christ was the first human. Why? That which is first in intention is the last in execution, just as a roof is the last part of a house to be built <note>Eckhart uses a classic philosophical principle: the goal or purpose (the roof/Christ) exists in the mind before the work begins, even if it is completed last.</note>.

22. The highest countenance of the soul has two works. With the one, it understands God and His goodness and what flows out from Him. For this reason, it loves God today and understands Him, but perhaps not tomorrow. Therefore, the <term>Image</term> <note>original: "Bild"; referring to the "Imago Dei" or the divine spark within the human soul.</note> does not reside in the soul's powers <note>The "powers" (will, memory, intellect) were seen as fluctuating and unstable.</note> because of their unsteady nature. The other work is in the highest countenance, which is hidden. In this hiddenness lies the Image. This Image has five qualities. First, it is formed after another. Second, it is ordered within itself. Third, it has flowed out. Fourth, it is like itself by nature, not that it is divin— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "gött-" (göttlich), likely continuing as "divine" on the following page.</note>

The soul has three powers within it. The image does not reside in these. # Meister Eckhart.

**Being** stands on such a high level that it gives being to all things. If there were no **being**, an angel would be the same as a stone.

23. A high <term>master of theology</term> <note>original: "Lesemeister"; literally a "reading master," this was a formal title for a teacher of holy scripture or theology, particularly within the Dominican Order.</note> related this story in a sermon before a high assembly: There was once a man—one reads of him in the writings of the saints—who for well over eight years desired that God might show him a person who could point him toward the way to the truth. And while he was in a state of intense longing, a voice came from God and spoke to him: "Go to the front of the church; there you will find a man who shall point you toward the way to the truth."

And he went and found a poor man whose feet were cracked and covered in mud, and all his clothes were worth barely three pennies. He greeted him, saying: "God give you a good morning," and the other replied: "I never had a bad morning!" He said: "God give you good fortune! Why do you answer me so?" And he replied: "I never had bad fortune." He spoke again: "By your <term>bliss</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit"; refers to both eternal salvation and the state of supreme happiness or beatitude.</note>! Why do you answer me so?" He replied: "I was never unblessed." 

Then he said: "May God give you <term>salvation</term> <note>original: "Heil"; carries the dual meaning of spiritual salvation and wholeness or well-being.</note>! Enlighten me, for I cannot understand it." 

He replied: "That I will do. You said to me that God should give me a good morning; then...

### Proverbs

...I said: I never had a bad morning. If I am hungry, I praise God; if I am miserable and in shame, I praise God: and therefore I never had a bad morning. When you spoke, saying that God might give me good fortune, I said I never had bad fortune. For whatever God gave me or ordained for me, be it joy or suffering, sour or sweet, I took it all from God as the best: therefore I never had bad fortune. You spoke, by my <term>bliss</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit"; refers to the state of being eternally blessed or in a state of perfect happiness in God.</note>, and then I said: I was never unblessed, for I have given my <term>will</term> <note>original: "Wille"; a central concept in Eckhart’s teaching, where the human will is "annihilated" or merged into the Divine will.</note> so completely into God's will: what God wills, that I also will, therefore I was never unblessed, for I wanted alone God's will." 

"Oh, dear man, if God now wanted to cast you into hell, what would you say to that?" 

Then he spoke: "Cast me into hell? I would like to see that! And even then, if He threw me into hell, I have two arms with which I would embrace Him. One is true **humility**, which I would lay around Him, and I would embrace Him with the arm of **love**." And then he said: "I would rather be in hell and have God, than in the kingdom of heaven and not have God."

### Master Eckhart

...did you leave him? — In virtuous hearts.
— Where are you going? — To God. — Where do you find him? — Where I left all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "Kreaturen"; in Eckhart’s mystical theology, this refers to all finite, created things. He taught that to find the Creator, one must look beyond the "creatures" or the material world.</note> behind.
— Who are you? — A king. — Where is your kingdom? — In my heart. — Take care that no one shares it with you. — I do that.
— Then he led him into his cell and said: "Take whichever robe you want." — "Then I would be no king," — and he vanished. It had been God himself, who had been playing a joke with him.

## Remarks.

[. . .] Square brackets mean that I am inclined to attribute the passage to the caution or the misunderstanding of a scribe — although <term>Eckhart</term> <note>original: "E."; refers to Master Eckhart (c. 1260–1328), the German theologian and mystic.</note> himself was no less clever than other brave people.

P. 14. “Means, Essence, Work.” These and many other words — especially “pure” (<gloss>Middle High German: lûter</gloss>), which means absolute or abstract — are fixed technical terms, mostly translations from the Latin. It would have been wrong, however, to substitute our smoothed-over modern expressions for them; the reader must remain aware that Eckhart’s time was young; that we are dealing with an improvised language; with words that are only just being won and formed.

P. 15. “No one touches the <term>ground of the soul</term> <note>original: "Grund der Seele"; a central concept in Eckhart's mysticism referring to the innermost essence of a person where the human and divine are one.</note> but God alone.” I know well that we place different nuances into all these words; in exchange, a thousand subtleties from Eckhart’s situation are lost to us. Moreover, it is strongly recommended, from the poetic and heightened [tone] always...

### Meister Eckhart.

to peel away the sober sense. One should not overlook that <term>Eckhart</term> <note>original: "E."; refers to Master Eckhart.</note>, when he says: the image of the creature has found lodging in the powers of the soul, intends to say little more than: the representation of the object has been <term>apperceived</term> <note>original: "appercipiert"; in philosophy, this refers to the process by which new experience is assimilated and transformed by the residuum of past experience of an individual to form a new whole.</note> with the help of the sense organs.

P. 17. “and in no other.” Note how Eckhart underlines these and similar remarks again and again. For us, there is a colossal amount of heresy behind this symbolic interpretation of the birth of the Son of God. What appears to us as heresy, however, rather explains alone the power of Christianity over the deep, rich spirit of the Middle Ages. Not only was the <term>Song of Songs</term> <note>original: "Hohelied"; a book of the Hebrew Bible often interpreted by mystics as an allegory for the relationship between the soul and God.</note> taken symbolically, but also the stories of the life and death of the Savior. Christians only exist as long as the symbolic content of tradition acts so overwhelmingly that the question of historical factuality does not arise at all. The Russians (<term>Dostoevsky</term>, <term>Tolstoy</term>) <note>Fyodor Dostoevsky and Leo Tolstoy; the author suggests these Russian thinkers maintained a medieval-like focus on the spiritual/ethical essence of faith rather than historical critique.</note> still experience this today, except that they are concerned only with ethical symbols, not at all with <term>epistemological</term> <note>original: "erkenntnistheoretischen"; relating to the theory of knowledge and how we distinguish justified belief from opinion.</note> ones. In Western Europe, however, Christianity ceased at the moment when people tried to force themselves to believe in the myths as life-deciding facts, because they were no longer strong enough for the reinterpretation of the symbols. **P. 20.** “super-divine” — <term>Middle High German</term> <note>original: "mhd."; refers to the period of the German language from roughly 1050 to 1350.</note> “übergotten.” <term>Lasson</term> <note>Adolf Lasson (1832–1917), a philosopher and scholar who edited Meister Eckhart’s works.</note> suggests reading it as “überguoten” [super-good]; completely baseless and senseless. The author suggests Eckhart’s "unknowing" is a spiritual state, not just a psychological one.</note>.

**P. 21.** “everything good” and the like in other places: to be understood as far as possible in a non-ethical, amoral sense; this must be noted again and again with Eckhart. <note>In mystical theology, "Good" often refers to the essential nature of Being rather than a moral category of behavior.</note>

**P. 26.** “psychic purpose.” A "blessed end," as the reader of Middle High German is inclined to interpret it, would be moralizing drivel, which one might find in later “mystics,” but must not look for in Eckhart.

**P. 26.** “Work.” The passage is best understood if one translates “work” <note>original: "Werk"</note> as “organ.”

**P. 29.** “Super-form.” <note>original: "Ueberform"</note> In such places it is difficult to decide whether the expression is a strong and bold one, or a common and

# Meister Eckhart.

...matter. In any case, “trans-formed” <note>original: “überformet”</note> arose following the Latin “transformare” <note>English: to transform</note>; perhaps, then, it sounded to Eckhart’s <note>original: “E.’s”</note> contemporaries as smooth, inconspicuous, and abstract as the word “reconfigured” <note>original: “umgestaltet”</note> does to us. However, this is likely not the case, for Eckhart would not without reason have found the unpoetic, image-less—though still living—Latin language unbearable. In any case, in such doubtful instances, I have preferred the rarer mode of expression, especially since the meaning, unfortunately, remains very much the same: even when language surpasses itself and turns on its head, the “trans-form” <note>original: “Ueberform”</note> is nothing other than an unknown and indescribable change.

**P. 30. “Receiving.”** Anyone wishing to render the <term>Middle High German</term> <note>original: “mhd.”</note> “lîden” <note>literally: to suffer or undergo</note> as “suffering” <note>original: “leiden”</note> would introduce into Eckhart a type of mysticism that was completely foreign to him in these contexts. It signifies merely passivity, whereby Eckhart thinks more of a joyful being-gifted than of enduring pain. It mostly touches upon the meaning of the word “to perceive” <note>original: “vernehmen”</note> (Reason! <note>original: “Vernunft”</note>), which Eckhart prefers to use over our “to observe” <note>original: “wahrnehmen”</note>. This delight of his in passive perception in contrast to active observation is connected to the fact that for him, hearing, whose impressions...

# Remarks.

...are of a more spiritual nature, which transform the external world into subjective feelings, seemed more valuable than materializing sight, which produces the external world, images, and creatures, and separates them from us. For this reason, “suffering” is the union of the I and the world, of the soul and God—but not for the sake of pain, as Eckhart’s <note>original: “E.’s”</note> closest and most gifted disciple, Henry Suso <note>original: “Seuse”</note>, advocated in his poetic writings and confessions. Nevertheless, this nuance of suffering does indeed resonate slightly within Eckhart at times. No era can escape its own language; and so it is possible that the asceticism of the Middle Ages largely stems from this double meaning of the theological-psychological term <term>undergoing or suffering</term> <note>original Middle High German: “lîden”; Latin: “pati”</note>, in connection, of course, with the “Passion” of Jesus Christ. Painful suffering was a blissful receiving; whoever kept still was gifted; it was therefore useful to suffer. — Regarding the same passage of the text and many others, it should also be noted: “Love” and “to love” in Eckhart’s work almost always carry the secondary meaning—often the predominant meaning—of “Will” and “to will.” Where he speaks of “Love” and “Knowledge,” he means “Will and Representation.” <note>This is a likely reference to Arthur Schopenhauer’s philosophy, suggesting Eckhart’s medieval concepts prefigure modern thought.</note>

**P. 39. “Book of Secrets”** — The Apocalypse. <note>The biblical Book of Revelation.</note>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological remarks and commentary on Meister Eckhart’s sermons, moving from general terminology to specific page references and the philosophical mindset of the medieval period.</meta>

# Meister Eckhart.

**P. 48.** Almost all of Eckhart’s <note>original: “E.’s”</note> sermons are preceded by a Vulgate <note>The standard Latin version of the Bible used by the medieval Church.</note> text, which was mostly omitted in this edition.

**P. 68.** “One of our oldest masters”: Cratylus. <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and student of Heraclitus who believed that the world was in such constant flux that true naming was impossible.</note>

**P. 72.** “a gnat.” The concept of the infinite is completely absent in Eckhart, for the small as well as for the large; for the latter, he says something like: “as far as you can count.” — That he lacks the correlative terms “positive” and “negative” will have been gathered by the reader from the discourse on “unknowing” <note>original: “Nichtwissen”; this refers to the mystical tradition of apophatic theology, where God is described by what He is not, leading to a higher state of spiritual "not-knowing."</note>; one should remember the arduous and therefore wonderful effort to distinguish mystical (positive) unknowing from the common kind.

**P. 73.** “He gives birth to his son.” Regarding this and many other passages: the myth-making power had not yet died out back then. Christianity lived because it was not yet finished; because it was the form in which the young, struggling science expressed itself. Even in Eckhart’s consciousness, it is often indistinguishable what is symbolism and what he naively believes, even though he invents (sees) <note>original: “schaut”; this implies an intuitive, visionary beholding of truth.</note> it in the moment. All invention and discovery among people at their cultural beginning is a seeing and a revelation; all activity of genius is a sinking into God. I recommend the reader, from time to time, [to consider] the word “mysticism”...

<summary>This page provides scholarly commentary on Eckhart's sermons, highlighting his lack of a formal concept of the infinite and the productive "myth-making" nature of medieval Christianity. The author emphasizes that for Eckhart, "unknowing" was a positive spiritual achievement rather than a mere lack of knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Vulgate, Cratylus, infinity, unknowing, mysticism, myth-making, visionary beholding, medieval science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editorial remarks on Meister Eckhart’s works, specifically discussing the difficulty of reconciling his medieval Scholastic framework with his modern-sounding mystical insights.</meta>

## Remarks.
16

...to translate as "genius" <note>original: "Genialität"</note>, while considering that he thereby replaces the unknown with something that is by no means known.

**P. 119 ff.** The sermon "On the Renewal of the Spirit" contains much indigestible Scholasticism <note>The dominant philosophical and theological system of the Middle Ages, which emphasized rigorous logic and the reconciliation of Christian faith with classical philosophy.</note>, but between it lies the deepest wisdom. This is often the case with Eckhart; the fragments are almost all unearthed from a mountain-high pile of word-rubble; a sign that what is ash and dead to us was a necessary element of thought in its time. The Scholastic element in this sermon — and also elsewhere at times; had the poetic greatness not been lost by it, I would have considered dissolving everything handed down into aphorisms — let the reader take this as an example of the much that has been omitted in this edition. To this small extent, Eckhart’s spiritual milieu must become familiar to readers. For without a thorough knowledge of Scholasticism, Eckhart cannot be historically assessed; he is completely rooted in it. One must not overlook that he arrived at results from a completely different language (worldview) <note>original: "Weltanschauung"</note> that so closely touch our boldest fantasies as well as our most profound skepticism. If one only paid attention to the linguistic aspects, to that which has become conscious, one would have to say that he arrived at what we almost [call] truth through nothing but errors and false premises...

<summary>The editor discusses the challenge of translating Eckhart's "Scholastic" terminology, arguing that while much of his medieval logic seems like "word-rubble" today, it was the necessary foundation for his profound mystical insights.</summary>
<keywords>Scholasticism, translation methodology, worldview, philology, spiritual milieu, aphorisms</keywords>

<page-num>242</page-num>
### Meister Eckhart.

...might wish to call it. It is always thus, and we fare no better.

Eckhart was thus a <term>Scholastic Realist</term> <note>A medieval philosopher who believed that "universals" (general concepts like "Beauty" or "Humanity") have a real, objective existence, rather than being mere names or mental labels.</note>, a follower of Plato’s theory of ideas, whom he calls the "great cleric." <note>original: "grossen Pfaffen." While "Pfaffe" later became a derogatory term for a priest, Eckhart uses it here as a title of respect for the ancient Greek philosopher.</note> He calls these Ideas "preceding images," <note>original: "vorhergehende Bilder"</note> yet by this he understands little else than what we—with the help of the concept of heredity—call "species" or "genus," which are indeed what precede and underlie concrete individuals. These "preceding images" are in God, and "therefore man begets a man, the lion a lion, the hawk a hawk." "The rose grows from a rose, not from a head of cabbage." From his great master <term>Thomas</term> <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent Scholastic theologian and Eckhart’s predecessor in the Dominican Order.</note> he quotes the words: "The preceding images are an origin or beginning of the creations of all creatures." This is not intended, however, to assert the constancy of species as opposed to their variability <note>A reference to the burgeoning scientific debates of the late 19th century regarding evolution and Darwinism.</note>; this question had hardly yet been born—although Eckhart touches upon it in the sermon "On Nature"—rather, it emphasizes the connection of all individual organisms in a higher, hidden unity. We call it heredity; instead of "beginning" or "origin" we say "principle," yet with this nonsensical-abstract way of speaking, we do significantly less to formulate the riddle than did the Realists of the

<summary>The editor explains Eckhart's position as a Scholastic Realist who viewed Platonic "Ideas" as the biological and spiritual archetypes (preceding images) that govern heredity and the continuity of species.</summary>
<keywords>Scholastic Realism, Plato, Theory of Ideas, Thomas Aquinas, heredity, species, preceding images, biological origin</keywords>

<page-num>243</page-num>
<header>Remarks.</header>

...as the people of the Middle Ages did. Eckhart would certainly—very differently from our Darwinists—have wondered more about heredity than about adaptation. It did not astonish him that funnel-web spiders <note>original: "Grasspinnen." These are spiders commonly found in grass, belonging to the Agelenidae family. The editor uses them here to illustrate Eckhart's focus on the consistency of nature.</note> were so different, but rather that they were so similar.

I note here that in the material not included in this edition, there is much that is characteristic of Eckhart's view of nature <note>original: "Naturanschauung"; a German philosophical term referring to a comprehensive intuitive perception of the natural world.</note> and his era, as well as many other things of cultural-historical interest. However, this book pursues no historical goals whatsoever; anyone wishing to research history must, in any case, stick to the original texts. Nor is this version of Meister Eckhart presented here intended for spiritual edification or ethics; otherwise, I should not have omitted, for example, the speeches in the *collations* <term>collationibus</term> <note>original: "collationibus." These were informal spiritual conferences or lectures given to a monastic community, often during a meal.</note>, which are excellent in their own way. My goal is simply this: the living Eckhart. May he work in this volume through his piercing skepticism, through his struggle for knowledge of the world and knowledge of the self, through his poetic power, his royal language, and his fundamentally kind, life-affirming nature. Everything else concerns only the scholars.

One single passage remains for me to quote out of context. Let it stand here, as the sermon from which it originates does not warrant a full translation, and because it also concerns the ques—

In the midst of the most dreadful hair-splitting, one finds the sentence: “And thus the word ‘I’ signifies the is-ness <note>original: "Istigkeit"; a term coined by Eckhart to describe the pure state of being or "is-hood" of God.</note> of divine truth, and it is a proof that something exists.” Eckhart says, then, more subtly than Descartes' <note>original: "Cartesius," the Latinized name for René Descartes.</note> *I think, therefore I am* <note>original: "Cogito ergo sum"</note>: *I think, therefore something exists* <note>original: "Cogito, ergo est aliquid"</note>.

P. 127. “Each thing possesses that with which it is one.” We call this specific sensory energy <note>original: "spezifische Sinnesenergie"; a 19th-century physiological concept suggesting that the brain perceives the same stimulus differently depending on which nerve is stimulated.</note>, and we do not know much more than he did.

P. 144. “On the personal essence.” This sermon has come down to us only in a late and corrupt text. I have tried to reconstruct the meaning to some extent; however, one would do well to stick to what is undoubtedly genuine—not to the syntax, but rather to the terminology.

P. 196. “...yet the soul possesses it linguistically with distinction.” Linguistically—the original says “*redelich*.” Here, as in many other places, it is impossible to decide whether the nuance of “language” or the nuance of “reason” predominates. In “*redelicheit*” <note>Middle High German: rationality or reasonableness.</note> the nuance of “language” has almost been lost; it means “rationality.” However that may be—the speaker of Middle High German thought of speech <note>original: "Rede"</note> when he spoke of reason <note>original: "Vernunft"</note>, whereas he did not think at all of what

<header>Remarks.</header>

...what we today call "integrity." <note>German: *Redlichkeit*. In modern German, this implies honesty or moral uprightness, but the editor is tracing its shift from the Middle High German root *rede*, meaning speech or reason.</note> However, the meaning "linguistic" is the more probable here, because shortly before there was talk of the "speech of those speaking" <note>Middle High German: *rede der redenden*.</note>.

P. 230. "The first in intention is the last in the work." This sentence is found frequently in E. <note>Meister Eckhart.</note>; it is his excellent, concise definition of **purpose**. Whatever finally enters into appearance must have been there from the beginning—as a preceding image or idea. It is not what is acquired within the individual that is divine, but rather humanity, the eternal heritage. Our newly stirring <term>neo-teleology</term> <note>A philosophical movement attempting to reintroduce the concept of "purpose" or "final causes" into the study of nature, often as a response to purely mechanical science.</note>, which appears as a contradiction to or a supplement of the <term>causality hypothesis</term> <note>The scientific view that all events are strictly determined by prior causes rather than future goals.</note>, will be permitted to look around quietly among the <term>Realists</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, "Realists" believed that universal concepts (like "Humanity") were real things that existed independently of human thought, as opposed to "Nominalists" who thought they were just names.</note> of the Middle Ages. The "old man with the white beard" <note>A personified, anthropomorphic image of God.</note> is so thoroughly dead that one can really set about reinterpreting the absolutely necessary concept of "purpose" without fearing that the dead man might be revived by it. — In E.’s work, that which is common to all is not the "commonplace" as we understand it now, nor is it the general or philistine, but rather the exquisite, the particular, the primordially individual. He was simply a "Realist" of a different style than our <term>reality-mongers</term> <note>German: *Realitätenkrämer*; a derogatory term for those who focus exclusively on material facts and superficial data.</note>; for him, the "nature of all humanity" reached down into the eternal past, which works its purposes into the present. More details regarding

meager citations. But of these, there are a stately number whose spiritual height and linguistic power rise with eternal value above the various fashions of the centuries, yet which must repeatedly sink beneath the debris of everyday phenomena for a larger audience. We wish to pull these forth again, one by one; and the very physical appearance of our books will make it known that our editions are intended for an appreciative public, to be read in one's finest and most elevated hours—just as one should read **Goethe** or **Shakespeare**, **Schopenhauer** or **Nietzsche**—that will be made clear by the outer appearance of our books.

In doing so, we do not limit ourselves at all to a specific, narrow field of literary culture. **Master Eckhart** <note>Meister Eckhart (c. 1260–1328) was a German theologian and philosopher whose mystical teachings often brought him into conflict with the Church hierarchy.</note> represents a program only through his genius, not through the particular direction of his nature—or at least only insofar as the **mystics** and all sorts of other **heretics**, **sectarians**, and **saints**—taking that word in its earthly, beautiful sense—belong specifically to those whose influence on our time, and whose knowledge, must be reawakened. But we shall also have enough of the exquisite and the forgotten to bring forth: poets and sages, pamphleteers and politicians, the quiet ones and the holy ones, fighters and hermits from all peoples and all times, from the **Occident** <note>The West.</note> and the **Orient** <note>The East.</note>. We would like to contribute toward finally leading the German people beyond the narrow, schoolmasterly concept of the "Classics"

<summary>In this introduction, Landauer outlines his vision for the book series, aiming to rescue profound historical thinkers and "heretics" from obscurity and present them as living literature for the modern reader.</summary>
<keywords>literary canon, mysticism, heretics, German classics, cultural revival, Meister Eckhart, Gustav Landauer</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Lost Masters" series, following the previous page's discussion on expanding the German concept of "Classics" beyond mere schoolbook staples.</meta>

to move beyond—since by this term one truly understands those authors who are permitted to be read in schools. Yet there is so much that has not yet entered the schools and which exceeds the horizon of the adolescent: all that which was previously buried under what <term>half-education</term> <note>German: *Halbbildung*. A term often used by German intellectuals to describe a superficial or "pseudo-culture" that lacks true spiritual depth.</note> acknowledged, what pedantry standardized, and what prudery wrapped around itself—all this shall be permitted to speak again in our collection to the adult, the mature, the longing, and the reborn. Furthermore, our first volume shall promise what those following will fulfill.

The fact that the **Mystical Writings** of **Master Eckhart**, whose Middle High German <note>Middle High German was the form of the German language spoken from roughly 1050 to 1350; it is significantly different from modern German.</note> text has been available for sixty years in the complete edition by **Franz Pfeiffer** <note>Franz Pfeiffer (1815–1868) was a pioneering scholar who published the first major collection of Eckhart’s works in 1857.</note>, find their first edition in our modern language here must astonish everyone who—through the citations in **Schopenhauer** and many others—senses the man’s significance but does not know the original. Indeed, whoever has tried to read it will be less surprised. With **Master Eckhart**, it is not enough to be a good translator; one must be a discoverer of the meaning of what Eckhart has to say to us, and one must be an inventor of new words and new sentence structures for the often-necessary task of <term>linguistic creation</term>. Above all, one must have already arrived at very similar intuitions and forms regarding the relationship between **world and soul**, proceeding from a completely new way of speaking, with entirely different symbolism, and from a completely different knowledge of nature,

<summary>Landauer explains that his series aims to rescue deep spiritual and "heretical" works from the stifling constraints of school curricula and superficial culture, beginning with the first modern German translation of Master Eckhart.</summary>
<keywords>Master Eckhart, Franz Pfeiffer, Schopenhauer, mysticism, Middle High German, translation philosophy, soul and world, education vs. culture</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Lost Masters" series, specifically discussing the task of modernizing Master Eckhart's medieval German mysticism for a contemporary audience.</meta>

...in order to find the philosophical core of Eckhart's teachings beneath their Christian attire; one might also say: to find our way back from our conventional Christianity, our historical dogmas, and our belief in anecdotes, to the soulful, entirely symbolic Christianity of **Master Eckhart**. Anyone who has grasped Master Eckhart in this way knows that he ultimately leads us far beyond such vulgar matters as <term>denominationalism</term> <note>German: *Konfessionalismus*. This refers to the rigid adherence to specific church dogmas (Catholic vs. Protestant) which Eckhart’s universal mysticism transcends.</note> or atheism.

Oppositions of this ordinary kind no longer exist in the realm where Johann Eckhart lived while he was a glowingly vibrant man—and where he still lives, at least as long as the German language, among whose greatest masters he will always belong.

**Gustav Landauer** <note>Gustav Landauer (1870–1919) was an influential German philosopher and anarchist who sought to revitalize Eckhart's mysticism as a tool for social and spiritual renewal.</note>, who undertook this edition after occupying himself with Eckhart for many years, believes he has found the key to Eckhart’s secrets through a certain kinship of spirit. In the deep-seated belief in this intellectual and spiritual proximity, he set joyfully to work; and because he succeeded in understanding Eckhart and reshaping him both linguistically and conceptually, he was also in a position to separate from the Master’s surviving writings that which was Eckhart’s true essence from that which was merely the <term>ecclesiastical-scholastic</term> <note>Scholasticism was the dominant medieval school of philosophy that used strict logic and authority to defend Christian dogma; the author suggests Eckhart’s true voice often had to struggle against these rigid structures.</note> milieu out of which Eckhart had to bring forth his own original thought. For no writer has ever truly left behind the remnants of such a milieu; they all have much—

<summary>This page argues that Gustav Landauer’s translation of Master Eckhart moves beyond rigid church dogmas to reveal a timeless, symbolic Christianity. It highlights Landauer's personal spiritual connection to Eckhart, which allowed him to distinguish the mystic's original philosophy from the restrictive scholastic language of the 14th century.</summary>
<keywords>Master Eckhart, Gustav Landauer, mysticism, German philosophy, Scholasticism, religious symbolism, denominationalism, translation</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor's introduction to the works of Master Eckhart. It justifies the controversial editorial decision to strip away the medieval "scholastic debris" to reveal the eternal, living core of Eckhart's mysticism. The editor, Gustav Landauer, positions this edition not as a scholarly "modernization," but as a spiritual resurrection of a lost master for the modern reader.</meta>

...more—always without having been consciously aware of the distinction—faithfully piled up in their works. Anyone who now wished to publish a new edition of Eckhart without first thoroughly removing this <term>scholastic</term> <note>Scholasticism was a medieval school of philosophy that used rigid logic and appeals to authority to defend church dogma. The editor views this as "debris" hiding Eckhart's true genius.</note> debris, from which the Master reared himself up into eternity, would have performed a useless task; for Eckhart would have remained what he was before: <term>Middle High German</term> <note>The form of the German language spoken from roughly 1050 to 1350; here, it implies the text is archaic and inaccessible to modern readers.</note>, unpalatable, and incomprehensible.

In our edition, everything that does not stem from Eckhart’s greatness, but rather from the <term>spirit of his times</term> <note>German: *Zeitgeist*. This refers to the cultural and intellectual atmosphere of the 14th century, which the editor believes limited Eckhart’s universal message.</note>, has been omitted. This omission is perhaps the greatest merit of this edition; however, only someone who felt and revered Eckhart as a living and active force could have presumed to take such a liberty. Therefore, designations such as "selection" or even "modernization" would be entirely incorrect for this book as it is presented here. It is the return of one who was lost, who should not be appreciated merely as a historical figure, but felt as a living presence. To what extent this has succeeded, the experts may judge; but the readers we hope for may they feel and enjoy Eckhart's rebellious and intimate wisdom, his spiritual immersion, and his proud human beauty!

> This is Master Eckhart,
> who was nearly burned at the stake.
> Book, go forth now in his name,
> and avoid the dim-witted.

->Amen.<-

<note>original vocabulary: Master Eckhart, Scholasticism, Middle High German, Spirit of the Times, Modernization, Spiritual Immersion, Human Beauty</note>

<summary>The editor defends his decision to remove medieval scholastic elements from Eckhart's texts, arguing that this "debris" makes the mystic's true message unpalatable. He presents the edition as a spiritual revival intended for modern readers to experience Eckhart's "rebellious and intimate wisdom."</summary>
<keywords>Master Eckhart, Gustav Landauer, Scholasticism, Middle High German, mysticism, spiritual revival, editorial philosophy</keywords>
